@ch I.19 | The gods' simplicity
Socrates refers to the divine the one-like and the simple. The gods' simplicity is not that of one thing in number, nor of a species or a kind, nor of nature, soul or intellect, each of which is mixed with multitude. The gods alone are simple, set above all multitude and division; the many shapes in which they appear come from what receives them. What generates the manifold must itself be simple.

@p
Next, let us consider what power the simplicity of the gods has. For Socrates adds this in his account of the divine nature: he does not admit what is varied, of many shapes, and seen differently at different times, but refers the one-like and the simple to the divine. Each of the gods, then, as he says, remains simply in his own form. What shall we conclude about this simplicity? That it is not like what is defined as one in number. For such a thing is made of many things, richly mixed. It appears simple so far as it has, distinctly, a common form. Nor is it like the simplicity in many things by a species or kind set in order. For these are simpler than the individuals in which they are, but are full of variety, share in matter, and receive the differences of material things. Nor is it like the form of nature. For nature is divided about bodies, leans towards bodily masses, sends out many powers about the compound below it, and is simpler than bodies, but has a being mixed with their variety. Nor is it like the simplicity of soul. For soul, existing as a middle between the undivided being and the being divided about bodies, shares with both extremes. By what is of many shapes in its nature it is joined to things below; but its head is set on high, and by this it is most of all divine, and akin to intellect.
!note Republic II 380d-381c: whether god is a wizard who appears now in one form and now in another, or is simple and least of all leaves his own form.

@p
Nor, again, is the simplicity of the gods like that of intellect. Every intellect is undivided and one-like, but it also has multitude and procession; by which it is clear that it has a relation to second natures, to itself, and about itself. It is also in itself, and is not only one-like but of many forms, and, as is said, a one-many. So it has a being below the first simplicity. But the gods have their hyparxis defined in one simplicity alone: as gods they are set apart from all multitude, and above all division and extension, all relation to second natures, and all composition. They are in places no one can reach, spread out above the whole of things, and for ever ride upon beings. But the lights that go out from them to second natures, mixed in many places with the things that share in them, which are compound and varied, are filled with a character like theirs. So let no one wonder if, though the gods are given their being in one simplicity by their transcendence, various apparitions are thrown out before their presence; or if, though they are one-like, their appearances are of many shapes, as we have learned in the most perfect of the mysteries. For nature and the making intellect put out images: of bodiless things, in the form of bodies; of intelligible things, perceptible images; and of things without extension, images with extension. Socrates too, in the Phaedrus, points to things of this kind. He shows that the mysteries into which souls without bodies are initiated are the most blessed and truly perfect, and says that they are initiated into whole, simple and unmoving visions, such souls being set there and united with the gods themselves, not meeting with the likenesses sent out from the gods into this region below the moon. For these are more partial and compound, and appear with motion. But visions lit up, one-like, simple and, as Socrates says, unmoving show themselves to the followers of the gods, and to souls that leave the great tumult of coming to be and go up to the divine pure and stripped of the garments of mortality. This, then, is what we conclude about the simplicity of the gods. For the nature that generates things of many forms must be simple and come before what it generates, just as the one-like comes before the many. So if the gods are the causes of all composition, and produce from themselves the variety of beings, the oneness of their nature, which generates the whole of things, must surely be simple. For as bodiless causes come before bodies, unmoving causes before things moved, and undivided causes before everything divided, so one-like intellectual powers come before natures of many forms, unmixed powers before mixed things, and simple powers before varied ones.
!flag various phantasms are hurled forth before the presence of them | In the rites, apparitions were believed to appear before a god shows himself; Proclus explains them as lights of the gods mixed with what receives them, not as the gods' own shapes.
!note The Phaedrus 250b-c: the souls, "pure and not entombed in this body", were initiated into "whole, simple, unmoving and happy visions". "Garments of mortality": the Chaldean Oracles' image of the soul's vehicles. Taylor's footnote supplies two words of the Greek he translated.

@ch I.20 | Truth in the gods, and where falsehood comes from
Divine truth is above the truth of speech, the truth of soul's opinions and sciences, and even intellect's truth, which has difference in it. It is the undivided union of the gods, by which they know all things at once, wholes and parts, the eternal and the temporal, even matter. Falsehood in oracles comes not from the gods but from what receives them. Truth unites the henads to the source of all good, as it joins intellect to the intelligible in the Republic.

@p
Next, let us speak of the truth that is in the gods. For Socrates concludes this too, in addition to what has been said: that a divine nature is without falsehood, and is the cause of neither deceit nor ignorance to us or to any other beings. We must understand, then, that divine truth is set above the truth that lies in words, since this is compound, in a way mixed with its contrary, and made of parts that are not true. For the first parts do not admit of such truth, unless someone, persuaded by what Socrates says in the Cratylus, says that they too are true in another way. Divine truth is also set above the truth of soul, whether seen in opinions or in sciences, since this is in a way divided, and is not the beings themselves but made like them and tuned to them, and, being perfected in motion and change, falls short of the truth that is always firm, stable and ruling. Divine truth is again set above intellectual truth. For though this is so by its being, and is said to be, and is, the beings themselves by the power of sameness, yet again by difference it is separated from their being, and keeps its own reality unconfused with them. So the truth of the gods alone is their undivided union and all-perfect sharing with one another. Through this the inexpressible knowledge of the gods goes beyond all knowledge, and all second forms of knowledge share in a fitting perfection. This knowledge of the gods alone holds these second forms of knowledge, and all beings, drawn together by an inexpressible union. Through it the gods know all things at once, wholes and parts, beings and non-beings, things eternal and things in time. They do not know as intellect does, knowing a part by the whole and non-being by being. They know each thing directly, what is common and what is particular, even if you speak of the most absurd of all things, of the countless things that may happen, or of matter itself.
!flag For the first parts do not admit of a truth of this kind | The "first parts" of speech are names and words taken alone, which are neither true nor false; in the Cratylus (385c) Socrates says that if a statement is true, its parts, down to names, are true too.

@p
If you look for the manner of the gods' knowledge and truth about all things that exist in any way, it is inexpressible, and cannot be grasped by the reaching of the human intellect; it is known only to the gods themselves. I admire those Platonists who give intellect the knowledge of all things, of individuals, of things against nature, and in short of evils, and so posit intellectual patterns of these. But I admire much more those who separate the distinctive character of intellect from divine union. For intellect is the first work and offspring of the gods. These give intellect the whole and first causes, those according to nature, and give the gods a power able to adorn and generate all things. For the One is everywhere, but the whole is not everywhere. Matter and every being share in the One; but not all things share in intellect, and in intellectual Forms and kinds. So all things come only from the gods, and real truth is with those who know all things in a unified way. This is why in oracles, too, the gods teach all things alike: wholes and parts, things eternal and things that come to be through all time. For being set apart from both eternal beings and those in time, they hold drawn together in themselves the knowledge of each thing and of all, by one united truth. So if any falsehood occurs in the oracles of the gods, we must not say that it comes from the gods, but from those who receive them, or the instruments, or the places, or the times. For all these play a part in sharing in divine knowledge. When they are fitly tuned to the gods, they receive a pure light of the truth set in them. But when they are separated from the gods by unfitness and fall out of tune with them, they darken the truth that comes from them. What falsehood, then, can be said to come from the gods, who produce all kinds of knowledge? What deceit can there be with those who set in themselves the whole of truth? In the same way, it seems to me, the gods hold out good to all things, but it is always what is willing and able that receives the good held out, as Socrates says in the Phaedrus. The divine nature is not the cause of evil; what leaves it and sinks downward is carried far off by its own doing. So too the gods are always the givers of truth, but those natures are lit up by them that share in them lawfully. For the wise man from Elea says that the eye of the soul in the many is not strong enough to look at the truth.
!note The Phaedrus 247a: "whoever is willing and able follows" the gods, "for envy stands outside the divine choir". The Sophist 254a: "the eyes of the soul of the many cannot bear to look upon the divine".

@p
The Athenian Stranger also praises this truth, which is first in the gods. He says that truth leads all good things for gods, and all good things for men. For as the truth in souls joins them to intellect, and intellectual truth leads all the intellectual orders to the One, so the truth of the gods unites the divine henads to the source of all good; joined with it, they are filled with all power that has the form of the good. For everywhere the hyparxis of truth has a cause that gathers multitude into one. In the Republic, too, Plato calls the light that comes from the Good, and joins intellect to the intelligible, truth. So this distinctive character, which unites and binds together what fills and what is filled, through all the orders of the gods, must be set down as beginning from on high and going out as far as the last of things.
!note Laws V 730c: "truth leads all good things, for gods and for men". Republic VI 508d-e: the Good gives truth to what is known and the power of knowing to the knower, as the sun's light does for sight.

@ch I.21 | The Phaedrus: everything divine is beautiful, wise and good
In the Phaedrus Socrates says that everything divine is beautiful, wise and good, a triad that runs through all the processions of the gods. First, goodness: it keeps all things and gives them existence, is everywhere the summit, and joins all the gods to the One, the cause of all things.

@p
As we discuss what belongs to every divine nature, what we say will be known from the common truths given in the Phaedrus, which we have mentioned before. Socrates says that everything divine is beautiful, wise and good, and points out that this triad runs through all the processions of the gods. What, then, is the goodness, what the wisdom, and what the beauty of the gods? Of the goodness of the gods we have said before that it keeps all things and gives them existence; that it is everywhere as the summit, as what fills the lower natures, and as existing first in every order, answering to the first principle of the divine orders. By it all the gods are joined to the One, the cause of all things, and through it they first have their existence as gods. For among all beings nothing is more perfect than the Good, and the gods. So to the best of beings, those perfect in every way, the best and most perfect of things belongs.
!note The Phaedrus 246d-e: "the divine is beautiful, wise, good, and everything of that kind"; by these the soul's wings are nourished. Taylor's footnote corrects a misprint in the Greek he translated ("possible" for "good").

@ch I.22 | The goodness of the gods: the Philebus' three marks of the good
In the Philebus Plato gives the three chief marks of the good: the desirable, the sufficient and the perfect. The desirable draws all things to the gods, unknowably, as the sun draws the eye; all things dance round it. The sufficient carries the gods' good out to all things, so that every being generates. The perfect turns all things back to their causes. By these three the gods' goodness remains, proceeds and returns.

@p
In the Philebus Plato gives us the three chief marks of the good: the desirable, the sufficient and the perfect. For the good must turn all things to itself and fill all things, must lack nothing, and must not lessen its overflowing. Let no one think the desirable is like what is so often held out among perceptible things as an object of appetite: such is apparent beauty. Nor let anyone suppose it is the kind of thing that can act on and rouse to itself the natures able to share in it, yet can still be grasped by thought, and is drawn out by us by the reaching and holding of discursive reason. For it is inexpressible, and reaches all beings before all knowledge. For all things desire the good and turn to it. If we must unfold in brief the distinctive character of the desirable: as the giver of light goes out by his rays into second natures, turns the eye to himself, makes it sun-like and like himself, and through another likeness joins it to his own bright splendour, so, I think, the desirable of the gods draws and leads up all things to the gods in an inexpressible way by its own lights, present everywhere to all things, and leaving no order of beings. For even matter is said to reach out to this desirable, and through this desire is filled with as many goods as it can share in. It is the centre of all beings, and all beings and all the gods have their being, powers and activities about it. The reaching and desire of things for it cannot be put out. For all beings long for this desirable, unknown and beyond grasp. Unable either to know or to receive what they desire, they dance round it, and are in labour with it and, as it were, divine it. They have an unceasing, never-ending desire for its unknown and inexpressible nature, while they cannot embrace it or hold it to themselves. For, set apart from all things at once, it is equally present to all and moves all things about itself, while by all of them it cannot be grasped. By this motion and this desire it keeps all things. But by its unknown transcendence, by which it is above the whole of things, it keeps its own union unmixed with second natures. Such, then, is the desirable.
!flag is educed by us according to a projecting energy, and an adhesion of the dianoetic power | Taylor's "projecting energy" (epibolē) is the mind's reaching towards an object; "dianoetic power" is discursive reasoning (dianoia). The desirable, Proclus says, cannot be grasped by either.
!note The Philebus 20d: the good must be perfect, sufficient, and what everything that knows it hunts and desires. The sun that makes the eye "sun-like": Republic VI 508b.

@p
The sufficient is full of power that has the form of the good. It goes out to all things, and reaches out the gifts of the gods to all beings. For we think of this sufficiency as a power that runs through and stretches to the last of things, reaching out the ungrudging and overflowing will of the gods. It does not remain in itself, but holds in a unified way what is more than full, never failing, unlimited, and generative of good in the divine hyparxis. For while the desirable is set firmly, above the whole of things, ordering all beings about itself, the sufficient begins the procession and multiplying of all good. By its own fertile overflowing, and by the giving of good that fills and runs through all things, it calls forth what is first in the one-like hyparxis of the desirable, and pours it out and gives it to every being. It is because of the sufficient, then, that the stability of divine natures, and what proceeds from its proper causes, is full of goodness; and, in short, that all beings are benefited, remaining in their principles, proceeding from them, united to them, and separated from them in their being. Through this power the intellectual kinds give existence to natures like themselves, souls desire to generate and imitate the beings before souls, natures hand on their forming principles into another place, and all things, in short, have a love of generating. For the sufficiency of the gods' goodness, proceeding from that goodness, is sown in all beings, and moves all things to ungrudging giving of good: intellect to giving intellectual good, soul to giving soul's good, nature to giving natural good.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated: "fertile" for "remaining".

@p
So all things remain through the desirable of goodness, and through the sufficient generate and proceed into second and third generations. The third, the perfect, turns the whole of things back and gathers them in a circle to their causes; and this is done by divine, intellectual, soul's and natural perfection. For all things share in turning back, since through it the unlimited stretching out of procession is called back again to its principles. And the perfect is mixed from the desirable and the sufficient. For everything of this kind is desired and generates things like itself. Or are not perfect things, even in the works of nature, everywhere lovely and fertile, through the full bloom of their beauty? The desirable, then, sets all things firm and holds them in itself. The sufficient rouses them into processions and generations. And the perfect brings processions to their end in turnings back and circlings round. Through these three causes, the goodness of the gods, fixing the unified power and authority of its own reality in this triad, is the first and chief source, the hearth, of all things that exist in any way.
!flag the primary and most principal fountain and vestal seat of things | Taylor's "vestal seat" is the hearth (hestia), the fixed centre of a household or city; Proclus uses it for the firm seat from which all things proceed.

@ch I.23 | The wisdom of the gods
Wisdom, second in the triad, is the very being of the gods' thinking, a knowledge united with what it knows. From the Symposium, the Republic and the Theaetetus Plato shows three marks of wisdom: it is full of what it knows, it generates truth, and it brings what is imperfect to perfection.

@p
After goodness, wisdom has the second rank. It is the thinking of the gods, or rather the hyparxis of their thinking. For thinking is intellectual knowledge, but the wisdom of the gods is an inexpressible knowledge, united with what it knows and with the intelligible union of the gods. It seems to me that Plato looked at this especially in the triad of the beautiful, the wise and the good, as can be gathered from the ideas scattered about it in many places. Diotima in the Symposium holds that wisdom is full of what is known, and that it neither seeks nor searches, but has the intelligible. So, she says, none of the gods loves wisdom or desires to become wise, for a god is wise. What loves wisdom, then, is imperfect and lacks truth; but what is wise is full and lacks nothing, has present everything it wants, and desires nothing. It is to the lover of wisdom that the desirable and the longed-for are set before him. Socrates, in the Republic, takes what generates truth and intellect as a sign of wisdom: in our souls the going up to divine fullness happens through knowledge, while in the gods intellect is present out of the fullness of knowledge. For with them procession is not from an imperfect state to a perfect one; from a self-perfect hyparxis goes out a power that generates lower natures. In the Theaetetus he shows that bringing what is imperfect to perfection, and calling out hidden thinking in souls, belong to wisdom. For he says, "the god compels me to act as midwife, but forbids me to give birth". So it is clear from these that the kind of wisdom is threefold. It is full of being and truth, it generates intellectual truth, and it brings to perfection the intellectual natures that are in act, while itself having a stable power. We must grant, then, that these things belong to the wisdom of the gods. For this wisdom is full of divine goodness, generates divine truth, and brings to perfection all that comes after it.
!note The Symposium 204a: "none of the gods loves wisdom or desires to become wise, for he is wise already". Republic VI 490b: the lover of learning, joined with what really is, "begets intellect and truth". The Theaetetus 150c: "the god compels me to act as midwife, but has forbidden me to give birth". Taylor's two footnotes correct "of generation" to "of knowledge" in the Greek he translated.

@ch I.24 | The beauty of the gods
Divine beauty is first in the intelligible, and from there lights up all the kinds of gods, down to their visible vehicles; through it everything divine is lovely. It brings the gods joy and friendship with one another. Plato gives three marks of it: it is delicate, it is bright, and it is loved. So love leads to beauty, truth to wisdom, and to the good, not knowledge, but a firm settling and stillness.

@p
Next, let us consider the beautiful: what it is, and how it is first in the gods. It is said to be the beauty that has the form of the good, intelligible beauty, older than intellectual beauty, beauty itself, the cause of beauty to all beings, and all such titles. And this is rightly said. But it is set apart not only from the beauty that appears in bodily masses, from the proportion in them, from the grace of soul and the splendour of intellect, but also from the second and third processions among the gods. Existing in the intelligible place of vision, it goes out from there to all the kinds of gods, and lights up their henads beyond being, and all the beings that hang from these henads, as far as the visible vehicles of the gods. As all the gods have the form of the good through the first goodness, and through intelligible wisdom have a knowledge that is inexpressible and set above intellect, so too, I think, through the summit of beauty everything divine is lovely. For from there all the gods take beauty, and, filled with it, fill the natures after them, rousing all things, stirring them in Bacchic frenzy with love for themselves, and pouring down on all things from above the divine outflow of beauty.
!flag the intelligible place of survey | That is, the "place above the heavens" of the Phaedrus (247c), where the souls see true being; Proclus places the first beauty there, in the intelligible order.

@p
Such, in short, is divine beauty, the giver of divine gladness, closeness and friendship. Through it the gods are united to one another and rejoice in one another, admire and delight in sharing with each other and filling each other, and do not leave the order they always have in their distributions among themselves. Plato gives three signs of this beauty. In the Symposium he calls it the delicate: for the perfect and the most blessed come to the beautiful by sharing in goodness. He speaks of it this way in that dialogue: "What is truly beautiful is delicate, perfect and most blessed." One sign of the beautiful, then, is this: the delicate. We may take another sign of it from the Phaedrus: the bright. For Plato gives this to the beautiful when he says: "It was then that we could see beauty shining bright." And afterwards he adds: "And coming here, we grasped it shining most clearly through the clearest of the senses." And at last he says: "But now beauty alone has this lot, to be the brightest and the most lovely." These two, then, are to be taken as signs of beauty. Another sign of beauty is that it is the object of love, which Plato seems to me here also to have called most lovely. In many other places he shows that the frenzy of love is concerned with the beautiful, defining love by it, and hanging love, in short, from the monad of beauty. "For love," he says, "is of the beautiful."
!flag denominating it the delicate | In the Symposium (204c) Phaedrus' word is "delicate" (habron): "what is lovable is truly beautiful, delicate, perfect and most blessed". Proclus gives the delicate, the bright and the loved as the three signs of beauty.
!note The Phaedrus 250b-d: beauty was "bright to see" in the heavenly vision, and here we grasp it "shining most clearly through the clearest of our senses", sight; "beauty alone has this lot, to be the most visible and the most lovely". "Love is of the beautiful": Symposium 201a, 204d.

@p
Because beauty turns and moves all things to itself, makes them act in divine inspiration, and calls them back through love, it is the object of love. It leads the whole series of love, walking on the tips of its feet, and rousing all things to itself through desire and wonder. Again, because it reaches out its fullness to second natures, with gladness and divine ease, charming, kindling and lifting up all things, and pouring on them lights from on high, it is delicate, and Plato calls it so. And because it bounds this triad, and covers the inexpressible union of the gods as with a veil, swims, as it were, on the light of Forms, makes intelligible light shine out, and announces the hidden nature of goodness, it is called bright, shining and plain to see. For the goodness of the gods is highest and most united; their wisdom is in a way in labour with intelligible light and the first Forms; but their beauty is set in the highest Forms, is the shining forerunner of divine light, and is the first thing that appears to souls as they go up, brighter and lovelier to see and to embrace than any light-bearing being, and received with wonder when it appears. Since this triad fills all things and goes through all things, the natures that are filled must turn to each of the three and be joined to it through akin middles, not the same ones. For different things filled by this triad have different middles, and different powers turn to a different perfection of the gods. I think it is plain to everyone, and Plato often says it, that the cause that gathers all second natures to divine beauty, makes them at home with it, and is the source of their being filled with it and drawing from it, is nothing else than love, which always joins, by the beautiful, the second to the first: the gods, the higher kinds and the best of souls. Again, it is truth that leads to divine wisdom and sets beings in it. Filled with it, intellect has knowledge of beings, and souls that share in it act by intellect. For full sharing in true wisdom comes about through truth, since truth everywhere lights up the natures that think and joins them to what is thought, just as truth too is the first thing that gathers intellect and the intelligible together. But for those who hasten to be joined to the good, knowledge and working with it are no longer needed, but a setting in place, a firm settling and stillness.
!flag walking on the extremities of its feet | From the Symposium (195d), where Agathon says Love is delicate and walks not on the earth but "on the softest things"; Proclus' image is of beauty treading lightly, scarcely touching what it moves.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated ("to the first" for "to itself").

@ch I.25 | Faith, truth and love
What joins us to the good is the faith of the gods: not belief about perceptible things, nor assent to common notions, nor intellect's activity, but a settling in the good beyond all knowledge. Plato in the Laws links faith with truth and love. So goodness, wisdom and beauty fill the divine; faith, truth and love gather what is filled back to them; and all things are saved by the frenzy of love, divine philosophy and theurgy.

@p
What, then, unites us to the good? What brings our activity and motion to rest? What sets all divine natures in the first, inexpressible unity of goodness? And how is it that each thing, set in what is before it by the good in itself, in turn sets the things after it as their cause? It is, in short, the faith of the gods, which unites in an inexpressible way all the kinds of gods, of daemons and of happy souls to the good. For we must seek the good not by knowing, nor imperfectly, but by giving ourselves up to the divine light, closing the eyes of the soul, and so becoming settled in the unknown and hidden unity of beings. Such faith is older than the activity of knowing, not only in us but among the gods themselves. By it all the gods are united, and about one centre gather all their powers and processions in a one-like way.
!flag neither gnostically | Taylor's "gnostically" means by way of knowledge (gnōsis); Proclus means that the good is reached by a faith beyond knowing, not by an act of knowledge.
!note "Faith, truth and love" is a triad of the Chaldean Oracles (frag. 46: "faith, truth and love", with hope), which Proclus finds in Plato as well.

@p
If we must define this faith more particularly, let no one suppose that it is the kind of belief concerned with what wanders among perceptible things. For this falls short of science, and much more of the truth of beings. But the faith of the gods goes beyond all knowledge, and joins second natures to first by the highest union. Nor, again, let anyone think it is a faith of the same kind as the famous belief in common notions; for we believe in common notions before all reasoning. But the knowledge of these is divided, and in no way equal to divine union; and the science of them comes not only after faith, but after intellectual simplicity too. For intellect is set above all science, both the first science and what comes after it. Nor, then, must we say that the activity of intellect is like this faith. For intellectual activity is of many forms, separated from what it thinks by difference; and, in short, it is intellectual motion about the intelligible. But divine faith must be one-like and still, wholly settled in the harbour of goodness. For neither the beautiful, nor wisdom, nor anything else among beings is so trustworthy and stable to all things, so free of all doubt, divided grasping and motion, as the good. Through it intellect too embraces another union older than intellectual activity, and before activity. And soul counts the variety of intellect and the splendour of Forms as nothing beside that transcendence of the good by which it is above the whole of things. It lets go of intellectual perception, running back to its own hyparxis; but it always pursues, seeks and longs for the good, hastens, as it were, to embrace it, and gives itself to this alone of all things without hesitation. But why speak of soul? These mortal living beings, as Diotima says somewhere, despise everything else, even life and being, through desire for the nature of the good; and all things have this one unmoving and inexpressible leaning to the good, while they pass over, put second and despise the order of everything else. This, then, is the one safe harbour of all beings.
!note "Common notions" are the self-evident axioms all men grant before any proof. The Symposium 207a-208b: living things will fight, starve and die for their young, through love of the good and of immortality.

@p
This, too, is what all beings most of all believe in. And through this, joining and union with it is called faith by the theologians; and not by them alone, but also by Plato (if I may say what seems to me so), who proclaims in the Laws the kinship of this faith with truth and love. The many do not see that one who has an idea of these, speaking of their contraries, draws the same conclusion about the swervings from this triad. Plato clearly says in the Laws that the lover of falsehood is not to be trusted, and that he who is not to be trusted is without friends. So the lover of truth must be trustworthy, and he who is trustworthy must be well fitted for friendship. From these, then, we can see divine truth, faith and love, and grasp by reasoning their stable sharing with one another. If you are willing, let us first recall that Plato calls faithfulness the virtue that reconciles those who disagree and puts down the greatest of wars, I mean civil strife in cities. From this, faith appears to be the cause of union, sharing and stillness. And if there is such a power in us, it is far more, and first, in the gods themselves. For as Plato speaks of a divine temperance, justice and science, how could faith, which holds together the whole order of the virtues, not exist among the gods? In short, there are three things that fill divine natures and are the sources of fullness to all the higher kinds of beings: goodness, wisdom and beauty. And again there are three that gather together the natures that are filled, second to the former but running through all the divine orders: faith, truth and love. All things are saved through these and joined to their first causes: some through the frenzy of love, others through divine philosophy, others through theurgic power, which is better than all human wisdom, and holds within it the good of prophecy, the cleansing powers of perfecting good, and, in short, all that comes from being possessed by the gods. Of these things, perhaps, we will speak again at a better time.
!note Laws V 730c: "the man who loves falsehood is not to be trusted, and he who is not to be trusted is without friends". Laws I 630a-c: faithfulness in dangers (pistotēs) is the virtue needed in civil war, which is the worst war. Divine temperance, justice and science: the Phaedrus 247d.

@ch I.26 | The Phaedo's axioms: the divine, the immortal and the intelligible
In the Phaedo Socrates sets the soul between what is divine, immortal, intelligible, one-like, indissoluble and always the same, and what is the contrary. The divine is being that shares in the One; the names "god" are given, by Plato, to henads first and then to intellects, souls, daemons and even men. There are many orders of immortality, from the renewed immortality of the heavens up to the gods. The intelligible, too, is said in many ways. The divine is first, the immortal second, the intelligible third.

@p
Again, if you are willing, let us look into Plato's common teachings about divine natures from other dialogues. Where, then, shall we start, and what teachings shall we take, as we proceed in our search in a natural order? Shall we next recall what is written in the Phaedo? In his proofs of the soul's immortality drawn from its likeness to the divine, Socrates says that the being above the soul (to which the soul is by nature like, and being like it, shares in an immortal lot) is divine and immortal, intelligible and one-like, indissoluble, and always the same; but the being below the soul is wholly the contrary, and to it belong corruption and being acted on. For such a thing is perceptible and of many forms, and dissoluble, being compound; and he says of these all that belongs to a bodily existence. Let us then turn our attention to these common teachings, and examine how each of them belongs to the gods.
!note The Phaedo 80a-b: the soul is most like "the divine, immortal, intelligible, one-like, indissoluble and always the same", the body like their contraries.

@p
First, then, what do we look to when we speak of what is said to be divine? From what has been said, it is clear that every god exists by the highest union of beings. For to us going up from bodies, the gods have appeared as henads beyond being, the generators, perfecters and measures of beings, who bind all first beings to themselves. But what is divine is not only the hyparxis and the one in each order of being, but at once what shares and what is shared: of these, what is shared is a god, and what shares is divine. Whether there is something separate and unshared before the shared henads will be clear in what follows. For now we will define the divine as this: being that shares in the one, or the one existing together with being, drawn together. For in the gods we take everything except the one, that is, being, life and intellect, as hanging from them and second. For the gods do not exist in these, but before them; they produce and hold these in themselves, but are not defined in them. We must not fail to see that these are really distinguished in this way. Yet in many places Plato grandly praises what shares in the gods by the same names, and calls them gods. Not only the Athenian Stranger in the Laws calls a divine soul a god, but so does Socrates in the Phaedrus. For he says that "the horses and charioteers of the gods are all good and of good stock"; and afterwards, still more clearly, "and this is the life of the gods". But this is not yet remarkable. Is it not admirable that he should call gods those beings who are always joined to the gods, and with them complete one series? For in many places he calls daemons gods, though they come after the gods in being and exist about them. In the Phaedrus, the Timaeus and other dialogues you will find him stretching the name of the gods as far as daemons. And, what is stranger still, he does not refuse to call certain men gods: in the Sophist he calls the Stranger from Elea so.
!flag but at the same time is that which participates and that which is participated; of which the latter is a God, but the former is divine | Taylor's "latter" and "former" are the wrong way round for his own order of words: what is shared (the henad) is a god, and what shares in it (being) is divine. The modern English follows the sense.
!flag Whether however, prior to the participated unities, there is something which is separate and participated | Taylor's "and participated" should be "and not participated", as the sense requires: the question is whether there is an unshared henad (the One) before the shared ones.
!note The Phaedrus 246a-b, 248a; the Laws X 899b; the Sophist 216a-b, where Theodorus calls the Stranger "not a god, but divine", and Socrates answers that such philosophers do seem divine.

@p
From all that has been said, then, we must take this: one thing is a god simply; another by union; another by sharing; another by contact; and another by likeness. Of the natures beyond being, each is a god first; of the intellectual natures, each is a god by union; of divine souls, each is a god by sharing. Divine daemons are gods by contact with the gods; and the souls of men are given this name by likeness. Each of these, as we have said, is rather divine than a god. For the Athenian Stranger calls intellect itself divine. But what is divine comes after the first godhead, as the united comes after the One, the intellectual after intellect, and the ensouled after soul. And always the more one-like and simple natures go first, while the series of beings ends in the One itself. Let this, then, be the definition and distinction of the divine.
!flag the series of beings ends in the one itself | "Ends" in the sense of having its summit there: going upward, the series of beings ends in the One.

@p
Next, let us look at the immortal. For in Plato there are many orders of immortality, running from on high as far as the last of things; and the last echo, as it were, of immortality is in those visible natures that are everlasting, which, the Stranger from Elea says in his account of the circuit of the universe, are given by the father a renewed immortality. For every body has its being and life hanging from another cause, but is not by nature fit to hold together, adorn or keep itself. The immortality of partial souls is, I think, clearer and more perfect than this, as Plato shows by many proofs in the Phaedo and in the tenth book of the Republic. By the immortality of partial souls I mean the one that is more ruling, as holding in itself the cause of everlasting remaining. We will not be wrong, however, if we set the immortality of daemons before both of these. For the kinds through which they exist are incorruptible: they neither lean towards mortality nor are filled with the nature of things that come to be and pass away. But I infer that the immortality of divine souls is still more worthy of reverence, and higher in being, than that of daemons. These souls we say are the first self-moving natures, the sources and principles of the life divided about bodies, through which bodies gain a renewed immortality. If, however, before these you think of the gods themselves and the immortality in them, and how in the Symposium Diotima does not give this kind of immortality even to daemons, but defines it as being in the gods alone, such an immortality will appear to you separate, and set apart from the whole of things. For there is eternity, the source of all immortality, and through it all things live and have life, some an everlasting life, others a life scattered into non-being. In short, the divine is immortal so far as it generates and holds in itself an everlasting life. For it is immortal not as sharing in life, but as the giver of a divine life, and as making life itself divine, whether you are willing to call such a life intelligible or by some other name.
!flag a renovated immortality | The Statesman 270a: the universe receives from its maker "a renewed (or acquired) immortality"; its body does not hold itself together, but is renewed by its cause.
!note The Symposium 202d-203a: love is not a god but a daemon, between mortal and immortal.

@p
Next, let us turn our attention to the intelligible. It is so called in contrast to what is perceptible, and grasped by opinion together with sense. For the intelligible first comes into light in the chief causes. Soul is intelligible, of this lot, set apart from perceptible things, and has a being separated from them. Before soul, intellect is intelligible; for we think it better to set soul in the middle than to count it among the first beings. That too is called intelligible which is older than intellect, which fills thinking and of itself brings it to perfection, and which Timaeus sets before the making intellect and intellectual activity, in the rank of a pattern. But above these is the divine intelligible, defined by union itself and a divine hyparxis. For this is intelligible as what intellect desires, as perfecting and holding intellect, and as the fullness of being. In one way, then, we must call the hyparxis of the gods intelligible; in another, true being and the first being; in another, intellect and all intellectual life; and in another, soul and the order of souls. And we must not shape the different natures of things to fit their names. Such, then, is the order of this triad: the divine is unmixed and ranks first; the immortal is second; and the intelligible is third. For the first of these is being made divine; the second is life existing by the immortality of the gods; and the third is intellect, called intelligible because it is filled with union.
!note Timaeus 28a-29a, 39e: the Craftsman makes the world looking to the intelligible living being as his pattern.

@ch I.27 | The one-like, the indissoluble and the always the same; the unbegotten
The Phaedo's second triad answers to the first: the one-like to the divine, the indissoluble to the immortal, the always the same to the intelligible. The gods are also unbegotten, above every generation, even the soul's; the births of gods in myths are symbols of their procession from causes, as the Orphics call the first cause Time.

@p
After this, we should next consider the one-like, the indissoluble, and what is always the same, from the same causes, as the forerunners of all the divine orders, running through them all. The one-like is highest. It is present with the divine monad, and seems most of all suited to what is first being, in which every kind of shared henad ends. For the One is before these, as will become clear as we proceed. The indissoluble is second. For it holds and binds the extremes by divine union; since the dissoluble is what it is through lack of connection and of a power that gathers multitude into one. And what is always the same is eternal, and full of the everlastingness of the gods; from it the sharing in immortality and everlasting sameness comes to other things. So the one-like belongs with the divine, the indissoluble with the immortal, and what is always the same we must refer to the intelligible.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
Do you not see how each of these is in a way fitted to the other? The first of them, through the first henad shared by being, is, as is fitting, one-like. For if a god exists by the One, the divine will surely be one-like. What is immortal through one cause of life is likewise indissoluble. For life is the bond of dissoluble natures; Timaeus too, pointing this out to us, sets the dissoluble against the immortal: "You are not immortal," says the Craftsman, "yet you shall never be dissolved, nor meet with the fate of death." Everything mortal, then, is dissoluble, but the immortal is indissoluble. What has a renewed immortality is, for the same reason, neither indissoluble nor mortal. For being in the middle of both, it is neither of the extremes in either opposition. The third of these, set by the fullness of whole intelligibles, is at once and always the same. For the intelligible is the cause of sameness and everlasting remaining, and intellect is wholly eternal through it. These triads, then, proceed from the first and chief causes, as we showed of the triads already mentioned. But these things we will consider later.
!note Timaeus 41b: the Craftsman to the young gods, "you are not wholly immortal nor indissoluble, but you shall never be dissolved, nor meet with the fate of death, having my will as a greater bond". Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
With these discussed, let us turn our attention to the unbegotten in divine natures, and unfold what we say it is. We say that all true being is without generation, and Socrates proves in the Phaedrus that souls are unbegotten. Before these, the gods themselves are set above generations and existence in time. How, then, shall we define the unbegotten when said of a divine nature, and by what reason? Is it because the divine is set apart from all generation: not only from that in parts of time, such as we say the generation of material things is, nor only from that stretched through the whole of time, such as Timaeus shows the generation of the heavenly bodies to be, but also from the generation of soul? For Timaeus calls soul unbegotten in time, but the best of things generated. In short, a divine nature is set apart from all division and separation in being. For the procession of the gods is always by a union of second natures, set in a one-like way in the natures before them, the producers holding the produced in themselves. The undivided, the unseparated and the united, then, are really unbegotten. So if certain generations of the gods are spoken of by Plato in myths, as in Diotima's myth the birth of Aphrodite is celebrated, and of Love at the birth of Aphrodite, we must not fail to see how such things are said: they are composed to point to things by symbols, and myths, for the sake of concealment, call the inexpressible coming into light through causes "generation". In the Orphic writings, for this reason, the first cause is called Time; for there is another reason too, that existing as cause may be the same as existing in time. And the procession of the gods from the best of causes is fittingly called generation in time. So when Plato tells myths, it fits him to devise such things in keeping with the theologians; but when he speaks dialectically, searching out and unfolding divine natures by intellect and not by mystery, it fits him to praise the unbegotten being of the gods. For the gods first set in themselves the pattern of not being generated. An intellectual nature is unbegotten in a second degree, and after it the being of soul. And in bodies there is a last likeness of unbegotten power; seeing this, some after Plato have declared, without qualification, that the whole heaven is unbegotten. The gods, then, are unbegotten. But there is an order among them of first, middle and last processions, and a rising above and a being set below of powers. There are in them also one-like embraces of causes, and offspring of many forms of things caused. All things exist in one another, but in different ways. Some exist before second natures as filling them; others, as being filled, long for more perfect natures, and by sharing in their power generate what comes after them and perfect their hyparxis.
!flag in the Orphic writings, indeed, the first cause is on this account denominated Time | In the Orphic theogony the first principle is Chronos, Time; Proclus takes the name as a symbol of order by cause, not of a beginning in time.
!flag some posterior to Plato perceiving, have indefinitely shown that the whole heaven is unbegotten | Proclus seems to mean Aristotle (On the Heavens I.10-12), who holds that the heaven is unbegotten and imperishable. "Indefinitely" means without qualifying in what sense.
!note The Phaedrus 245d: the source of motion is "unbegotten and indestructible". Timaeus 34b-37a, the soul as "the best of things generated" (37a). The Symposium 203b-c: Love was conceived at the feast for Aphrodite's birth.

@ch I.28 | Father and mother causes among the gods
In the myths, the father is the higher, more one-like cause, like the monad and limit; the mother the lower, more partial one, like the dyad and the unlimited. Mothers are sometimes above their offspring, sometimes below, as Poverty is the mother of Love, and matter the mother and nurse of coming to be. Offspring either proceed from both or bind them together; some resemble the father, producing, guarding and holding, others the mother, generating, giving life and motion.

@p
With this in view, we can unfold what is said in myths about father causes and the fertile powers of mothers. Everywhere, we may suppose, the cause of a higher, more one-like nature is a father; and we may say that the cause of a lower, more partial nature exists before it in the rank of a mother. For among the gods a father answers to the monad, the cause of limit; a mother to the dyad, and to the unlimited power that generates beings. With Plato, the father cause is one-like, set in a higher order than the natures that proceed from it, and comes before its offspring in the lot of the desirable. The mother cause, again, has the form of the dyad: at one time it appears in the myths as higher than its offspring, at another as below it in being; as in the Symposium Plato calls Poverty the mother of Love. And this is so not only in myths but in the philosophic study of beings, as is clear in the Timaeus. For there Plato calls being the father, and matter the mother and nurse of coming to be. So the powers that are fertile and perfect second natures, giving life and causing separation, are mothers, set above the natures they produce. But the powers that receive the natures coming into light, multiply their activities, and stretch out even the lower lot of the offspring, are also themselves called mothers. Again, the offspring of such causes at one time proceed by union from their own principles and are filled from both the father and the mother cause; at another they hold the bond of them, set in the middle, carrying the gifts of the fathers to the mothers' laps, and turning what receives them to the completion of the first causes. Of the natures that come from two principles existing before them, some are made like the father cause, and such kinds of gods produce, guard and hold together. For to produce, to hold and to guard belong to the cause of limit. Others are made like the mother cause, and generate, give life, and give motion, the multiplying of powers, variety and processions. For all these are offspring of the unlimited and the first multitude.
!note Symposium 203b-c: Love is the son of Poros (Plenty) and Penia (Poverty). Timaeus 50d, 51a, 52d: the Forms are like a father, the receptacle like a mother and nurse. Limit and the unlimited: the Philebus 23c-27c. Taylor's footnote supplies "father" in the Greek he translated.

@ch I.29 | The divine names, from the Cratylus
The first names are in the gods themselves; the second, intellectual images of these, belong to daemons; the third are made by wise men, who, imitating the Craftsman, fashion names as statues of the gods, as theurgy calls the gods into statues. So Socrates in the Philebus fears greatly for the names of the gods.

@p
This, then, may be enough about the unbegotten hyparxis of the gods. It remains, I think, to speak of the divine names. For Socrates in the Cratylus sees fit to unfold with great care the rightness of names among divine natures. Parmenides, in the first hypothesis, just as he denies of the One everything else that is known, and all knowledge, so also denies it name and speech. But in the second hypothesis, besides everything else, he shows that this one can be spoken of and has a name. In short, we must grant that the first, chief and truly divine names are set in the gods themselves. We must say that the second names, imitations of the first, which exist in an intellectual way, are of the lot of daemons. And again we may say that the names third from the truth, devised by reason and receiving the last likeness of divine natures, are unfolded by men of science, acting at one time divinely and at another by intellect, and generating moving images of their inward visions. For as the making intellect sets up likenesses about matter of the first Forms in itself, and produces images in time of things eternal, divided images of undivided things, and, as it were, shadowy images of true beings, so too, I think, our science, imitating intellect's production, fashions likenesses of other things and of the gods themselves. It shows what is uncompounded in them by composition, what is simple by variety, and what is united by multitude; and so, shaping names, it shows at last images of divine natures. For it generates every name as if it were a statue of the gods. And as the theurgic art, through certain symbols, calls the overflowing and ungrudging goodness of the gods to light up statues made by art, so the intellectual science of divine things unfolds the hidden being of the gods by the compositions and divisions of sounds. Rightly, then, does Socrates in the Philebus say that out of reverence for the gods he feels the greatest fear about their names. For we must revere even the last echoes of the gods, and, revering these, become settled in their first patterns. So much, then, about divine names, which may be enough for now for understanding Plato's theology. For we will discuss them exactly when we speak of the partial powers.
!note The Parmenides 142a (the One of the first hypothesis has no name), 155d (the One of the second can be named). The Cratylus 400d-401a: we do not know the gods' true names, "which they call themselves". The Philebus 12c: "my fear about the names of the gods is not human, but beyond the greatest fear". Taylor's footnote supplies a word of the Greek he translated.

@book II | The One, the first principle, beyond being, and how it is spoken of
Book II goes up to the first cause of all things. By three arguments Proclus shows that the first principle is the One itself, beyond being, set apart from all multitude and shared by nothing, though everything that is shares in some one. He then shows how Plato speaks of it: in the first hypothesis of the Parmenides by denials, in the Republic by the image of the sun, and in the Letters by riddles.

@ch II.1 | First argument: from the one and the many
All beings are either many only, one only, or both. If many only, with no one in them, everything falls apart: each thing is endlessly many, things are like and unlike, the same and different, moved and at rest at once, and there is no number and no knowledge. If one only, there is no whole, part, shape, place, motion, rest, sameness or likeness. So beings are both one and many; the many share in the One, which is unmixed with multitude and the cause of their being.

@p
The most fitting beginning of the study we have set ourselves is the one from which we can discover the first cause of all beings. For if we set out from this as we should, with our ideas about it made pure, we will more easily distinguish everything else. About these things, then, we must speak from the beginning as follows. All beings, and all the natures of beings, must be either many only, with no one in them, neither in each nor in all of them; or one only, with no multitude, all things being forced into one and the same power of existence; or both one and many, being being one, so that neither does multitude alone by itself overcome beings, nor are we forced to bring all things and their contraries together into the same thing at once. These being three, which shall we choose? To which of these claims shall we give our vote? We must discuss one by one the absurdities that follow from these positions, and so see how the truth stands.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated ("cause" for "being"). The method follows the Parmenides, which tests what follows if the One is, and if it is not; and the Sophist (244b-245e), against those who say that all is many or all is one.

@p
If beings are many, and many in the way we said at the start, so that the One is nowhere to be found, many absurdities follow; or rather, the whole nature of beings is destroyed at once from the first, since there will at once be nothing able to share in the One. For we must grant that every being is either some one thing, or nothing. What is some being is also one; what is not even one being does not exist at all. So if many things exist, each of the many is something, a certain one. But if each of them is nothing, not even one thing, the many cannot exist either, for the many are many so far as each member of the multitude exists. So if the many alone exist, and the One in no way is, the many will not exist either. For things that are in no way one do not exist at all. And if the One is not, far less do the many exist; for it follows of necessity that none of the things the many are made of will exist.
!note Compare the Parmenides 165e-166c: if the One is not, nothing is, neither one nor many.

@p
Further, if the many alone exist, as we said, all things will be endlessly unlimited; and whichever of these unlimited things you take, this too will at once be unlimited. And of the things that make it up, which are unlimited, each too will be unlimited. For take some one of the many, which we say is not one. This will be a multitude by its own nature, since it belongs to beings and is not nothing. But if it is a multitude, it too will be made of many things, and will be many. And if you take something of these many, it will at once appear to you not one but many. The same reasoning will at once hold for these; and in the same way each (and we say "each" falsely) will be a multitude in act. Each, so to speak, will be unlimited, or rather endlessly unlimited. For there is nothing that will not be of this kind, since a part is many, and in the same way the part of a part, and so on without end. For multitude, as it proceeds, will never stop, nor will unlimitedness, being deprived of the nature of the One. But to make beings endlessly unlimited is impossible, both in truth and for our purpose. For if being is endlessly unlimited, being can be neither known nor discovered, since the unlimited cannot be grasped or known at all. If being is endlessly unlimited, there will also be something more unlimited than the unlimited. But if something is more unlimited, something else will be less unlimited. And what is less unlimited, since it is not perfectly unlimited, will clearly be limited, so far as it falls short of the nature of the unlimited. So if there is something more unlimited in multitude than what is unlimited in multitude, there will be something more than the unlimited, and the unlimited will be less, and that not in multitude. But this is impossible. So there is no endlessly unlimited.
!flag will be multitude in energy | That is, many in act and not only in potential: each supposed part would actually be many, without end.
!note The Parmenides 164c-165a: if the One is not, each mass of the others is endlessly many, and the smallest appears huge.

@p
Again, on this hypothesis the same things will be like and unlike in the same respect. For if all the many are not one, and each thing in all respects is not one, what is not one will clearly undergo the same thing through being deprived of the One. All things, then, being deprived of the One in the same way, will for this reason be in the same state with respect to each other. And things in the same state, so far as they are so, are clearly like each other. So the many will be like each other so far as they are deprived of the One. But by this same deprivation of the One they will be wholly unlike. For things that are like must undergo the same thing, so that things that undergo nothing the same will not be like. And things that undergo something the same undergo one thing. So things deprived of every one will not undergo anything the same. The many, then, will be like and unlike in the same respect. But this is impossible. So it is impossible for the many to exist that are in no way one.
!note The Parmenides 165c-d: the others, if the One is not, appear both like and unlike.

@p
Moreover, the many will be the same as and different from each other in the same respect. For if all things are equally deprived of the One, then, so far as all are equally deprived, they will be the same by this deprivation; since things that are in the same way by possession are the same, and so are things deprived in the same way by deprivation. But so far as each of them is deprived of every one, so far the many will be different from each other. For if the One in the many is the same, what is in no way one will in no way be the same. The many, then, will be the same and not the same as each other. And if they are the same and not the same, they are clearly different from each other. For what is the same and not the same, so far as it is not the same, is not the same by nothing else than the different. Further, these many will be moved and unmoved, if the One is not. For if each of them is not one, they will be unmoved by the deprivation of the One. For if what is not one were changed, each of them would come to have the One; since when deprivations are changed, they always lead what is changed into possessions. So what is not one must remain unmoved by the deprivation of the One; though this very thing is impossible, that the many should stand still. For everything that stands still is in something the same, either in the same form or in the same place. And everything in the same is in one thing that is the same, for the same in which it is is one thing. So everything that stands still is in one thing. But the many do not share in the One. And it is wholly impossible that things that do not share in the One should be in some one thing. And things not in one thing cannot stand still, since things that stand still are always in one and the same thing. So it is impossible that the many should stand still and remain unmoved. Yet it was shown that the many must stand unmoved. The same things, then, by the same condition (I mean the deprivation of the possession of the One) are moved and unmoved. For unmoved things, and things that stand still, so far as they are unstable, must so far appear to be moved.
!flag privations being changed, entirely lead into habits the things that are changed | Taylor's "privation" and "habit" are Aristotle's pair, deprivation (sterēsis) and possession (hexis): a thing that changes out of lacking something comes to have it.

@p
Moreover, there is no number of beings if the One in no way is; all things and each thing will be not one. For the monad, the smallest part of number, is one, and every number itself is one. If there are five units, there is the pentad; if three, the triad. But the triad itself is a certain unity, and so is the pentad. So if there is no one, there will be neither any part nor the whole of numbers. For how can there be any number if the One does not exist? The One is the principle of numbers. But if the principle does not exist, the things that proceed from it cannot exist either. So if the One does not exist, there will be no number.
!note The Parmenides 165b-c: without the One, the others cannot be numbered.

@p
Again, there will be no knowledge of beings if the One is not. For it will not be possible to speak of or think of any being. For each thing itself, and everything we can speak of and on which we stamp the nature of the One, will not exist, because the One does not exist. So there will be no speech and no knowledge. For speech, if it is complete, is one thing made of many things. And knowledge exists when what knows becomes one with what is known. But if there is no union, there will be no knowledge of things, and it will be impossible to speak about things we know. To this we may add that what cannot be unfolded in each of the unlimited things will always escape the limit of knowledge. For each seeming unlimited thing that the knower wants to understand will at once slip away from the power of knowing as it hastens to touch it and hold to it, since it can be neither touched nor held. If, then, the many alone exist and the One does not exist at all, so many absurdities, and still more, must follow for those who take up this hypothesis.

@p
But if the One, the One itself, alone exists, and there is nothing else (for if there were, there would be not one but many things, since one and another are more than one, and not one thing only), then among all things there will be neither a whole nor what has parts. For everything that has parts is many, and every whole has parts. But the One is in no way many. So there will be neither a whole nor what has parts. Further, there cannot be a beginning or end of anything. For what has a beginning, middle and end is divisible. But the One is not divisible, since it has no parts. So it has neither beginning, middle nor end. Again, if the One alone exists, no being will have a shape. For everything that has a shape is either straight, or round, or mixed from these. If it is straight, it will have as its parts the middle and the ends. If it is round, there will be one thing in it as a middle, and other things as the ends, to which the middle reaches. And if it is mixed from the straight and the round, it will be made of many things, and will not be one.
!note The Parmenides 137c-138a: the One of the first hypothesis is neither a whole nor has parts, has no beginning, middle or end, and no shape.

@p
Moreover, no being will be either in itself or in another. For what is in another is different from what it is in. But if the One alone exists and nothing else (for it will by no means be in another), there will be no being that is in another. And what is in itself will at once hold and be held; and in it to hold will not be the same as to be held, nor will the account of both be the same. So there will be two things, and no longer the One alone. Again, no being will be moved. For if it is moved, it must be changed. And if changed, it must be in another. But if the One alone exists, nothing can appear to be in something else. So no being can be changed. Yet everything that stands still must be in the same. And what is in the same is in some same thing. But the One is in no same thing. For what is in some thing is either in itself or in something else. And it has been shown that it is neither in itself nor in another. So it is not in some same thing either. Nor, then, does any being stand still.
!note The Parmenides 138a-139b: the One is neither in itself nor in another, neither moved nor at rest. Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
Moreover, it is impossible for anything to be the same as, or different from, anything. For if there is nothing besides the One itself, there is nothing that will be the same as or different from something else, since there will be no other being. And the One itself will not be different from itself, for then it would be many and not one. Nor will it be the same as itself. For the same is something other, and the same is not the One itself. The One is simply one, because it is not many; but what is the same is the same as something else. Again, nothing can be like or unlike anything. For everything like undergoes something the same, and everything unlike something different. But the One cannot undergo anything, nor can anything else besides the One, since nothing else exists at all if the One alone exists.
!note The Parmenides 139b-140b: the One is neither the same nor different, neither like nor unlike, itself or another.

@p
Further, we say that nothing can be touched or be separate, if there is nothing besides the One. For how can things that do not exist be separate, or touch anything? And the One cannot be separate from itself or touch itself, for then it would undergo being touched and being separate. But the One undergoes nothing besides itself. Likewise, nothing can be equal or unequal to anything. For what is equal to another is said to be so in relation to another, and so too with the unequal. But nothing else exists, if the One alone exists. Nor can the One be equal or unequal to itself. If unequal, there will be one thing in it greater and another less, so that it will be two and not one. And if the One is equal to itself, the One will measure itself. But this is impossible. For the One will measure and be measured by itself, so that it will not be the One itself. So there will be no equality or inequality among beings. But if these things are impossible (that, if the One alone exists, free of multitude and without all these things, no being can touch another or be separate, be like or unlike, the same or different, stand still or be moved, be in anything at all, have a shape, be a whole or have parts), and if it is also impossible, as was shown before, for the many alone to exist, then every being must be both many and one.
!note The Parmenides 140b-d, 148d-149d: touching and being separate, equal and unequal.

@p
If this is so, either the many must share in the One, or the One in the many, or both must share in each other, or neither in the other, the many being separate and the One separate too, so that the many and the One may exist, as reason shows. If, then, neither the One shares in the many nor the many in the One, the same absurdities will follow that we gathered on the hypothesis that the many alone exist. For again there will be the many separate from the One. For if the One exists by itself, and the many do not share in the One in any way, the many will be endlessly unlimited, like and unlike, same and different, moved and stable, and there will be neither number nor knowledge of the many. For the absence of the One forces all these consequences on the many. So it is impossible that neither the many share in the One nor the One in the many.

@p
But if the One shares in the many and the many in the One, and both are in each other, there must be another nature besides these, which is neither one nor many. For since both are mixed in each other, there must be a cause of their mixture, which joins multitude to the One and the One to multitude. For everything mixed must have a cause of its mixture. In short, if the One and multitude share in each other, neither is the One the cause of being to multitude, nor multitude to the One, but some third thing, before them, is the cause of being to both. For what will make this to be multitude, and that to be one? And what is the cause of their sharing and keeping company with each other, when the One, so far as it is one, never shares with the many? For the many as many and the One as one are different from each other; and so far as neither comes from the other, they have no sympathy with each other. What, then, gathers them into one, when they flee each other and are unmixed? Being so at odds, they cannot desire each other; or if they did, their coming together would be by chance. For if this happened, there was a time when they were separate from each other, since now they exist together by a cause. But it is impossible for the many to exist separate from the One. So the mixture is not by chance. Nor does the mixture come from the many, if neither the One is the cause of the many, nor the many of the One. What, then, is this higher thing that causes the mixture? It is either one or not one. If it is the One itself, we must ask again of it whether it shares in multitude or in nothing. If it shares, clearly some other thing before it will for the same reason come into view, and so on without end. But if such a thing is wholly free of multitude, then what was said at first will not be true, that the many do not share in the One, nor the One in the many. I mean, though, what is most chiefly and first one. For there is a one in the many, and there is also the unshared One, which is simply one and nothing else. If, however, what is before both is not one, this not-one must be higher than the One. Yet all things are, and come to be what they are, through the One. Together with the One, every being is kept; separate from the One, it goes to its own destruction. And the mixture of the One and multitude, which the not-one would give to beings, is sharing and union. So the One and the not-one would be the cause of nothing else to beings than of their being one. But if the One is the cause of this, the not-one will not be the cause of anything higher than union. Yet everywhere what is higher must be the cause to beings of some other, higher thing, by its own power. For this is how it will be higher: as more good, and as the cause from its own nature of a greater and higher good to those things to which a lesser good is the cause of lesser goodness. From this, then, it follows that the many must share in the One, that the One must be unmixed with multitude, that nothing must be better than the One, and that it must also be the cause of being to the many. For everything deprived of the One at once flees into nothing and its own destruction. But what is not many is not at the same time not many and nothing. For to the One is opposed what is nothing, or not one; and to the many, what is not many. If, then, the One and the many are not the same, being not many will not be the same as being nothing. Considering the matter this way, then, it appears that the One is beyond multitude and is the cause of being to the many.
!flag since now also they subsist together causally. It is however impossible for the many to subsist separate from the one. The mixture therefore is not causal | Taylor's "causally" here must mean "by chance" (the Greek is probably kata symbebēkos or apo tychēs): the argument is that the mixture is not a matter of chance, since the many cannot ever have existed apart from the One.
!flag The one therefore, and that which is not one, are the cause of nothing else to beings than of their being one | That is, even a supposed not-one above the One would give beings nothing but union, which is what the One gives; so it would be no higher than the One. Taylor's footnotes correct and supply words of the Greek here.

@ch II.2 | Second argument: from the kinds of principle
There must be one principle or many. Many principles, whether out of sympathy or in it, finite or unlimited, all need a one before them. The one principle is not a body, since a body needs something undivided to hold it together, and an unlimited power to last; nor a bodiless power tied to bodies, or there would be no intellect; nor moved, since it lacks nothing. Nor is it being: the One comes before being, and the principle is not below being, nor shared by it. So the cause of all is beyond all being.

@p
We must now treat the same subject in another way, and see again whether we can in some way follow what has been said and bring it to the same end. Let us begin from this. There must be either one principle or many. If there are many principles, they must either be in sympathy with each other or torn apart from each other, and they must be either finite or unlimited. If there is one principle, it must be either not being or being. If it is being, it must be either bodily or bodiless. If bodiless, either separate from bodies or inseparable from them. If separate, either moved or unmoved. And if it is not being, it must be either lower than all being, or shared by being, or unshared. If, then, there are many principles, with no sympathy with each other, no being will come from them jointly, nor will they be common to all things, but each will produce by itself. For what sharing can there be between things foreign by nature, or what working together between things wholly different in kind? Besides this, there will be the many that do not share in the One. For if there is some one common in all of them, they will not be wholly separated in being from each other. If, then, they are different, and there is nothing the same about them, they are many only and in no way one. But if there are many principles, in sympathy with each other, they will have something common that leads all of them to sympathy, and shows all of them alike. For we call those things sympathetic that are affected by the same thing. And like things are like by sharing in one form and one nature. If this is so, this whole that is everywhere and in all the principles must be more of a principle than the many. It gives them the power to be in sympathy with each other, and gives them sharing by nature.
!flag they must be divulsed from each other | Taylor's "divulsed" means torn apart: principles with nothing in common.

@p
Again, if there are unlimited principles, either the things that come from them are unlimited, and there will be the unlimited twice over; or they are limited, and so not all the principles will be principles.
