For things limited in number will always come from limited principles. So the principles are unlimited in vain. Besides, unlimitedness makes both the principles and what comes from them unknown. For if the principles are unknown, what comes from them must be unknown, since we think we know anything when we know its causes and first principles. But if the principles are limited, there will clearly be some number of them; for we say that number is a defined multitude. And if there is a number of principles, there must be a cause of their whole number. For every number comes from one; and this, the One, is the principle of numbers. This, then, will be the principle of principles, and the cause of limited multitude, since number itself is one, the end in the many is one, and it bounds the many by what is one. But if the principle is one, and it is being, then, if this is granted, whether it is bodily or bodiless, it must be acknowledged as the principle of other things.
!note Aristotle, Physics I.4 (187b): if the principles are unlimited, what comes from them cannot be known.

@p
If, then, body is the cause of the generation of beings, it must be divisible and have parts. For every body is divisible by its own nature, since every magnitude is a certain whole, and a whole is made of parts. These parts (I mean each of them) must either share in some one, or not. If they do not, they will be many only, and in no way one. So neither will what is made of them be a whole. For if there is no one, what is made of all of them will not be one. But if each of the parts shares in something of this kind, and there is something the same in all of them, it must be bodiless and undivided in its own nature. For if it too is bodily, it is either wholly in each of the parts, or not wholly. If wholly in each, it will be separated from itself, for the parts it is in are separate from each other. If not wholly in each of the parts, it too will be divisible, and will have parts as the parts already named do; and the same question will arise again about these, whether there is something common in them too, or nothing. For if there is nothing common, we will set the many apart from the One.

@p
Let us consider the whole, though; for every body is a whole and has parts. What, then, will hold together the parts, since they are many? We must say either that the whole unifies the parts, or the parts the whole, or that some third thing before both, which neither is a whole nor has any part, holds together and unites the whole with its parts and the parts with the whole. If the whole holds the parts together, the whole will be bodiless and undivided. For if it is a body, it too will be divisible, and will need a nature able to hold the parts together; and so on without end. But if the parts hold the whole together, how can the many hold together the one, and divided things what is made of them? For, on the contrary, the one must have the power of uniting the many, and not the many of uniting the one. And if what holds both together neither is a whole nor has parts, it will be wholly undivided. But being undivided, it must also be without extension. For everything with extension has parts and is divisible. And being without extension, it is bodiless; for every body has extension.

@p
Further, the principle must be everlasting; for every being is everlasting or perishable. So we must grant that the principle of beings is everlasting or perishable. But if we grant that it can perish, there will be no imperishable being. For if the principle is destroyed, it will not itself be generated from anything, nor will anything else be generated from it. For it cannot generate itself (since it is not, if it is not everlasting), nor can anything else generate it, if it is the principle of all things. But if it is imperishable, it will have the power of not perishing, and this power will be unlimited, so that it may exist without end through the whole of time. For every limited power of existence belongs by nature to what is perishable, while an unlimited power belongs to everlasting natures, whose existence goes on without end. This unlimited, then, I mean unlimited in power, is either undivided or divisible. If divisible, there will be the unlimited in a limited body. For the principle is limited; if it were unlimited, there would be nothing else besides it. But if it is undivided, the power of existing without end will be bodiless. And the principle of beings is bodiless, so far as it is this power by which what underlies it always is. From this, then, it is clear that the principle of beings cannot be bodily.
!note Aristotle, Physics VIII.10 (266a-267b): no limited body can have an unlimited power, so the first mover is without magnitude. Taylor's footnote supplies the words "is bodiless" in the Greek he translated.

@p
If it is bodiless, it must be either separate from bodies or inseparable from them. If inseparable, it will have all its activities in bodies and about them. For what is inseparable from body is what is by nature fit to act nowhere except in and with bodies. And if the principle is of this kind, it is clearly necessary that none of the things that exist by it should be more powerful or have more authority than the principle of all beings. But if nothing in bodies is higher than the power that exists in bodies and acts about them, and a bodily being, there will nowhere be intellect, or the power that acts by intellect. For every such motion (that is, activity) comes from a power that is, in its activities, wholly free of body. And it never was, nor is, lawful for what is generated to go beyond the power of its causes. For everything in what is begotten comes from first natures, and these are the masters of the being of what comes after them. So if the principle of beings can generate intellect and wisdom, how could it not generate it, on its own account and in itself? For one of two things is necessary: either intellectual perception belongs in no way to beings, or it is lower than them, and if it exists, acts in bodies only. But these things cannot be said. And if what is first of beings, and the principle of all things, is separate from bodies, we must certainly grant that it is either unmoved or moved. If it is moved, there will be something else before it, about which it is moved. For everything moved is by nature fit to be moved about something else that remains. And besides this, it is moved through desire for something else. For it must be moved because it desires something, since motion by itself is undefined; but its end is that for the sake of which it is. It desires either something else or itself. But everything that desires itself is unmoved. For why should anything present to itself want to be in something else? Of things that are moved, the motion is less in that to which the good is nearer, and greater in that from which the good is further. But what has the good in itself, and that for the sake of which it is, will be unmoved and stable, since, being always in itself, it is in the good. And what is in itself is in the same; for each thing is the same as itself. Of what is in itself we say that it stands still and is unmoved; while what is not unmoved is not in itself but in another, is moved towards another, and wholly lacks the good. If, then, the principle of beings is moved, and everything moved is moved through lack of good, towards something else it desires, there will be something else, besides itself, desired by the principle of beings, about which, as always the same, we must say it is moved. But this is impossible. For the principle is that for the sake of which all things are, which all things desire, and which lacks nothing. If it lacked something, it would be wholly below what it lacked, to which its activity was directed as to what it desired. But if the principle is unmoved (for this is what remains), it must be one bodiless being, eternally the same. But how does it have the One? And how is it one being? If being and the One are the same, we must grant that the principle of beings is being. But if being is different from the One, we must grant that to be one is not the same as to be being. And if being is better than the One, then by this it must be said to be with the principle. But if the One is better than being and beyond it, the One is also the principle of being. And if they rank together, the many will be before the One. But this is impossible, as we showed before. It is clear, indeed, that being is not the same as the One. For to say "one" and to say "being is one" are not one and the same; the first is not yet a sentence, and the second is. Besides, if being and the One are the same, multitude will be the same as what does not exist and is not being. But this is impossible. For the many are held in being, and the One is in what is not being. But if being and the One are not the same, they will not rank together; for if they did, there would be something else before them, if all things must come from one principle. And if one of them is better than the other, either the One is before being or being before the One. If the One is before being, this and not being is the principle of all things. For nothing must be better than the principle. And if being is before the One, the One will be an attribute of being, and not being of the One. But if the One is an attribute of being, the One and being must be everything, and all things that are one must be being, but not all things that are being must be one. There will, then, be some being deprived of the One. But if this is so, it will be nothing. For what is deprived of the One is nothing. So the One is before being.
!flag the one will be passive to essence | Taylor's "passive to" means "an attribute of", something that being "undergoes" or has: if being were before the One, oneness would be only a property of being. Taylor's footnote: if being and the One ranked together, they would be two, and so share in the first many.
!note "Many are in being, and the One is in what is not being": things that do not exist are not many, but even "nothing" is spoken of as one. Aristotle, Metaphysics XII.7 (1072a-b): the first mover moves as desired, being itself unmoved.

@p
But if what is first is something that is not being, it is absurd to say that it is below being. For the principle is what has the greatest power and the most absolute authority, and is most sufficient to itself; it is not the meanest thing, needing the many. In short, no second nature must be better than the principle, for beings must not be badly governed. But if the principle has an order lower than the things that proceed from it, and these are better than it, all things will be badly confused, and the principle will by nature be nothing else than something that is not the best of things, and the things that proceed from it will not have from it a power of ruling over their principle. The principle of beings, then, will be by chance, and so will the beings that are its offspring. But this is impossible. For things by chance (if to be by chance is not to exist by intellect, nor with a view to a defined end) are disordered, unlimited and undefined, and are all things that happen less often. But the principle is always principle, and other things proceed from it. If, then, what is not being is better than all being, it will be either shared by it, or wholly unshared. If being shares in the principle, of what will it be the principle? And how will it be the principle of all beings? For the principle of beings must be none of the beings; if it were one of them, it would certainly not be the principle of all beings. And everything shared by another is said to belong to what shares in it, and to be first in it. But the principle is separate, and belongs more to itself than to other things. Besides, everything shared proceeds from another, higher cause; for the unshared is better than the shared. And it is not possible to think of anything better than what is best, which we call the principle. For it is not lawful to say that things second to the principle, which proceed from it, are in any way better than their principle. So the cause of all beings is above all being, separate from every being, and is neither being, nor has being added to its nature. For such an addition is a lessening of its simplicity and of its being one.
!note "Beyond being": Republic VI 509b, the Good is "not being, but beyond being in dignity and power".

@ch II.3 | Further arguments: the One is everywhere, and the One first
The first cause must be shared in by all beings. Not life, rest, intellect or being is in everything; only the one is, for whatever is deprived of the one is nothing. So the one everywhere comes from the first One. Again, causes cannot go on without end either way, or in a circle, nor can the higher come from the lower; so the first is one, and the last too. Many principles need a one that orders them, and the unshared One comes before the one in the many.

@p
See, then, the third argument after these, leading us to the same conclusion as the earlier arguments. The cause of all beings must be that in which all beings share, to which they refer their existence, and which is separated from nothing that is said to exist in any way. For this alone is the object of desire to beings which, first or in some other way, is itself the cause of their existence. And everything produced in relation to it and because of it must have a certain relation to it, and through this a likeness to it. For every relation of one thing to another is spoken of in two ways: either both share in one thing that gives them sharing with each other; or one shares in the other, the higher giving something to what is below it, and the lower being made like the higher, so far as it shares in it. So if all perceptible natures have a relation to what is first, long for it and exist about it, either some third thing must be the cause of the relation, or the principle must give the natures after it a leaning towards itself, and the desire by which each thing is kept and exists. But nothing is higher than what is first. So the relation of beings, their existence, and their leaning towards the first come from there. And all things share in their own principle, if what is shared must appear from there in all beings, since it is the principle of all things and leaves no being. What, then, will this nature be, which is everywhere and in all beings? Is it life and motion? But many things lack these. Is rest everywhere, then, and in all things? But this is not true either. For motion, as motion, will not share in remaining at rest. Is intellect, so much honoured, as intellect, shared by all beings? This too is impossible. For then all beings would have intellectual perception, and no being would lack intellect.
!flag Is, therefore, permanency every where | Taylor's "permanency" is rest, stillness (stasis), one of the Sophist's five great kinds.

@p
Shall we say, then, that being itself, and being in the sense of what is, is shared by all things that exist in any way? But how can this be? For what is in coming to be, or passing into existence, is said to be, yet lacks being. Nor should we wonder if this too, since it ranks among beings, should at last share in being. For so far as it is coming to be, it is not; but it ends in existence and being when it has actually come to be, and is no longer rising into existence. So not all things that exist in any way share in being. What, then, will be everywhere and shared by all things? Let us consider every being, and see what all beings undergo, and what is common in all of them, as in being, sameness, difference, rest (remaining as it is) and motion. Can we say that each of these is anything other than one thing? And not only each by itself, but the things that come from them; in short, in a certain way we cannot speak of all things otherwise than this, that all things and each thing are one. For if anything were deprived of the One, whether you speak of parts or of beings, what becomes deprived of the One will at once be nothing at all. For why do we say that what is not is wholly nothing, not even one thing, unless the One is the last thing to leave beings? This, then, is what it is to become what in no way is: to be wholly deprived of the One. For it is possible for what is not moved to be, and for what has no being to have a hyparxis; but what is not even one thing, and is deprived of the One itself, will be nothing at all. So the One is present to all beings; and even if you speak of multitude itself, this too must share in the One, for if it does not become one thing, it cannot exist. And if you divide the whole without end, at once nothing else than one turns up. For either what is divided does not exist, or, coming to be or being something else, it will at once be one. The one, then, that appears everywhere, is in all beings, and leaves no being, comes either from the One that is simply one, or from what is higher than the One. For it is not possible for the one to be an attribute of anything except from the first One, to which the one is no longer present as an attribute, but which is the One itself, nothing else than one.
!flag that which is in generation, or passing into existence, is said to be, and is destitute of essence | Proclus means that what is coming to be (genesis) is spoken of as being, yet is not yet being in the full sense; Timaeus 27d-28a: what always comes to be "never really is".
!note "Being, sameness, difference, rest and motion" are the five great kinds of the Sophist (254b-255e). Taylor's footnote corrects "being" to "one" in the Greek he translated.

@p
Again, from another starting point, we can reach the same conclusion, speaking as follows. Either the causes of beings and the things caused must go on without end, with nothing first or last among beings; or there is no first, but there is a last, the unlimited being on one side only; or, on the contrary, beings go on without end from a defined principle; or there is a first and a last, and a boundary of beings both ways. And if there are boundaries of beings, either things come from each other, and the generation of beings is in a circle; or, if they do not come from each other, either one of them comes from the other, or the first is one and the last not one, or the contrary, or both are one, or neither is one. If, then, first things and the causes of beings are unlimited, each thing will be made of unlimited things. For what proceeds from a principle must share in the principle it proceeds from. And what has its existence from many causes will be of many forms in its own nature, as sharing in many powers. And what is produced from unlimited things before it will have unlimited distinctive characters from the principles, suited to itself. So every being, being unlimited and made of unlimited things, will make all things endlessly unlimited, and there will be neither knowledge of any being nor any unfolding of powers. For the power of the unlimited is wholly unknown and beyond grasp by the natures to which it is unlimited.

@p
But if things are unlimited in a downward procession, is each of them unlimited, always proceeding further down, in the same way as we say all things are? Or is each whole limited, while the beings produced from them are unlimited? For if every being is defined at its beginning but unlimited at its end, there will be, neither in parts nor in wholes, a return of beings to their own principle; nor will what is second in order ever be such as to be made like the last of the order before it; though, as we often say, the summits of the lower are joined to the boundaries of the higher. For where there is no last, by what means can such a likeness in procession, and such holding together of beings, remain, by which second things are always joined to the natures before them? But if all things alone have such an unlimitedness, each bounded by what comes after it, wholes will be lower than parts, and the parts of beings will by nature be more perfect than wholes. For wholes will have no return to the principle before them, while parts will return to it after their procession. Yet the more every being hastens to be joined to what is more perfect than itself, the more it must excel, as it seems to me. And if this whole, going on without end, is not rolled back to its own summit, and turned back in a circle and perfected by such a return, it will not desire its own good. If, however, we grant that there is unlimitedness both ways, these things must follow.
!flag the summits of inferiors are conjoined with the boundaries of superiors | A rule of Proclus' system (Elements of Theology 147): the highest member of each lower order is joined to the lowest member of the order above.

@p
Besides this, there will be no common object of desire for all beings, nor any union or sympathy among them. For things wholly unlimited have nothing first in them; and having no first, we will not be able to say what the common end of beings is, nor why some things are higher while others have a lower nature. For we call one thing better and another worse by nearness to what is best, just as we define the more and less hot by sharing in what is hot in the first degree. In short, we judge the more and the less by reference to what is greatest. So the boundary among beings must be what is first and what is last.
!note Aristotle, Metaphysics II.1 (993b): what is most of all a thing is the cause of it in others, as fire, the hottest, is the cause of heat.

@p
But if these come from each other, the same thing will be older and younger, cause and at the same time what is caused, and each thing will be first and last. For it makes no difference whether these come from each other, or the things between them do. For if the extremes are no different, how could a change in being come between them? But if one comes from the other, does the first come from the last, as some say, who generate higher things from lower, and more perfect from less perfect? But in that case, must not what has the power of generating and producing the perfect, far before that, perfect and adorn itself by the power present to it? And how could it leave itself in a lower lot and assign a higher order to another? For everything longs for its own perfection, and simply desires good, though not everything can share in it. If, then, it has the power of producing this most perfect thing, what is last will act on its own account before other things, and the whole of good, and all perfection, will be set first in itself.
!flag as some say, who generate things more excellent from things subordinate | Proclus seems to mean the old physicists, and the Pythagoreans and Speusippus as Aristotle reports them (Metaphysics XII.7, 1072b; XIV.4-5), who held that the good and the beautiful come at the end, not the beginning.

@p
But if what is last is produced from what is first, and the most imperfect from the most perfect, is each of them one, or is this one and that not one? If what is first, or what is last, is not one, neither of them will be first or last. For since there will be multitude in each, each will have the better and the worse in it. Neither will what is best be unmixed with what is lower than it, nor will what is dimmest of all in being be so low as to be wholly deprived of a more perfect nature. There will be something further out than the last, and something more perfect than the first. For everywhere, if what is best receives some addition through what is lower, it will be less perfect than what remains in the best without that addition. If, then, we say these things rightly, the One is the principle of all things, and the last of beings is one. For the end of the procession of beings must, I think, be made like the principle, and the power of the first must reach as far as this.
!flag that which is best if it receives another addition through that which is inferior will be more perfect than that which does not abide in the best | Taylor's English says the opposite of what the argument needs: what is best, if mixed with something lower, will be less perfect than what remains purely in the best. The modern English follows the argument.

@p
To sum up what we have said, then: either the first principle must be one, or there must be many first principles only, or one holding multitude in itself, or many sharing in one. But if there are many first principles only, nothing one will come from them. For what will make one thing, and a whole, if there are many principles and nothing that produces one? For the things after the principles must surely be made like them. Either, then, there will be no one in any being, or it will not come from these principles; so that each of the things that exist in any way will be divided multitude only. And again each of the parts of any being will be of this kind, and we will never stop dividing being and existence into tiny parts. For all things will be many, and the One will be nowhere in the whole of things, and neither wholes nor parts will appear.
!note Taylor's footnote: "many first principles only" means multitude without any sharing in the One.

@p
But if there must be many principles, and they share in the One, the One will be ranked together with the many. Again, though, everywhere the unranked must be older than the ranked, and what is set apart than what is shared. For how is the One in each of the many, except from one principle that orders the multitude together and turns it to itself by sharing in the One? Again, if the first One were multiplied, the One would be acted on; for at the same time it would be one and not one, and would not be that which is one only. But in each kind of thing there must be what is unmixed with a lower nature, so that there may be what is mixed, as we say of the Forms. For from the Equal itself, things here that are equal appear equal, though they are filled with a contrary nature; and from what is first being comes what is mixed with non-being, and appears as being. In short, everywhere the simple, unmixed existence of each thing comes before the things that, by falling short, are mixed with their own deprivations. So the One by itself is set apart from all multitude; and what is one and at the same time not one is not the first One, but hangs from what is first one: sharing in the One through the principle, but, through the lessening that comes with multitude, now plainly showing in itself the cause of separation.
!note The Equal itself and equal things here: the Phaedo 74a-75b. Taylor's footnote corrects "is set apart" to "hangs from" in the Greek he translated, and changes its punctuation.

@ch II.4 | Against Origen: the first principle is above intellect
Origen the Platonist stopped at intellect and first being and left out the One. Against him Proclus argues from Plato that the first cause is beyond intellect and being: from the Republic, where the Good is beyond being as the sun is beyond what it makes visible; from the Sophist, where the one being shares in the One; from the Philebus, where intellect is not the good; and from the Parmenides, where the One is neither being, nor at rest or moved, nor in itself.

@p
That the One, then, is the principle of all things and the first cause, and that all other things come after the One, is, I think, clear from what has been said. I am astonished, though, at all the other interpreters of Plato who grant the existence of the intellectual kingdom, but do not revere the inexpressible transcendence of the One, and its hyparxis above the whole of things. I wonder at it most in Origen, who shared the same schooling as Plotinus. For Origen ends in intellect and first being, and leaves out the One, which is beyond every intellect and every being. If he leaves it out as something better than all knowledge, speech and intellectual perception, we must say he disagrees neither with Plato nor with the nature of things. But if he leaves it out because the One has no hyparxis at all and no existence, because intellect is the best of things, and because what is first being is the same as what is first one, we cannot agree with him in saying these things, nor will Plato admit him and count him among his own. For I think such a teaching is far from Plato's philosophy, and full of Peripatetic novelty. If you are willing, we will bring some arguments against this teaching, and against all others who champion this opinion. We will fight hard for Plato's teaching, and show that by him the first cause is beyond intellect and set apart from all beings, as Plotinus and Porphyry, and all who have received their philosophy, take him to say.
!flag Origen, who was a partaker of the same erudition with Plotinus | This is Origen the Platonist, a fellow pupil of Plotinus under Ammonius Saccas, not the Christian Origen. He wrote a treatise "That the King is the only Maker", which Proclus takes as making intellect the first principle.
!flag because the one is perfectly unhyparctic | Taylor's "unhyparctic" means without hyparxis, without any existence of its own.

@p
We will begin, then, from the Republic. For here Socrates clearly shows that the Good is set above being and the whole intellectual order, following the analogy of the first goodness to the sun. As the ruling sun is to coming to be, to everything visible and to all that sees, by its power of generating light, so the Good must be to intellect and intelligibles, as the cause that produces truth. If this is so, we must say that the sun is set apart both from what sees and from what is seen, and must grant that the Good is above the natures that always think and those that are eternally intelligible. It is better, though, to hear Plato's own words: "You will say that the sun not only gives to visible things the power of being seen, but is also the cause of their coming to be, growth and nourishment, though it is not itself coming to be. Certainly. Say, then, that the things known not only have from the Good their being known, but their being and their very being are added to them by it, though the Good is not being, but beyond being, above it in dignity and power." Through all this, then, it is clear how the Good and the first principle are defined by Plato as spread out above not only the intellectual but the intelligible extent, and being itself, by union, just as it follows that the sun goes beyond all visible things, and perfects and generates all things by its light. How, then, following Plato, can we grant that intellect is the best of things and the cause of all things? How can we say that being itself and the very being of things are the same as the principle that leads all the divine processions? For being and intellect are said to exist first from the Good, to have their hyparxis about the Good, to be filled with the light of truth that comes from there, and to receive the share suited to them from the union of this light, which is more divine than intellect itself and being, as hanging first from the Good, and giving beings a likeness to what is first. For the light sent out from the sun makes everything visible sun-like; and sharing in the light of truth makes the intelligible good-like and divine. So intellect is a god through a light older than intellectual light and intellect itself; and what is at once intelligible and intellectual shares in a divine hyparxis through the fullness of this light given to it as fits it. In short, every divine nature is what it is said to be because of this light, and through it is united to the cause of all beings. By no means, then, is the first Good to be thought the same as intellect, nor must we grant that the intelligible is older than all the hyparxis of the whole of things, since it is even below the light that comes from the Good, and, perfected by this light, is joined in its own order to the Good itself. For we must not say that the intelligible is united to the first in the same way as the light of truth. The light, through being continuous with the Good, is set in it with nothing between; the intelligible, through this light, shares in nearness to the Good. So too among perceptible things the light of the sun is born first together with the sun's circuit, rises as far as the centre of the whole sphere, and is everywhere about it; and all perceptible things through it gain a likeness to the sun, each, by its own nature, filled with sun-like light. These things will be enough to remind those who love the contemplation of truth of Plato's ideas on this subject, and to show that the order of intellect comes after the set-apart transcendence of the One.
!note Republic VI 508a-509b; the words quoted are 509b. Taylor's footnotes: the Greek he translated has "the One itself" for "being itself", and one sentence of it is badly corrupt.

@p
If we must show the same thing through many witnesses, let us look at what the Stranger from Elea in the Sophist decides about these things. He says that the multitude of all beings, whether contraries or not, must hang from the one being (being marked by the one), and that the one being itself must hang from the One. For when we call the hot or the cold, rest or motion, being, we do not call each of these the same as being itself. If rest were being itself, motion would not be being; or if motion were such being, rest would not share in the name of being. But it is clear that being comes to rest, to motion, and to every multitude of beings from one thing that is first being. This very thing, then, which is the cause of being to all things and is shared by all other things, shares in this One; and for this reason, as it is being only, so it is first being. It is being itself, and does not have its being by sharing; but it is one by sharing, and so is acted on by the One. But it is being first. If, then, Plato gives the One an existence beyond being, as he supposes that what is first among wholes goes beyond beings, how could being not come after the One, since it shares in it and is for this reason called one?
!flag permanency or motion | Taylor's "permanency" is rest (stasis): the Sophist's kinds, rest and motion, both are, but neither is being itself (250a-c). "Rest" here means remaining still.
!note The Sophist 244b-245d: the whole of being, if it is one, is acted on by the One and is not the One itself, "for the truly One must be wholly without parts". Taylor's two footnotes correct "one" to "being" in the Greek he translated.

@p
Moreover, Socrates in the Philebus clearly shows the same thing to those who can know wholes from parts: that intellect does not have the same rank as the first cause of all. Searching for the good of the human soul and its end, sharing in which fully in every way it will reap the fruits of a happiness suited to its nature, he first removes pleasure from such an end, and after it intellect, because this too is not filled with all the marks of the good. If, then, the intellect in us is an image of the first intellect, and the good of our whole life is not to be defined by this alone, must not, among wholes too, the cause of good be set above intellectual perfection? For if what is first good existed as the whole intellect, then in us too, and in all other intellectual natures, self-sufficiency and their proper good would be present through sharing in intellect. Our intellect, indeed, is cut off from the good, and lacks it, and so needs pleasure for human perfection. But a divine intellect always shares in the good, and for this reason is divine. For it has the form of the good by sharing in good, but is divine as hanging from the first godhead. It is the same reasoning, then, that concludes that the Good is set apart from the first intellect, and that defines happiness as lying not in thinking alone, but in the all-perfect presence of the good. For the intellectual kind of activity is of itself lacking with respect to blessedness. And why go on at length? Parmenides teaches us most clearly the difference of the One from being and what is, and shows that the One is set apart from all other things and from being; for he shows this of the One at the end of the first hypothesis. And how could the cause of being, set apart from it by highest transcendence, not also be beyond the intellectual order? For intellect is being. And if in intellect there are rest and motion, and Parmenides shows that the One is above both, does he not at once bring us to the inexpressible cause of all things, which is before every intellect? And if every intellect turns back to itself and is in itself, but the One is shown to be neither in itself nor in another, how can we still think intellect the same as the first cause of all? And in what will the One before being differ from the one being, the subject of the second hypothesis, if intellect is the best of things and the first principle of all? For the one being shares in the One, and what shares is second to what is shared. That the One, by Plato, is older than intellect and being has been recalled to our memory by what has been said.
!note The Philebus 20b-22e, 60a-61a: neither pleasure nor thought alone is the good, but a mixed life; intellect is only "akin" to the good. The Parmenides 141e-142a: the One "in no way is", and is not even one; 138a-139b: it is neither in itself nor in another, neither at rest nor moved.

@ch II.5 | Two ways up to the One: analogy and negation
Plato leads us to the One in two ways: by analogy, as in the Republic's sun, and by negation, as in the Parmenides. Negations answer to the procession of all things from the One, analogies to their return to it. Negations can be above affirmations, equal to them, or below them as mere privations; here they are above. Analogies show only the likeness of what comes after to the first, which itself has no relation to anything.

@p
Next, if the One is neither intelligible nor intellectual, nor in short shares in the power of being, let us look at what ways will lead us to it, and through what intellectual ideas Plato, as far as he can, unfolds to his own the inexpressible and unknown transcendence of the first. I say, then, that at one time he unfolds it through analogy and the likeness of second natures to it, and at another shows its set-apart transcendence and its separation from the whole of things through negations. In the Republic he points to the inexpressible character and hyparxis of the Good through its analogy to the sun; in the Parmenides he shows the difference of the One from all that comes after it through negations. He seems to me to unfold, through one of these ways, the procession from the first cause of all other things, and before other things of the divine orders. For this is why the first cause is set apart from all the natures it produces: everywhere a cause is set above its effects. And this is why the first is none of all things: all things proceed from it. For it is the principle of all things, of beings and also of non-beings. But through the other of these ways he sketches the return to the first of the things that have proceeded from it. For in each order of beings, through likeness to it, there is a monad answering to the Good, which has the same relation to the whole series joined to it that the Good has to all the orders of the gods. And the cause of this likeness is surely the return of the whole of things to the Good. These things, then, proceed from there and return to it. The procession of all things shows us the ascent to the first through negations; the return of all things shows it through analogies. But let no one, thinking these negations are like privations, despise this way of discussion; nor, defining sameness in words analogously, and words in relations, try to slander this leading up to the first principle. For negations, it seems to me, show a threefold character in things. At one time, being of earlier birth than affirmations, they generate them and bring them to perfection. At another, they have a rank equal to affirmations, and negation is then in no way more worthy of reverence than affirmation. And again at another time they have a rank below affirmations, and are nothing else than their privations. For of non-being itself, with which there is also a denial of beings, at one time, thinking of it as beyond being, we say that it is the cause and giver of beings; at another we show that it is equal to being, as, I think, the Stranger from Elea shows in the Sophist that non-being is, if it is lawful to say so, in no way less than being; and at another we leave it as a privation of being and lacking being. For in this way we call all coming to be, and matter itself, non-being.
!flag nor defining the sameness in words analogously, and words in habitudes, endeavour to calumniate this anagogic progression | The sense seems to be: let no one object that analogy only finds a sameness of words or of relations (habitudes), and so slander this way that leads up (anagogic) to the first.
!note The Sophist 258b: "non-being is in no way less being than being". Matter as non-being: Plotinus II.4.16 and I.8.3.

@p
Analogies, though, are taken only to point to the likeness of second natures to the first principle. No proportion, no relation and no sharing of this principle with what comes after it appears from them. For its set-apart nature is not like what we see in the second and third orders of beings. The Good goes beyond the whole of things far more than intellect goes beyond the natures after it, whether the making intellect, or the intellect of the whole world, or any other of those called divine. Every intellect and every god has a transcendence over lower natures, and over what it causes, less than that which the first principle has over every being. For this principle goes beyond all things alike, not some more and others less, since so we would bring in a greater and lesser relation of it to second natures. We must keep it without relation to all things, and equally set apart from the whole of things. Of other natures, some are nearer to it and others further. For each thing proceeds from it, since it produces all things by one cause; and different things return to it in different ways, while this principle meanwhile receives no relation to or sharing with what comes after it.

@ch II.6 | The two names of the first: the One and the Good
As there are two ways up, there are two names: the One, in the Parmenides, which answers to the procession of all things from it, since multitude comes from the One; and the Good, in the Republic, which answers to their return, since all things desire the good. The first itself is beyond names; we name it from what comes after it. Its union with all things, which is hidden, we cannot name.

@p
The way of showing what concerns the One is, then, twofold, as we have said. And again Plato gives us two names for the inexpressible cause. In the Republic he calls it the Good, and shows it to be the source of the truth that unites intellect and intelligibles. In the Parmenides he calls such a principle the One, and shows that it gives existence to the divine henads. Again, of these names, one is the image of the procession of the whole of things, the other of their return. Because all things have their existence and proceed from the first principle, we give it the name of the One, and show that it is the cause of all multitude and every procession. For from where does multitude come into light except from the One? And because, again, what proceeds from it by nature returns to it, and desires its inexpressible hyparxis, which cannot be grasped, we call it the Good. For what else turns all things back, and is reached for by all beings as their object of desire, but the Good? All other things exist in a distributed way, and are prized by some beings but not by others; and everything said to exist in any way longs for some things and avoids others. But the Good is the common object of desire of all beings, and all things by their nature lean and reach towards it. And everywhere the leaning of desiring natures is towards the object of desire proper to them. So the Good turns back, and the One gives existence to, all second natures. But let no one suppose that the inexpressible can for this reason be named, or that the cause of all union is made double. For here we carry names over to it, looking to what comes after it, and to the processions from it, or the turnings back in a circle to it. Because multitude exists from it, we give it the name of the One; because all things, even those with the dimmest existence, turn back to it, we call it the Good.

@p
We try, then, to know the unknown nature of the first principle through the things that proceed from it and return to it; and through the same things we also try to name what is inexpressible. This principle, however, is neither known by beings nor expressible by any of all things. Set apart from all knowledge and all speech, and beyond grasp, it produces from itself by one cause all knowledge, everything known, all words, and whatever can be grasped by speech. Its unified nature, above all division, shines out to view in a twofold way in the natures after it, or rather in a threefold way. For all things remain in the One, proceed from it, and return to it. At one and the same time they are united to it, are set below its union, which is set apart from the whole of things, and desire to share in it. Union gives to all second natures a stable transcendence, which exists in a joining with their cause that does not proceed. Being set below defines the procession of beings and their separation from the unshared, first unity. And desire perfects the return of the natures that exist, and their circling towards the inexpressible. First natures, then, being always wholly united to the inexpressible, some more distantly and others more nearly, and receiving through this union their hyparxis and their share of good, we try to show by names the procession and return of the whole of things. But their stable holding in the first, if it is lawful to say so, and their union with the inexpressible, since this cannot be grasped or reached by knowledge, those wise in divine things could not point to in words. For as the inexpressible is first hidden in places no one can reach, and set apart from all beings, so too the union of all things with it is hidden, inexpressible and unknown to all beings. For every being is united to it neither by intellectual reaching nor by the activity of being, since things without knowledge are united to the first, and things deprived of all activity share, in their order, in a joining with it. What is unknown in beings by their union with the first, then, we neither try to know nor to show by names. Better able to look at their procession and return, we give the first two names, which we draw as likenesses from second natures. We also define two ways up to the first, joining the way through analogy with the name of the Good, and the way through negations with the name of the One. Plato points to this: in the Republic he calls the first the Good, and at the same time makes a return to it through analogy; in the Parmenides, setting it up as the One itself, he unfolds its transcendence, set apart from beings, through negative conclusions. By both ways, then, the first principle goes beyond the powers of knowing and the parts of speech, while all other things give us the cause of knowledge and of naming. The first principle gives existence in a unified way to all the unions and hyparxes of second natures, while the things after this cause share in it in a divided way. These, as we said before, are made many by remaining, proceeding and returning; but the One is at once wholly set apart from all fertile processions, all powers of return, and all one-like realities in beings. What, then, are the ways of the teaching about the first, through what names Plato tries to point to it, and from where the names and ways of this pointing, which is unknown to all things, are drawn, is, I think, clear enough from what has been said.
!flag neither by intellectual injection, [or projection] | Taylor's "injection" and "projection" render epibolē, the mind's reaching out to its object. Things are united to the One not by any act of knowing.

@ch II.7 | The Republic: the Good and the sun
In the sixth book of the Republic Plato sets a monad over intelligibles and another over perceptibles, the Good and the sun, its offspring, and hangs the second from the first. So in every order of gods there is a monad answering to the first. The Good in the Forms is not the first Good, which is beyond being and knowledge; the denials of the Republic sum up those of the Parmenides. The first is the cause of all, not by motion, multiplying, power or activity, but by being the Good itself, the end of all ends.

@p
If we must look at each of the teachings about the first principle scattered in Plato's writings, and bring them together into one science of theology, let us consider first, if you are willing, what Socrates shows in the sixth book of the Republic by the way already mentioned, and how through analogy he teaches us the wonderful transcendence of the Good over all beings and the summits of the whole of things. First, he distinguishes beings from each other: he sets some down as intelligible and others as perceptible, and defines science by knowledge of beings. He joins sense with perceptible things; and, dividing all things in two, he sets one set-apart monad over intelligible multitude, and a second over perceptible multitude, by likeness to the first. Of these monads, he shows that one generates intelligible light and the other perceptible light. And he shows that by intelligible light all intelligibles are god-like and good-like, by sharing in the first God; and by perceptible light, through the perfection that comes from the sun, all perceptible natures are sun-like, and like their one monad. Besides this, he hangs the second monad from the one that reigns in the intelligible. And so he stretches all things, the first and the last of beings, intelligibles and perceptibles, towards the Good. This way of leading back to the first seems to me the best, and most suited to theology: to gather all the gods in the world into one union and hang them from their nearest monad; to refer the gods above the world to the intellectual kingdom; to hang the intellectual gods from intelligible union; and to refer the intelligible gods themselves, and all beings through them, to what is first. For as the monad of the natures in the world is above the world, as the monad of the natures above the world is intellectual, and that of intellectual natures intelligible, so first intelligibles must hang from the monad above intelligibles, be perfected by it, and, filled with godhead, light up second natures with intelligible light. The intellectual natures, which enjoy their being from intelligibles, but their good and one-like hyparxis from the first cause, must hold together the natures above the world by intellectual light. And the kinds of gods before the world, receiving a pure intellect from the intellectual gods, intelligible light from the intelligible gods, and a unified light from the father of the whole of things, must send into this visible world the light they have. For this reason the sun, the summit of the natures in the world, proceeding from the depths of the aether, gives visible things a perfection beyond nature, and makes them as like as possible to the worlds above the heavens. These things, though, we will discuss more fully later.
!note Republic VI 507b-509b: the many beautiful things and the Beautiful itself; sight, light and the sun, "the offspring of the Good, which the Good begot in proportion to itself" (508b-c).

@p
The present account, then, hangs all things from the Good, the first unity, in the way described. For if the sun holds together everything perceptible, and the Good produces and perfects everything intelligible, and of these the second monad (the sun) is called the offspring of the Good, and so makes the perceptible bright, adorns it and fills it with good because it imitates the first-born cause of itself, then all things will share in the Good in this way, and will reach towards this one principle: intelligibles, and the most divine of beings, with nothing between, and perceptible things through their monad, the sun.
!flag imitates the primogenial cause of itself | Taylor's "primogenial" means first-born, original; Proclus means that the sun imitates the Good, its own cause.

@p
Again, and in another way, Plato tells us in this passage of the Republic the analysis that leads up to the first principle. He hangs all the multitudes in the world from the intelligible monads: all beautiful things, for instance, from the Beautiful itself, all good things from the Good, and all equal things from the Equal itself. Again, he takes some things as intelligible and others as perceptible, but their summits are set in a one-like way in the intelligibles. Again, from these intelligible Forms he sees fit to go up still higher; and, revering more the goodness beyond intelligibles, he takes it that all intelligibles, and the monads they hold, exist and are perfected through it. For as we refer perceptible multitude to a monad not ranked with perceptible things, and think that through this monad the multitude of perceptible things has its existence, so we must refer intelligible multitude to another cause, not counted among intelligibles, from which they have their being and their divine hyparxis.

@p
But let no one imagine that Plato grants the Good the same rank in the intelligible Forms as before intelligibles. The good that is ranked with the beautiful must be taken as a good of being, one of the Forms among intelligibles. The first Good, which, by joining the article to the noun, we usually call "the Good", is taken to be beyond being, and better than all beings in dignity and power. For Socrates too, discussing the beautiful and the good, calls one the Beautiful itself and the other the Good itself, and so, he says, we name all the things we then rightly took as many. Again, taking each as being one, we name each thing what it is. So Socrates leads us from perceptible things that are beautiful and good, and in short from things that are shared, exist in other things, and are made many, to the henads of intelligibles, beyond being, and the first beings; and from these again he carries us over to the set-apart cause of everything beautiful and good. For in the Forms the Beautiful itself leads many beautiful things, and the Good itself many goods, and each Form gives existence only to things like itself. But the first Good is the cause not only of what is good, but of beautiful things as well, as Plato says elsewhere: "all things are for its sake, and it is the cause of everything beautiful".
!flag to the superessential unities of intelligibles and the first essences | Proclus seems to mean the henads at the summit of the intelligible order, beyond being, and the first beings that hang from them.
!note Republic VI 507b: "we speak of many beautiful things and many good things ... and again of the Beautiful itself and the Good itself". The Second Letter 312e, quoted here, is discussed in chapters 8-9.

@p
Further, the good that is in the Forms is intelligible and known, as Socrates himself teaches; but the Good before the Forms is beyond beings and set above all knowledge. The first is the source of the perfection of being; the second gives good to the gods as gods, and generates goods that come before beings. So we must not take it that, when Socrates calls the first principle the Good from the name of a Form, he means outright the intelligible goodness. Though the first principle is above all speech and naming, we allow Socrates to call it the cause of everything beautiful and good, carrying names over to it through the things it fills most nearly. For I think this is what Socrates points to in all he says about the Good: that it is beyond knowledge and things known, and beyond being and what is, by its analogy to the sun. And in a wonderful way he gives us a short form of the negations of the One in the Parmenides. For saying that the Good is neither truth, nor being, nor intellect, nor science, at once separates it from the henads beyond being and every kind of gods, from the intellectual and intelligible orders, and from every existence of soul. These are the first things, and through the first hypothesis of the Parmenides these are taken away from the principle of the whole of things.
!flag from the name of idea | That is, from the name of a Form (idea): the Good is named after the Form of the good, though it is beyond the Forms.
!note Republic VI 508e-509a: the Good is the cause of knowledge and truth, yet "more beautiful than these"; neither science nor truth is the Good.

@p
Moreover, when he praises the Good, the leader of the divine orders, as the brightest of being, he does not call it brightest as sharing in light. For the first light proceeds from it to intelligibles and intellect. He gives it this name as the cause of the light spread everywhere, and as the source of every intelligible, intellectual or cosmic godhead. For this light is nothing else than sharing in a divine hyparxis. As all things become good-like by sharing in the Good, and are filled with the light that comes from there, so the natures that are first beings are god-like, and, as is said, intelligible and intellectual beings become divine by sharing in godhead. Looking, then, to all that has been said, we will keep the set-apart transcendence of the Good over all beings and the divine orders. But again, in each order of beings we must grant that there is a monad answering to it: not only among perceptible things, as Plato says the sun is, but among the natures above the world, and in the kinds of gods ranked from the Good before these. For clearly the natures nearer to the first cause, which share in it more, are more like it. And as it is the cause of all beings, so these set up monads that lead more partial orders. Plato ranks the multitudes under the monads, but stretches all the monads towards the set-apart principle of the whole of things, and sets them in a one-like way about it. So the science of theology must be unfolded in keeping with the divine orders, and our ideas about the first must be transcendent, unmixed and unconnected with other things. We should see all second natures as existing by it and perfected about it; but we should set it above all the monads in beings, by one excess of simplicity, and as ranked in a unified way before the whole orders of gods. For as the gods themselves enact the order among them, so the truth about them, the prior causes of beings, and the second and third offspring of these, must be clearly distinguished.
!flag as the most splendid of being | Republic VI 518c: the Good is "the brightest of being" (to phanotaton tou ontos), which the soul must learn to bear to look at.

@p
This, then, is the one truth about the first principle, with one reasoning that fits Plato's hypothesis remarkably well: that this principle exists before the whole orders of gods, gives existence to the good-like being of the gods, is the source of goodness beyond being, and that all things after it, reaching towards it, are filled with good, are united to it in an inexpressible way, and exist in a one-like way about it. For its unified nature is not barren; it generates other things all the more as it sets up beforehand a union set apart from the things that exist. Nor does its fertility tend to multitude and division; it remains with undefiled purity, hidden in places no one can reach. For in the natures after it too, we see everywhere that what is perfect desires to generate, and what is full hastens to give its fullness to other things. Much more, then, must the nature that holds all perfections in one, and is not a certain good but the Good itself and more than full (if it is lawful to say so), generate the whole of things and give them existence: producing all things by being set apart from all things, and by being unshared, generating alike the first and the last of beings.

@p
You must not suppose, however, that this generation and procession is sent out by the Good being moved, or multiplied, or having a power of generating, or acting; for all these come after the singleness of the first. For if the Good were moved, it would not be the Good, since the Good itself, which is nothing else, would leave goodness if it were moved. How, then, could what is the source of goodness to beings produce other things when deprived of good? Or if the Good is multiplied through weakness, there will be a procession of the whole of things through a lessening, not an overflowing, of goodness. For what, in generating, leaves its own transcendence hastens to adorn lower natures not through fertile perfection, but through a lessening and lack of its own power. And if the Good produces all things by using power, there will be a lessening of goodness about it. For it will be two things and not one: good and power. And if it lacks power, what is first good will be in need. But if to be the Good itself is enough for the perfection of the things produced and the fullness of all things, why do we take on power in addition? For additions, among the gods, are takings away from transcendent unions. So let the Good be alone, before power and before activity. For all activity is the offspring of power. The Good, then, neither gives existence to all things by acting through activity, nor fills all things with powers by lacking power, nor do all things share in good by its being multiplied, nor do all beings enjoy the first principle by its being moved. For the Good comes before all powers and all activities, every multitude and motion, since each of these is referred to the Good as to its end. The Good, then, is the final end of all ends, and the centre of all desirable natures. All desirable natures give an end to second beings; but what exists before all things, not bounded by any, is the first Good.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects "all things present" to "all beings" in the Greek he translated.

@ch II.8 | The Second Letter: the king of all
In the letter to Dionysius Plato speaks in riddles of three kings. Some object that this ranks the first God with the second and third. But Plato calls only the first the king, and says that all things, not only first things, are about him; so he leaves him unranked with the others. The letter also shows that the first cannot be spoken of: whatever we say of it, we speak about it, not it. Everything, going into the inexpressible in itself, finds there a token of the father of all.

@p
After this, let us turn our attention to the ideas about the first principle in Plato's letter to Dionysius, and look at the way he considers its inexpressible and boundless transcendence. But perhaps someone will be angry with us for rashly drawing Plato's words to our own hypotheses, and will say that the three kings he speaks of are all intellectual gods, and that Plato does not see fit to rank or count the Good with second natures. For such counting together should not be thought fitting to the set-apart transcendence of the Good over other things; nor, in short, must we say that the Good joins, as first, with some second or third cause, to complete a triad together with other natures. Rather, it goes before every triad and every number far more than the intelligible gods go before the intellectual. How, then, can we count the Good, which is set apart at once from all the divine numbers, with the other kings, and rank one as first, another as second, and another as third? Someone may also bring many other things showing the transcendence of the first principle over everything divine. But such a person, in interpreting Plato's words this way, will agree remarkably well with us, who say that the Good is unshared, above all the intelligible and intellectual kinds, and set above all the divine monads.
!note The Second Letter 312e: "All things are about the king of all, and all are for his sake, and he is the cause of all beautiful things; second things are about the second, and third things about the third." Proclus' commentators often doubted the letter; he takes it as Plato's.

@p
That Plato grants the first God to be the king of all things, and says that all things are for his sake and that he is the cause of all beautiful things, does not, I think, need much proof for those who consider his words by themselves, apart from their own guesses, by bringing in which they try by force to agree with Plato. But that we do not say these things counting the first God with second natures Plato himself makes plain. He does not call the first king the first, but only the king of all things; nor does he say that some things are about him, as he says that second things are about the second and third things about the third; he says, in short, that all things are about him. To the other kings he gives number and a divided kingdom; but to the king of all he gives neither a place in a number nor a share of rule set against that of the others. This way of speaking, then, neither counts the king of all with the other kings, nor ranks him as the leader of a triad with the second and third power. For in a threefold division the first monad leads the first orders, those ranked with it; the second the second, and the third the third. If, however, someone takes it that the first monad leads all things, so as to hold at once the second and third lots, still the cause that exists by holding things is different from the cause that reaches equally to all things. To the king of all, all things are subject by one reason and one order; but to the first of the triad, first things are subject by the same order, and second and third things must serve by their sharing with the remaining kings. Does not what Plato says here, then, remarkably praise the set-apart nature of the first cause, and his being unranked with the other kingdoms of the gods? For he says that this cause reigns equally over all things, that all things exist about him, and that being and activity are in all things for his sake.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects "and" to "but" in the Greek he translated.

@p
And if Socrates in the Republic clearly teaches that the sun reigns over the world by analogy with the Good, let no one dare to accuse this analogy of counting the Good together with the king of the natures in the world. For unless, together with the likeness of second causes to first principles, we see fit to keep that set-apart rule of the first cause, we will be unable to show that the kings above the world have their lot by analogy with the first cause, who exists before the whole of things by one transcendence. But why go on at length? Plato calls the first God king, but does not see fit to give the others the same name, not only at the beginning of what he says about the first, but shortly after, where he adds: "About the king, and the things I have spoken of, there is nothing of this kind." The first God alone, then, is called king. And he is called the king not only of first things, as the second is of second things and the third of third, but as the cause at once of all being and all beauty. So the first God goes before the other causes in a set-apart and one-like way, by a transcendence over the whole of things, and Plato neither praises him as ranked with them nor as the leader of a triad.
!note The Second Letter 313a: "About the king and the things I spoke of, there is nothing of the kind" (nothing like the qualities the soul looks for). Taylor's two footnotes correct the Greek he translated.

@p
That these things are said by Plato about the first God we will learn by going back a little to the words just before, which are these: "You say that I have not sufficiently shown you the things about the first nature. I must speak to you in riddles, so that if the letter comes to harm, on land or sea, whoever reads it may not understand. All things are about the king of all, and all are for his sake, and he is the cause of all beautiful things." In these words, then, Plato, setting out to purify our ideas about the first principle through riddles, praises the king of all things, and refers to him the cause of the whole of beautiful and good things. Who, then, is the king of all things, if not the unified God, set apart from all things, who produces all things from himself, and leads all orders by one cause? Who turns all ends back to himself and sets them about himself? For if you call him, for whose sake all things are, the end of all ends and the first-born cause, you will not stray from the truth about him. Who is the cause of all beautiful things, shining on them with divine light, and shutting up what is ugly and without measure, and the dimmest of all things, at the furthest edge of the universe?
!note The Second Letter 312d-e.

@p
If you are willing, we will also show from the words of Plato that follow these that it is not fitting for the first principle to receive either speech or knowledge. For the words, "This question of yours about the cause of all beautiful things is a question about something with a certain quality", are to be referred to this principle. For it cannot be grasped by intellect, because it is unknown, nor unfolded, because it is unbounded; whatever you say of it, you will speak of it as of something; you will speak about it, but you will not speak it. For speaking of the things of which it is the cause, we cannot say, or grasp by thinking, what it is. Here, then, the adding of quality and the busy activity of the soul take it away from the goodness set apart from all things, by the overflow of the soul's ideas about it. This also draws the soul down to akin, inborn and many-formed intelligibles, and keeps it from receiving what is marked by unity and hidden in sharing in the Good. And it is not only fitting that the human soul should be cleansed of the things ranked with it, for union and sharing with what is first, and for this should leave all its own multitude behind, and, rousing its own hyparxis, come near with closed eyes, as is said, to the king of all things, and share in his light as far as is lawful for it. Intellect too, which is before us, and all divine natures, by their highest unions, their torches beyond being and their first hyparxes, are united to what is first, and always share in its overflowing fullness; and this not as they are what they are, but as they are set apart from what is akin to them and converge on the One, the principle of all. For the cause of all has sown in all things tokens of his own all-perfect transcendence, and through these sets all things about himself, and, set apart from the whole of things, is present to all things in an inexpressible way. So everything, going into the inexpressible of its own nature, finds there the symbol of the father of all. All things too by nature revere him and are united to him through a fitting mystic token, stripping off their own nature and hastening to become his token alone, and to share in him alone, through desire for his unknown nature and for the source of good. So when they have run up as far as this cause, they become still, and are freed from the birth pangs and the desire for goodness, unknown, inexpressible, unshared and more than full, that all things have by nature. That what is said here is about the first God, and that Plato in these ideas leaves him unranked with the other causes and set apart from them, has, I think, been shown well enough.
!flag but you will not speak it | Proclus' distinction: we can speak about the first, from what comes after it, but never say what it is.
!flag superessential torches | Taylor's "torches" are the lights or flames of the gods (a Chaldean image): their summits, beyond being, by which they touch the first.
!note The Second Letter 313a: "the soul asks what kind of thing it is, looking at things akin to itself, none of which is adequate". "With closed eyes": compare Plotinus I.6.8, "close your eyes and wake another way of seeing".

@ch II.9 | The letter's three sayings: about him, for his sake, cause of the beautiful
Of the letter's three sayings, the first, "all things are about him", says nothing of the first itself, only of what comes after; the second makes him the end, for whose sake all things are; the third, cause of all beautiful things, adds a lower kind of cause. "About which", "for the sake of which" and "from which" belong first to the three orders of the intelligible gods, and are carried up from them to the first.

@p
Let us next consider each of these teachings, and fit them to our ideas about cause, so that from them we may grasp by reasoning the aim of the whole of Plato's theology. Let one truth about the first principle, then, be most of all the one that praises his inexpressible, simple and all-transcending nature: which sets all things about him, but does not say that he generates or produces anything, or that he exists before as the end of what comes after him. For such a way of speaking neither adds anything to the unknown one, set apart from all things, nor makes many him who is set above all union, nor attributes the relation and sharing of second things to him who is wholly unshared. Nor, in short, does it claim to teach anything about him or his nature, but only about the second and third natures that exist after him.

@p
This, then, being this way of pointing to the first God, and such the way it reveres the inexpressible, the second after it is the one that turns all the desires of things to him, and praises him as the object of desire and the common end of all things, by one cause that comes before all other causes. For the last of things exists only for the sake of something else, but the first is only that for the sake of which all other things exist; and all the middle natures share in both these characters. So they cling truly to the natures above them, as objects of desire, but give the perfection of desire to lower beings.

@p
The third view of the principle of things is far below the ones before, taking him as giving existence to all beautiful things. To praise him as the giver of good, and as the end coming before the two orders of things, is not very far from the account that says that all causes come after him and have their existence from him, both those that are fathers and sources of good and those that give fertile powers. But to give him a producing and generating causality is still further from the all-perfect union of the first. For since it cannot be known or spoken of by second natures, we must not say that it is the cause, or that it generates beings, but should praise in silence this inexpressible nature, this wholly causeless cause before all causes. If, though, as we try to give it the name of the Good and the One, we likewise give it causality, and the character of end or of father, we must forgive the soul's labour about this inexpressible principle, as it longs to see him with the eye of intellect and to speak about him; but at the same time the set-apart, boundless transcendence of the One must be taken as going beyond this kind of pointing. From these, then, we can receive Plato's sacred ideas, and an order suited to the things themselves. We may say that the first part of this sentence points well enough to the simplicity, transcendence, and in short the unrankedness with all things, of the king of all. For saying that all things exist about him unfolds the hyparxis of second things, but leaves what is beyond all things without any connection to what comes after it. The second part praises the cause of all the gods as set beforehand in the rank of the end. For the highest of all causes is joined at once to what comes before cause; and of this kind is the final cause, that for the sake of which all things exist. This part, then, comes after the other, and is woven together with the order of things and the procession of Plato's teaching.
!note Taylor's three footnotes correct the Greek he translated ("order" for "action", and "of the gods" for "of the god").

@p
Again, the third part says that he produces all beautiful things, and so adds to him a kind of cause lower than the final. This, I think, is why Plotinus does not hesitate to call the first God the source of the beautiful. We must give what is best to the best of all things, so that he may be the cause of all, and really before cause. And this is the Good. This too, which is remarkable, can be seen in Plato's words: the first of these three divine teachings neither dares to say anything about the Good, this inexpressible nature, nor lets us refer any kind of cause to it. The second leaves the Good inexpressible, as it should, but from the relation of what comes after it lets us gather the final cause: it does not refuse to call it that for the sake of which all things are. But when it says that all things are for the sake of the Good, it rouses in us the idea of sharing and ranking between the object of desire and the natures that desire it. And the third shows that the Good is the cause of all beautiful things. But this is to say something about it, to add to the simplicity of the first cause, and, not remaining in the idea of the end, to join to it the producing principle of second things. It seems to me that Plato here points to the natures that come into light next after the first. For it is not possible to say anything about it except as moved to this at one time from all things, and at another from the best of things; for it is the cause of hyparxis to all things, and unfolds its own separate union through their distinctive characters. We give it the name of the One and the Good, then, from the gift that reaches all things from it. For of what all things share in, we say there is no other cause than what is set before all of them. But the "about which", the "for the sake of which" and the "from which" belong first to the intelligible gods, and from them are given to the first God. For from where can we suppose the unified gods get their distinctive characters, if not from what is before them? So "about" fits this summit of intelligibles, because all the divine orders proceed in a hidden way about this summit, ranked before them. "For the sake of which" belongs to the middle order of intelligibles, for all things exist for the sake of eternity and a wholly entire hyparxis. And "from which" fits the furthest edge of intelligibles, for this first produces all things and adorns them in a one-like way. These things we will make better known in the teaching about the intelligible gods, which will follow shortly.
!flag the fountain of the beautiful | Plotinus (VI.7.32-33, I.6.9) calls the Good "the source and principle of beauty", beyond beauty itself.
!note The three intelligible orders (being, eternity or life, and the intelligible living being, Timaeus' "pattern") are the subject of Book III. Taylor's footnotes correct two words of the Greek he translated ("of cause"; "for the sake of which" for "through which", the final, not the instrumental, cause).

@ch II.10 | The first hypothesis of the Parmenides: why it denies all things of the One
