The first hypothesis denies of the One as many things as the second affirms, since the second unfolds all the orders of gods from the One, and the first shows the One set apart from them all. The denials follow the order of the divine monads, beginning where multitude first appears. They are not privations but the generators of their opposites. Yet in the end the One is set apart even from the denials, and speech about it overturns itself.

@p
Next, let us finish the discussion of the first God with Parmenides' study, and unfold the mystic ideas of the first hypothesis as far as our present purpose requires; for the most complete interpretation of them we refer the reader to our commentary on that dialogue. First, we must settle this about the first hypothesis: that it holds as many conclusions in denial as the hypothesis after it holds in affirmation. The second shows all the orders proceeding from the One; the first shows that the One is set apart from all the divine kinds. From both, it is plain to everyone that the cause of the whole of things must go beyond what it produces. For because the One is the cause of all the gods, it goes beyond all things. And because it is set apart from them by transcendence, it gives all things their realities. For through being spread out above all things, it makes all things exist. In the second and third orders of beings too, causes wholly set apart from their effects generate and hold together their offspring more perfectly than causes ranked with their effects. And the One, by producing all the divine orders in an inexpressible way, appears set above all in a unified way. For in the productions after it, the cause is everywhere different from what it causes. So nature, being bodiless, is a cause that goes beyond bodies; soul, being wholly everlasting, is the cause of things that come to be; and intellect, being unmoved, is the cause of everything moved. If, then, in each procession of beings the effects are denied of their causes, we must surely take all things away alike from the cause of all.
!note Proclus' Commentary on the Parmenides, of which the books on the first hypothesis survive (Book VII in the Latin of William of Moerbeke).

@p
Second, I think the order of the negations must be defined by those who receive theology in keeping with Parmenides' aim. We must grant that they proceed from the monads that exist first in the divine kinds, and that Parmenides takes away from the One all second and third natures in the order fitting each. For what goes beyond more ruling causes must far more come before lower ones. But Parmenides does not begin his negations from the gods united to the first. For this kind is hard to distinguish from the One, since, ranked by nature right after it, it is most unified and hidden, and surpassingly like its producing cause. So Parmenides, beginning where division and multitude first appear, before all other things, and going in order through all the second orders as far as the last of things, comes back again to the beginning, and shows how the One differs from the gods most like it, which first share in it, by one inexpressible cause.
!flag does not begin his negations from the Gods that are united to the first | That is, the first denial ("the One is not many", 137c) answers not to the highest gods (the intelligible gods, united to the One) but to the order where multitude first appears; the denials of being at the end (141e) return to the highest.

@p
Third, besides what has been said, I settle this about the manner of the negations: they do not deprive their subjects, but generate what are, as it were, their opposites. For because the first principle is not many, the many proceed from it; because it is not a whole, wholeness proceeds from it; and so with the rest. In settling this I speak in keeping with Plato, who sees fit to remain in negations and add nothing to the One. For whatever you add, you lessen the One, and then show that it is not the One, but what is acted on by the One, or shares in it. For it is then not one only, but besides this has something else by sharing. This way of negation, then, is set apart, unified and first, a going out from the whole of things into an unknown and inexpressible transcendence of simplicity. And having given such a way to the first God, we must again set him apart from the negations too. For no speech and no name belongs to the One, says Parmenides. But if no speech belongs to it, clearly no negation does either. For all things come after the One: things knowable, knowledge, and the instruments of knowledge; and in a way the impossible shows itself at the end of the hypothesis. For if nothing at all can be said of the One, this discussion itself does not fit the One either. Nor is it at all surprising that the speech of those who want to know the inexpressible by words should end in the impossible, since all knowledge joined to an object that does not belong to it loses its power. Sense, if we said it belonged to what science knows, would overturn itself; and so would science and every kind of knowledge if we said they belonged to the intelligible. So speech, when concerned with the inexpressible, overturned about itself, has no rest, and opposes itself.
!note The Parmenides 142a: "there is no name of it, nor speech, nor any science, perception or opinion"; and Parmenides concludes, "Can these things be so about the One? I think not."

@ch II.11 | How to approach the One: the stillness of the soul
Before the denials, Proclus calls the soul to put away all multitude and become still, to adore the first as the rising sun with half-closed eyes, and to praise him not as maker of earth and heaven, but as the God of all gods, the henad of all henads, more inexpressible than all silence. Then, coming down into dialectic, we can see how the first is set apart from all things.

@p
Now, then, if ever, let us put away knowledge of many forms, root out of ourselves all the variety of life, and in perfect stillness come near to the cause of all things. For this, let not only opinion and imagination be at rest, nor only the passions that hinder our rising up to the first be at peace; but let the air be still, and the universe itself be still. Let all things carry us with a calm power to sharing with the inexpressible. And standing there, having gone beyond the intelligible (if we hold anything of this kind), with eyes almost closed, as if worshipping the rising sun, since it is not lawful for any being at all to look at him steadily, let us look at the sun from which the light of the intelligible gods proceeds, rising, as the poets say, from the bosom of the ocean. Then, coming down from this divine stillness into intellect, and from intellect using the reasonings of the soul, let us tell ourselves what the natures are from which, in this procession, we will see the first God set apart. Let us praise him, as it were, not as founding the earth and the heavens, nor as giving existence to souls and to the generations of all living things; for these he produced too, but among the last of things. Before these, let us praise him as bringing into light the whole intelligible and intellectual kind of gods, together with all the gods above the world and in the world: as the God of all gods, the henad of all henads, beyond the first inner sanctuaries, more inexpressible than all silence and more unknown than all being, holy among the holy, and hidden in the intelligible gods. And again after these things, coming down into reasoning from an intellectual hymn, and using the blameless science of dialectic, let us, following the contemplation of first causes, see how the first God is set apart from the whole of things. And let our descent go as far as this. For opinion, imagination and sense keep us from sharing in the presence of the gods, draw us down from Olympian goods to motions born of earth, divide the intellect in us in the manner of the Titans, and tear us away from being settled among wholes to the images of beings.
!flag beyond the first adyta | The adyta are the innermost shrines of a temple, which only priests may enter; Proclus sets the One beyond even the first, the intelligible gods.
!flag Titannically divide the intellect that is in us | In the Orphic myth the Titans tore the child Dionysus to pieces; the Neoplatonists read this as the dividing of the one intellect into many, and of the soul into the body.
!note "Let the air be still, and the universe itself be still": an echo of Plotinus V.1.2, where the soul is to see the world-soul flowing in "while the earth is still, and the sea and the air, and the heaven itself". The sun "rising from the ocean": Iliad VII.421-422.

@ch II.12 | A hymn of denials: the One set apart from every order of gods
Following the Parmenides' first hypothesis, Proclus sets the One apart from each order of gods in turn: not many (the summit of the intellectual-intelligible gods), not a whole with parts, without beginning, middle, end or shape; neither in itself nor in another; neither at rest nor moved; neither same nor different; neither like nor unlike; neither equal nor unequal; neither older nor younger. Then he returns to the beginning: the One is not even being, nor one by sharing. All knowledge falls short, and ends in union with the unknown.

@p
What, then, will be the first idea of the science that proceeds from intellect and unfolds itself into light? What else can we say it is than the simplest and best known of all the ideas this science holds? What is this? "The One," says Parmenides, "if it is one, will not be many." For the many must share in the One; but the One does not share in the one, but is the One itself. Nor is what is first one shared. For it would not be purely one if mixed with the many, nor would it be what is one, if it took on the addition of something lower. So the One is set apart from the many. The many first exist in the summit of the first intellectual gods, and in the intelligible place of vision, as we are taught in the second hypothesis. So the One wholly goes beyond an order of this kind, and is its cause. For the not-many is not a privation, as we have said, but the cause of the many. Parmenides does not think he needs to prove this; as something most plain to everyone, he shows it first, through the opposition, as it were, of the many to the One. Using this, he takes away what follows; and he takes away what comes after this using the conclusions before it, and he always does this in the same way. At one time he takes the elements of his proofs from the nearest conclusions, at another from those further off. For after this intelligible order of gods, as we have said, he gives existence, from their set-apart cause, to the order that holds together and bounds their extent. This order he calls, in the second hypothesis, parts and a whole. These he denies of the One, using the many to distinguish the subjects from the One. For, as he says, what is a whole and has parts is many; but the One is beyond the many. If, then, the One goes beyond the intelligible simplicity, but the whole and what has parts proceed from it, to become the bond of the whole of this division, must not the One be neither a whole nor in need of parts? And I think it is through this transcendence that the One exists before as the cause of this order of gods, and produces this order, but in a set-apart way.
!flag in the summit of the first intellectual Gods, and in the intelligible place of survey | Proclus' "intellectual gods" here are the intelligible and at the same time intellectual gods, the middle rank between the intelligible and the intellectual (Book IV); "the place of vision" is the Phaedrus' plain above the heavens, which he puts at their summit.
!note The Parmenides 137c-d: "If the One is, it will not be many ... so it will neither be a whole nor have parts."

@p
Third after these, we can look at the order that has the boundary of the gods at once intelligible and intellectual, proceeding from the One, and see the One wholly spread out above it. This order exists from the second kinds, and from the intellectual wholeness of the kinds. But the One, as has been shown, is set apart from this wholeness as its cause. So the One has neither beginning, middle nor end, nor has it extremes, nor does it share in any shape. For through these gods the order of gods just mentioned comes to light. Whether, then, there is a perfecting summit in these gods, or what is praised as the middle centre, or an ending that turns the end of these gods back to their beginning, the One is equally beyond every threefold division. For the One would have parts, and would be many, if it shared in things of this kind. But it has been shown that the One exists in a unified way before the many, and before wholeness with its parts, as their cause. And you see how Parmenides shows us one negation of the highest order, two of the middle, and three of the last. Besides this, he shows that the One has no limit; but the unlimited is a thing of this kind. And separately from this he shows that the One does not receive any shape.
!note The Parmenides 137d-138a: the One has no beginning, middle or end, is unlimited, and has no shape, neither straight nor round.

@p
Again, after these threefold orders, we must turn our attention to the intellectual gods that exist from them and receive a threefold division, and show that the One goes beyond these too. For such is the One, says Parmenides, since it is neither in itself nor in another. If it were in another, it would be held all round by what it is in, and would touch everywhere what holds it. But then it would have a shape, would be made of parts, and so would be many and not one. And if it were in itself, it would hold itself wholly in itself. But holding and at the same time being held, it will be two, and no longer first one. The argument, then, comes to the same conclusion, and shows that the One will not be one, through the summit of the intellectual order, if anyone tries to mix it with other things. So the One, wholly set apart from this summit too, gives it existence; this summit at one and the same time sharing in the third of the gods set above it, being produced from the second of these gods, and perfected from the first, and wholly set in it.
!note The Parmenides 138a-b: the One is neither in another, for it would be held round and touched at many points, nor in itself, for it would be two.

@p
Moreover, the One also generates the second intellectual order, unmixed with it. For the One neither stands still nor is moved. It shares in neither of these; but, equally set apart from both, it goes at the same time beyond the middle orders of the intellectual procession of the gods. If it were moved, it would be moved in one of two ways, by change of quality or by motion in place. But the One cannot be changed in quality, for, so changed, it would not be one, and would fall away from a unified hyparxis. Nor can it be moved in place. It cannot be moved in a circle, because it would have parts, a middle and extremes. And if it exchanged one place for another, it would be divisible. For it would have to be neither wholly in the place it is moved to, nor in the place it starts from. For if it were wholly in either, it would be unmoved, partly not yet moved, partly having already stopped. But if the One stands still, it must surely remain in the same thing. And it has been shown that the One is nowhere; so it is neither in itself nor in another. In no way, then, is the One moved, nor does it stand still: things (motion and rest) that belong most of all to the middle order of the intellectual gods, as will be clear from the second hypothesis. For the first God produces this order too, being set apart from it.
!note The Parmenides 138b-139b: the One is neither altered nor carried round nor moved from place to place, nor does it stand still.

@p
Third, through what follows next, we can look at the last order of the intellectual gods, proceeding from the One and set below it. For in this order sameness and difference exist united. But at the same time the One exists before both. For the different is said to be different both from itself and from other things; and likewise the same is the same as itself and as other things. But the One is not different from itself, because what is different from the One will be not one. And it is not the same as other things, lest, becoming the same as them, it should pass unnoticed into their nature. Moreover, the One is not different from other things either. For it would at the same time be one and have, as an addition, the power of difference. For so far as it is different, it will not be one, since difference is not the One. So, being one and different, it will be many and not the One. Nor is the One the same as itself. For if the One and the same differed only in name, then the many, by sharing in sameness with each other, would not be many. For it is impossible that the many should become one by sharing in the many. But if the One and sameness are different in being, what is first one does not share in sameness, lest, taking on sameness in addition to the one, it should become a one that is acted on, and not what is first one. If, then, the furthest edge of the intellectual gods is marked by this tetrad, it is clear that the One, existing beyond this too, unfolds it into light from above, and sets over the wholes of the universe a fourfold monad, the source of adornment to all second natures. For from here other things first receive sharing with the One, being also produced and held together by the One.
!flag For if the one and the same differ only in name, the many will not be in consequence of participating of sameness with each other | The argument (Parmenides 139d-e): if being the same were being one, then whatever is the same as something would be one with it, and the many, being the same as each other, would be one. The modern English follows the sense.
!note The Parmenides 139b-e: the One is neither the same as nor different from itself or another.

@p
After the intellectual gods, the inexpressible transcendence of the One orders the extent of the gods above the world, while the One meanwhile is set apart, in a hidden way, from its offspring above the world. This extent exists next after the intellectual gods, but receives its hyparxis in a one-like way from the first God. Parmenides produces this through likeness and unlikeness, from the godhead that closes the boundary of the intellectual monads. For the like is what is acted on by sameness, as unlikeness is what is acted on by difference. So Parmenides shows that the One goes beyond such a character of the gods too, by one simplicity. For what is set above the power of the same and the different goes far more beyond the kinds whose existence is by likeness and unlikeness.
!note The Parmenides 139e-140b: the One is neither like nor unlike, since it has nothing the same and nothing different.

@p
What, then, remains after this? Is it not clear that it is the multitude of the gods in the world? But this too is twofold: one heavenly, the other below the moon. Of these, the kind that circles in the heavens proceeds together with the equal, the greater and the less. In the kind below the moon, the equal has a difference in multitude from the heavenly equality, and the unequal is again divided by the power of the more and the less. By another kind of the divine orders, then, there will be a monad and a dyad; but above they are akin to the One and to sameness, and below to multitude and the intellectual cause of difference. So the One goes beyond all these. For the equal everywhere is made of the same parts. By what means, then, could the nature that is at once set apart from sameness, and from the difference that goes with it, share in equality and inequality?
!note The Parmenides 140b-d: the One is neither equal nor unequal, to itself or to another, since it would have the same or different measures.

@p
Beyond all these divine orders, then, we must see with intellect the kinds of souls made divine and distributed about the gods. For in each of the divine processions, and in the processions of souls, the first kind appears born together with the gods, since both in the heavens and in the region below the moon divine souls receive the division of the gods into the world, as the Athenian Stranger shows somewhere. The extent of soul, then, is marked by time and a life in time. And the distinctive character of divine souls is shown by Parmenides to lie in their becoming at the same time younger and older than themselves and other things. For, circling always by the same time, and joining the beginning with the end, as at one and the same time they go on to the end of the whole period they become younger, and as they come round to its beginning they become older. Yet all their ages always keep the same measures of time. Again, there is sameness in them, and difference: the first keeps equality, the second inequality, in time. So the One exists before divine souls, and generates these too together with the gods. We now come to the end of the whole division of the higher natures; and the cause of all intelligibles at once brings into light the kinds that follow the gods, divided three ways by the three parts of time. But this cause is shown by Parmenides' intellectual reachings to be set apart from these too. For what is beyond all time, and the life in time, can by no means become subject to the more partial periods of time.
!flag younger and at the same time older both than themselves and other things | The Parmenides 140e-141d: the One is neither older nor younger, nor the same age, nor in time at all. Proclus reads these as the marks of divine souls, which live in time but in circles, always returning to their beginning.
!note The Laws X 898c-899b: the souls that drive the sun, moon and stars are gods, and divide the heaven among them.

@p
What is first of all things, then, brings into light all the gods, divine souls and the higher kinds, and is neither entangled with its offspring nor multiplied about them; wholly set apart from them in a wonderful simplicity and transcendence of union, it gives all things alike procession, and order in the procession. So Parmenides, beginning from the intelligible place of vision of the first intellectual gods, goes this far, by the measures of generation, giving existence to the kinds of gods and to the natures united to and following the gods, and always shows that the One is inexpressibly set apart from all things. But again from here he returns to the beginning, and, imitating the return of the whole of things, separates the One from the highest, the intelligible gods. For in this way most of all we can see the transcendence of the One, and the boundless difference of its union from all other things: if we show not only that it is set above the second or third processions in the divine orders, but that it exists before the intelligible henads themselves; and this in a way fitting the simplicity of their hidden nature, not through a variety of words but through the reaching of intellect alone. For intelligibles are by nature fit to be known by intellect. Parmenides shows this too in fact, leaving logical methods behind, and, acting by intellect, says that the One is above being and what is marked by the One. For this was not gathered from the conclusions before it. For the account of the first gods themselves would be without proof, if it drew its credit from lower things. At the same time, then, Parmenides contends that all knowledge, and all the instruments of knowledge, fall short of the transcendence of the One, and end beautifully in the inexpressible of that God who is beyond all things. For after acts of science and reachings of intellect, a union with the unknown follows. To this Parmenides refers his whole discussion, and so concludes the first hypothesis, hanging all the divine kinds from the One, but showing that the One is set apart from all things in a unified way, existing without sharing in intelligibles or perceptibles, and giving existence in an inexpressible way to the shared monads. So the One is also said to be beyond the one joined with being, and at the same time beyond every shared multitude of henads.
!flag asserting that the one is above essence, and being characterized by the one | The Parmenides 141e-142a: the One does not share in being, and so "is not even one"; Proclus reads this as setting the One above the one being, the summit of the intelligible gods. Taylor's footnote corrects the Greek he translated ("to the gods").

@book III | The henads, the two principles after the One, and the intelligible gods
Book III comes down from the One to the gods. Proclus proves that the One produces a multitude of henads, the gods, each shared by some order of being, the nearer henads by simpler beings: being, life, intellect, soul and body. After the One come two principles, limit and the unlimited, and from them the mixed, which is first being. Then he begins the orders of gods themselves with the intelligible gods, the three intelligible triads, found in the Philebus, the Timaeus, the Sophist and the Parmenides. Taylor's printed text heads only chapters 1-3 and 9 onward of chapters 1-9; the Workshop divides his paragraphs among the chapters his table of contents names, by their matter.

@ch III.1 | From the One to the gods
After the first God comes the multitude of gods, the most united to him. Before he sets out Plato's account of them, Proclus will prove that there are as many orders of gods as the second hypothesis of the Parmenides unfolds.

@p
Such, then, as it seems to me, is Plato's theology concerning the first God, and so great is the transcendence it has over all other discussions of divine things. At one and the same time it reverently keeps the inexpressible union of this God, set apart from the whole of things, beyond the grasp of all acts of knowing, and apart from all beings; and it unfolds the paths that lead up to him, brings to fulfilment the desire, always in labour, that souls have for the father and forefather of all things, and kindles in them that torch by which they are most of all joined to the unknown transcendence of the One. After this unshared and inexpressible cause, truly beyond being, separated from all being, power and activity, the discussion of the gods follows at once. For what else before the henads is it lawful to join to the One, or what can be more united to the unified God than the multitude of gods? Concerning these, then, we will next unfold Plato's account, which is not a matter of art, calling on the gods themselves to kindle in us the light of truth. Before going into the details of this account, though, I want to convince the reader, and make it plain to him by proof, that there are necessarily as many orders of gods as the Parmenides of Plato unfolds to us in the second hypothesis.
!flag the inartificial theory of Plato | Taylor's "inartificial" seems to mean unlaboured, natural (atechnos): Plato's account of the gods comes not from art or contrivance, but from the things themselves.

@ch III.2 | The henads come after the One: four axioms
Four axioms: every cause first produces what is like it; every monad produces a number of its own kind; the nearer a procession is to its cause, the more it produces; and unshared causes come before shared ones. From these it follows that the One, before all things, produces a number of self-perfect henads, most like itself.

@p
This, I think, is plain before all else to those whose ideas are not perverted: that everywhere, and especially in the divine orders, second processions are completed through their likeness to their own principles. For nature, intellect and every generating cause are by nature fit to produce and join to themselves like things before unlike things. For if the procession of beings must be continuous, with no void coming between, whether in bodiless natures or in bodies, then everything that proceeds by nature must proceed through likeness. For it is by no means lawful for what is caused to be the same as its cause, since it is a slackening and falling short of the union of the producing cause that generates second natures. Again, if the second were the same as the first, each would be the same alike, and one would not be cause and the other caused. But if one, by its very being, has an overflowing of productive power, and the other falls short of the power that produced it, they are separated from each other by nature, the generating cause goes before what is generated in excellence, and there is no sameness between things that differ so greatly. But if the second is not the same as the first, and is only different, they will not be joined to each other, nor will one share in the other. For contact and sharing are a communion of things joined, and a sympathy of the sharers with the natures they share in. But if it is at once the same as and different from the first, and the sameness is in need and overcome by the contrary power, the One will no longer lead the procession of beings, nor will every generating cause exist before second things in the rank of the good. For the One is the cause not of division but of friendship. And the Good turns what is generated back to its causes. But the return and friendship of second things to first is through likeness, not through an unlike nature. If, then, the One is the cause of the whole of things, and the Good is desired by all things in a set-apart way, it will everywhere give existence to the offspring of prior causes through likeness, so that procession may be by the One, and the return of what has proceeded may be to the Good. For without likeness there will be neither the return of things to their own principles nor the generation of effects. Let this, then, be taken as granted here.
!note Elements of Theology 28-29: every producing cause brings into existence things like itself before unlike things, and all procession is through likeness.

@p
The second point, after this, which is shown through it, is that every monad must produce a number ranked with itself: nature a natural number, soul a soul's number, and intellect an intellectual number. For if everything generating generates like things before unlike ones, as has been shown, every cause will surely hand on its own form and character to its own offspring, and before it gives existence to far-off processions, separated from its nature, it will produce things near it in being and joined to it through likeness. Every monad, then, gives existence to a multitude, as generating what is second to itself, which divides the powers that exist beforehand in it in a hidden way. For what is in the monad one-like and drawn together appears separately in the monad's offspring. The wholeness of nature shows this: it holds in one the forming principles of all things, in the heavens and below the moon, but distributes its powers to the natures divided from it about bodies. For the nature of earth, of fire and of the moon has its character and form from the wholeness of nature, acts together with this wholeness, and holds its own lot. The monad of the mathematical sciences and of numbers shows this too. For it is all things first, and produces in itself the forms of numbers like seeds, and distributes different powers to different numbers that proceed outward. For what is generated cannot receive all at once the whole abundance of what generates it; and the fertile power of everything that exists beforehand in the cause itself must come to light. The monad, then, gives existence to a multitude about itself, and to a number that distributes the characters that remain gathered in itself. But since, as was said before, the like is always more akin to the cause than the unlike, there will be one multitude like the monad, proceeding from the monad, and another of unlike things. And again, the multitude like the monad is, in a divided way, what the monad is undividedly. For if the monad has a distinctive power and hyparxis, there will be the same form of hyparxis in the multitude, with a slackening with respect to the whole.
!flag it contains in one the reasons, [i.e. productive principles] of all things | Taylor's "reasons" and "productive principles" are logoi, the forming principles that nature holds and spreads into bodies.
!note Elements of Theology 21: every order begins from a monad and proceeds into a multitude ranked with the monad. Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
After this, third, we must consider that among processions, those nearer their cause point to a greater multitude of things, and are in a way equal to the causes that hold them; while those further off have a less extended power of meaning, and, through the lessening of their power, change and lessen at the same time the breadth of their production. For if, of processions, the first in order is more like its principle, and what gives existence to the greatest number is more like, in being and power, to the principle that generates all things, then of second natures those nearer the monad, which receive rule after it, must give a greater extent to their productions; while those more separated from their first monad will neither reach through all things alike nor stretch their effective activities to far-off processions. And again, likewise, the nature that gives existence to the greatest number of effects must be ranked next to the monad, its principle; and the nature that generates a more numerous offspring, because it is more like the giver of all things than what generates few, must be ranked nearer the monad in hyparxis. For if it is further off, it will be more unlike the first principle; and if more unlike, it will have neither a power that holds the power of like natures nor a richly fertile activity. For an abundant cause is akin to the cause of all. And in general, what generates more is more akin by nature to its principle than what produces fewer offspring. For producing fewer effects is a lack of power; a lack of power is a lessening of being; and a lessening of being comes about through unlikeness to its cause and departure from the first principle.
!flag possess a less extended power of signification | Taylor's "signification" seems to mean reach or scope: the further causes are from the first, the fewer things they extend to. Compare Elements of Theology 56-62.

@p
Again, besides what has been said, we will state this, which has the most certain truth: that before shared causes, unshared causes must everywhere exist first in the whole of things. For if a cause must have the same relation to its offspring as the One to all the nature of beings, and must by nature have this order towards second things; and if the One is unshared, equally set apart from all beings, as producing all things in a unified way; then every other cause that imitates the transcendence of the One over all things must be set apart from the natures in the second ranks and shared by them. And again, likewise, every unshared and first cause must set up monads of second natures like itself before unlike ones. I mean, for instance, that one soul must distribute many souls to different natures, and one intellect shared intellects to many souls. For so the first set-apart kind will everywhere have an order answering to the One. And second natures that share in akin causes will answer to these causes, and through their likeness will be joined to their unshared principle. So before the Forms that are in other things are set those that exist in themselves; set-apart causes before ranked ones; and unshared monads before shared ones. And so, as is shown together with this, set-apart causes generate the ranked ones, and unshared natures stretch out shared monads to their offspring; and natures that exist from themselves produce the powers that reside in other things. These things discussed, let us consider how each of the divine kinds exists by analogy, and, following Plato himself, see what the first and most universal orders of the gods are. For having found and shown this, we will perhaps be able to see the truth about each of these orders.
!note Elements of Theology 23-24: every unshared term gives rise to shared ones, and the unshared comes before the shared. Taylor's footnote corrects "analysis" to "analogy" in the Greek he translated.

@p
From the axioms set out, then, since there is one unity, the principle of the whole of things, from which every hyparxis has its existence, this unity must produce from itself, before all other things, a multitude marked by unity, and a number most akin to its cause. For if every other cause sets up offspring like itself before what is unlike, much more must the One bring into light in this way the things after it, since it is beyond likeness, and the One itself must produce by union the things that first proceed from it. For how could the One give existence to its offspring except in a unified way? For nature generates what is second to it naturally, soul as soul does, and intellect intellectually. So the One is the cause of the whole of things by union, and the procession from the One is one-like. But if what first produces all things is the One, and the procession from it is unified, the multitude produced from there must surely be self-perfect henads, most akin to their producing cause. Further, if every monad sets up a number suited to itself, as was shown before, far more must the One generate a number of this kind. For in the procession of things, what is produced is often unlike its producing cause through the rule of difference: such are the last of things, far from their own principles. But the first number, born together with the One, is one-like, inexpressible, beyond being, and wholly like its cause. For in the first causes, neither does difference come between to separate the begotten from the begetter and carry it into another order, nor does the motion of the cause, slackening its power, bring the generation of the whole of things out into unlikeness and indefiniteness. The cause of all things, raised in a unified way above all motion and division, has set up about itself a divine number, and united it to its own simplicity. So the One, before beings, has given existence to the henads of beings.
!note Elements of Theology 113-114: the whole number of the gods is one-like, and every god is a self-perfect henad.

@ch III.3 | The henads join beings to the One
Being is unlike the One, so first beings share in the first cause through the henads nearest them, as bodies share in the whole soul through souls, and souls in intellect through intellects. This is why the Parmenides joins the one with being in the second hypothesis. But the henads must be shared by beings, each the flower and centre of a being; otherwise they would not differ from the One. Like things join the many to the One.

@p
Again, in another way, first beings must share in the first cause through their nearest henads. For second things are each joined to the natures before them through like things: bodies to the soul that ranks as a whole through the particular souls they share in; souls to the universal intellect through intellectual monads; and first beings to the One through unified hyparxes. For being is by its own nature unlike the One. For being, and what needs a union brought from outside, is not fit to be joined to what is beyond being and to the first union, and is far from it. But the henads of beings, since they have their existence from the unshared unity, set apart from the whole of things, are able to join beings to the One and turn them back to themselves.
!note Elements of Theology 64, 108-112: each order is joined to the order above it through its highest members.

@p
It seems to me, then, that Parmenides, showing these things through the second hypothesis, joins the one with being, sees all things about the one, and shows that this nature that proceeds, and stretches its processions as far as the last of things, is the one. For before true beings the henads had to be set up, since it neither was nor is lawful, says Timaeus, for what is best to do anything but what is most beautiful. And this is, in a remarkable degree, most like what is best. But to the One a unified multitude is most like; since the Craftsman of the universe too, being good, made all things like himself through goodness itself. Much more, then, does the source of all good produce goodnesses united to itself by nature, and set them in beings. So there is one God, and many gods; one henad and many henads before beings; one goodness, and many after the one goodness, through which the making intellect is good and every intellect is divine, whether intellectual or intelligible. And what is first beyond being is the One; and there are many beyond being after the One. Is this multitude of henads, then, unshared, as the One itself is, or is it shared by beings, so that each henad of beings is, as it were, the flower of some being, its summit and centre, about which each being exists? If these henads too are unshared, in what do they differ from the One? For each of them is one, and exists first from the One. Or in what, more than the first cause, were they set up by it? For everywhere what is second, being lower than what is before it, must fall short of the union of its producing cause, and by the addition of something have a lessening of the first's simplicity as a monad. What addition, then, can we name, or what more besides the one, if each of these too is by itself one? For if each of them is one and many, we will seem to carry over to them the character of being. But if each is one only, as the One itself is, why does that rank as the cause set apart from all things, while each of these has a second dignity? So we will neither keep the transcendence of the first over what comes after it, nor can we grant that the henads proceeding from it are unconfused with each other or with their one principle.
!flag connects the one with being, surveys all things about the one | Proclus means the one being of the Parmenides' second hypothesis (142b), "if one is", from which all the orders of gods are drawn. Taylor's two footnotes correct "one" to "being" and "present" to "leading" in the Greek he translated.
!note Timaeus 29e-30a: the Craftsman was good, and "wanted all things to be as like himself as possible"; "it neither was nor is lawful for the best to do anything but the most beautiful".

@p
Nor will we fail to be persuaded by the Parmenides, who produces the one together with being, and shows that there are as many parts of the one as of being; that each being shares in the one, and the one everywhere exists together with being; in short, who says that the one of the second hypothesis shares in being and is shared by being, the sharing not being the same in each. For the one shares in being, as not being first one, nor set apart from being, but as lighting up truly existing being. And being shares in the one, as held together by it, filled with divine union, and turned back to the unshared One itself. For the shared monads join beings to the One, set apart from the whole of things, as shared intellects join souls to the intellect that ranks as a whole, and shared souls join bodies to the soul that ranks as a whole. For the unlike kinds of second natures cannot be united without middles to the cause set apart from multitude; contact must be made through like things. For a like multitude, so far as it is multitude, shares with the unlike; but so far as it is like the monad before it, it is joined to it. Set, then, in the middle of both, it is united to the whole and to the One before multitude, but holds in itself far processions, of themselves unlike the One. Through itself, too, it turns all things back to that One, and so all things reach towards the first cause of the whole of things, the unlike through the like, and the like through themselves. For likeness by itself leads and binds the many to the one, and turns second natures back to the monads before them. For the very being of like things, as like, comes from the One. So it joins multitude to that from which it has its procession. And for this reason likeness is what it is, making many things akin, in sympathy with themselves, and friends with each other and with the One.
!flag But neither shall we be persuaded by Parmenides who produces the one together with being | The sense required is the opposite: "Nor shall we be persuaded otherwise by Parmenides", or "We shall not fail to be persuaded": Proclus is calling on the second hypothesis, which joins one and being, as evidence that the henads are shared by beings. Taylor's footnote corrects "unlike" to "like" in the Greek he translated.
!note The Parmenides 142b-144e: the one being has two parts, one and being, each of which is again one and being, so that the one is divided as far as being is.

@ch III.4 | Every god is a shared henad: the Republic's light
In the Republic the light that comes from the Good unites intellect and beings, as the sun's light makes visible things seen. This light is the one in beings. So, apart from the One itself, which alone is truly beyond being, every god is a henad shared by being, binding beings to itself and through itself to the One.

@p
If we must not only unfold the multitude of gods shared by beings through Parmenides' intellectual reachings, but also show briefly Socrates' account of these things, we must recall what is written in the Republic, where he says that the light that comes from the Good unites intellect and beings. For through this the Good is shown to be set apart from being and what is, as the sun is set apart from visible things. But this light, being in the intelligibles, lights them up, as the sun-like light in visible things does. For visible things become plain and known to sight in no other way than through the light born in them. All intelligibles, then, become good-like by sharing in light, and through this light every true being is most like the Good. If, then, it makes no difference whether we speak of this light or of the one (for this light joins the intelligibles and makes them one, having its existence from the One), the godhead that proceeds from the first is shared, and all the multitude of henads is shared. And what is truly beyond being is the One. But each of the other gods, by his own hyparxis, by which he is a god beyond being, is like the first; yet they are shared by being and what is. By this reasoning, then, the gods appear to us as henads, and shared henads, binding all beings to themselves, and, through themselves, joining the natures after them to the One that is set apart from all things alike.
!note Republic VI 508d-509b. Taylor's footnote supplies "truly" in the Greek he translated.

@ch III.5 | Nearer henads are shared by simpler beings
The henads have an order, like numbers: those nearer the One are shared by simpler and more universal beings, those further off by more composite ones, which are less in power but more in number. So too souls: the heavenly souls have simple bodies, the souls of the elements ethereal and material ones, and partial souls a third, composite life; and intellects likewise.

@p
Since, then, each of the gods is a henad, but is shared by some being, shall we say that the same being shares in each of the henads, or that those sharing in some henads are more, and in others fewer? And if so, either those sharing in the higher henads must be more and those of the lower fewer, or the reverse. For there must be an order of the henads, as we see that of numbers some are nearer their principle and others further from it; some simpler and others more composite, greater in quantity but lessened in power. And it is well that we have mentioned numbers. For if we must look at the order of the first monads among themselves, and their procession about beings, from these as images, then here too the monads nearer the One will be shared by things simpler in being, and those further from it by more composite beings. For so the sharing will be by analogy: first monads always shared by first beings, and second monads by second beings. Again, if the first is set apart from all things and unshared, and what is born together with the simplest nature, and the One, is more like the unshared than what is born together with a more varied and many-formed nature, with more powers hanging from it, then it is wholly plain that the henads nearer the One must be shared by the first and simplest beings, and those further off by more composite beings, which are less in power but greater in number and multitude. For in short, additions in these henads are takings away of powers; and what is nearer the One, which goes beyond the whole of things by a wonderful simplicity, is more one-like, and exists together with more universal orders. It follows, by the ratio of power, that the simplicity of the first henads is transcendent. For those that are the causes of more effects imitate as far as possible the cause of all things; but those that are the causes of fewer have a being more varied than the natures before them. For this variety breaks into small parts and lessens the power that remains in one. Moreover, among shared souls too, the first and most divine exist in simple and everlasting bodies. Others are joined to simple bodies, and together with these to material bodies too. And others are joined at once to simple, material and composite bodies. The heavenly souls rule over simple bodies, which exist immaterially and unchangingly. The souls that govern the wholes of the elements are clothed in ethereal garments, and through these are carried in the wholes of the elements, which as wholes are everlasting and simple, but as material receive generation, corruption, and composition from unlike natures. And the souls of the third rank are those that give life directly to their vehicles of light, but also draw from the simple element material garments, pour into these a second life, and through these share with composite, many-formed bodies, keeping up through this sharing another, third life.
!flag their luciform vehicles | The soul's "vehicle of light" (augoeides ochēma), the subtle body of light that, for the later Platonists, the soul has always, before it takes on the material body. Taylor's footnote corrects the Greek he translated here ("light-formed" for "self-formed").
!note Elements of Theology 196-210, on the soul's vehicles. Taylor's other footnote rewrites a corrupt sentence of the Greek he translated ("those that are the causes of more ... imitate the cause of all").

@p
If you are willing to look at the intellectual orders, some of these are set in the souls that rank as wholes, and in the most divine of the souls in the world, which they also govern as is fitting. Others, set in the souls of the higher kinds, are shared directly by the rulers among them, and secondarily by more partial beings. And again they set third intellectual orders in partial souls. And as the power they have is lessened, so the sharing in them is more varied, and far more composite than the sharing of the natures before them. If, then, this is the way of sharing in all beings, it is surely necessary that of the gods too, those nearer the One should be carried in the simpler parts of being, and those that have gone further should be carried in the more composite parts of being. For the sharings of second kinds are divided in this way, by likeness to the gods.
!flag in the souls of the more excellent genera | The "more excellent kinds" are the angels, daemons and heroes, set between the gods and human souls.

@ch III.6 | Being, life, intellect, soul and body
The orders of beings that share in the henads are, from the last up: body, which cannot give itself being; soul, which makes bodies better; intellect, above soul, eternal and without evil; life, above intellect, shared even by plants; and being, above life, shared even by things without life. The Sophist finds these four in perfect being. Each is unshared before it is shared, and there are as many orders of henads as of these.

@p
Again, then, we may say in brief that after the One, the one principle of the whole of things, the gods appear to us as self-perfect monads, shared by beings. How many orders of beings there are we will unfold later, and show which beings have a simpler and which a more varied hyparxis. The last of all beings, then, is the bodily. For it has its being and all its perfection from another, older cause, and has neither simplicity nor composition, nor everlastingness nor incorruptibility from its own power. For no body is self-existent or self-begotten; everything of that kind, drawing together into one cause and what proceeds from the cause, is bodiless and undivided. In short, what is the cause of hyparxis to itself also gives itself an unlimited power of existence. For never leaving itself, it will never cease to be, nor depart from its own existence. For everything that perishes perishes through being separated from the power that gave it being. But what gives being to itself, since it is not separated from itself, has through itself an everlasting being. No body, however, since it is not the cause of everlastingness to itself, will be everlasting. For everything everlasting has an unlimited power; but body, being limited, is not the cause of unlimited power. For unlimited power is bodiless, because all power is bodiless. And this is plain, because greater powers are everywhere; but no body can be wholly everywhere. If, then, no body gives itself power, whether unlimited or limited, but what is self-existent gives itself the power of being and of existing for ever, no body will be self-existent. From where, then, is being given to bodies, and what is fit to give them being most directly? Must we not say that the first cause of being to bodies is what, by being present, makes the nature of body more perfect than akin bodies deprived of it? This is plain to everyone. For it belongs to what gives perfection also to hold together the being of second natures, since perfection is the perfection of being. What, then, is that by sharing in which bodies are said to be better than bodies that do not share in it? Is it not clear that it is soul? For we say that ensouled bodies are more perfect than those without soul. So soul is first beyond bodies; and we must grant that the whole heaven, and everything bodily, is the vehicle of soul. So these two orders of beings appear to us: one bodily, the other, above it, of soul.
!note Elements of Theology 40-46 (the self-existent is bodiless and imperishable) and 186-188. Taylor's footnote: about a line and a half of the Greek he translated is so defective that he left it untranslated.

@p
Is soul itself, though, the same as intellect, or different? For as the body that shares in soul is perfect, so the soul that shares in intellect is perfect. And not all things share in the soul that can live by reason; but rational souls share in intellect and the light of intellect, and so do all things that have any kind of knowledge. And soul acts in time, while intellect holds in eternity both its being and its stable activity together. And not every soul is fit to keep its own perfection unchanged and undiminished; but every intellect is always perfect, and has an unfailing power of its own blessedness. So the intellectual kind is above the soul's kind in being; since the first, neither in whole intellects nor in partial ones, lets in the nature of evil, while the second, undefiled in whole souls, in partial souls falls away from its own blessedness. What, then, is the first of beings? Shall we say intellect, or, before this, the extent of life? For soul lives of itself, giving itself life; and intellect is the best and most perfect, and, as we have said, an eternal life. But the life of intellect is in a way intellectual, mixed from the intellectual and the living character. There must, though, be life itself. Which, then, is better, life or intellect? If only knowing beings share in intellect, but beings without knowledge share in life (for we say that plants live), life must surely be ranked above intellect, as the cause of more effects, giving more gifts from itself than intellect. What then? Is life the first of beings? Is to live the same as to be? This is impossible. For if life is what is first being, and to live is the same as to have being, with the same definition of life and being, then everything that shares in life would share in being, and everything that shares in existence would share in life. For if each is the same, all things would share alike in being and life. All living natures have being and existence; but there are many beings without life. So being exists before the first life. For what is more universal, and the cause of more effects, is nearer the One, as has been shown before. So soul is first set above bodies; intellect is beyond soul; life is older than intellect; and being, which is first being, is set above all these. Everything that shares in soul shares far before that in intellect; but not everything that enjoys the working of intellect is fit to share in soul. For only rational animals share in soul, since we say that the rational soul is truly soul. For Plato says in the Republic that the work of soul is to reason and look at beings; and every soul (every rational soul) is immortal, as is written in the Phaedrus, the irrational soul being mortal, according to the Craftsman in the Timaeus. In short, it is clear in many places that Plato takes the rational soul to be truly soul, and the others to be images of souls, so far as these too are intellectual and living, and together with whole souls produce the lives distributed about bodies. In intellect, however, we grant that not only rational animals share, but also such other animals as have a power of knowing, I mean those with imagination, memory and sense; since Socrates too, in the Philebus, refers all such animals to the intellectual series. For taking away intellect from the life of pleasure, he takes away not only the rational life but every power of knowing in the irrational life. For all knowledge is the offspring of intellect, as all reason is an image of soul.
!note Republic I 353d: the work of soul is to take care, rule and deliberate. The Phaedrus 245c: "all soul is immortal". Timaeus 69c-d: the gods made the mortal kind of soul. The Philebus 21b-d: without intellect, a life of pleasure would have no memory, opinion or knowledge of its pleasure. Taylor's footnote corrects "life" to "soul" in the Greek he translated.

@p
Moreover, all things that share in intellect share far before that in life, some more dimly and others more clearly. But not all living things share in intellectual power, since plants are living beings, as Timaeus says, but share in neither sense nor imagination, unless someone says they have a dim feeling of what pleases and pains. And in short, the powers of appetite are everywhere lives, images of the whole of life and its last productions; but of themselves they are without intellect and have no share in the power of knowing. So they are of themselves indefinite and deprived of all knowledge.
!flag the orectic powers | Taylor's "orectic" means of appetite or desire (orexis).
!note Timaeus 77a-c: plants are living beings that share in the third kind of soul, the appetitive, with sensation of pleasure and pain but no opinion or reason.

@p
Again, all animals receive a share of being, different animals a different share, according to their natures; but not all beings can share alike in life, since we say that qualities, all affections, and the last of bodies receive the last working of being, but we do not say that they share in life. So being is older than life, life than intellect, and intellect than soul. For the causes of more effects, being older and more ruling in order, must preside over causes able to produce and adorn fewer effects. So Plato rightly, in the Timaeus, gives soul its existence from intellect, as second to it by its own nature. In the Laws he says that intellect is moved like a sphere turned on a lathe. For what is moved is moved by sharing in life, and is nothing else than real life about motion. And in the Sophist he sets being apart from all the universal kinds of things, and from motion. For being, he says, by its own nature neither stands still nor is moved. And what neither stands still nor is moved is beyond eternal life.
!note The Laws X 898a-b: the motion of intellect is most like that of "a sphere turned on a lathe". The Sophist 250c: being, "by its own nature, neither stands still nor is moved". Taylor's footnote corrects the Greek he translated ("on a lathe").

@p
These four causes, then, being before bodily existence (being, life, intellect and soul), soul shares in all the causes before it: it has reason from its own character, but intellect, life and being from older causes. So it gives existence to what comes after it in a fourfold way. By its being, it produces all things down to bodies; by its life, all things said to live, down to plants; by its intellect, all things with a power of knowing, down to the most irrational natures; and by its reason, the first of the natures able to share in it. Intellect, set beyond soul, and existing as the fullness of life and being, adorns all things in a threefold way: it lights up all knowing beings with the power of the intellectual character, but gives sharing in life to still more, and in being to all those to whom first being gives itself. Life, set above intellect, exists before as the cause of the same things in a twofold way: giving life to second natures together with intellect, and filling from itself with the rivers of life the things by nature fit to live; and, together with being, producing being in all things from above. But being itself, which is first being, generates all things by its very existence, all lives, intellects and souls, is present to all things in a one-like way, and is set apart from the whole of things by one cause that gives existence to all things. So it is the most like the One of all things, and unites the embrace of beings in itself to the first principle of the whole of things, through which all beings and non-being exist, wholes and parts, Forms and the privations of Forms; and these privations do not need to share in being, but must certainly share in the One.
!note Elements of Theology 57: every cause acts before its effect and goes further down than it; the higher a cause, the further its working reaches. Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
These things, it seems to me, persuaded the Stranger from Elea in the Sophist, discussing what perfectly is, to grant that not only being is there, but also life, intellect and soul. For if true and real being is revered and honoured, intellect, he says, is there first. For it is not lawful for what is of itself revered and immaterial to be without intellect. And if intellect is in what perfectly is, intellect will surely be moved. For intellect cannot ever exist without motion, or without rest. But if intellect is moved and stands still, there are in being both life and motion. So from what has been said three things appear: being, life and intellect. Moreover, soul too is found next through these. For life and intellect, which before were by themselves, must, he says, also be in soul. For every soul is a fullness of life and intellect, sharing in both, which the Stranger points to when he adds: "Shall we say that both these are in it, but that it does not have them in soul?" For to have, as someone says somewhere, comes after to be. Soul shares in each of these by its own character, and mixes the rational form of its own hyparxis with the intellectual, life-giving power. But both intellect and life exist before soul, the first as moved and standing still at the same time, and the second as motion and rest. These four monads, soul, intellect, life and being, are mentioned by Plato not only here but in many other places. And as in soul all things exist by sharing, so in intellect the things before it exist, and in life what is before life. For we say that life exists, or has being. Or how could it be said to be ranked in being unless it shared in being? We likewise say that intellect is and lives. For it is moved, and is a portion of being. So it is the third of the more embracing monads. Before shared beings, though, unshared causes must everywhere exist, as was shown before, in keeping with the likeness of beings to the One. Being, then, which is first being, is unshared; life first shares in being, yet is unshared, set apart from intellect. Intellect is filled from being and life, but is unshared by souls and the natures after it. Intellect also presides over soul, giving it by its light a share in life and being; but being unshared, it exists before bodies. The last order of beings, then, is that to which bodies are attached: heavenly bodies first, and bodies below the moon with material garments added. This, then, is the procession of beings, through life, intellect and soul, ending in a bodily nature.
!flag the former as being moved and standing still at one and the same time, and the latter as being motion and permanency | That is, intellect is moved and at rest, while life is motion and rest themselves. Taylor's "permanency" here is rest (stasis).
!note The Sophist 248e-249b: "Shall we be easily persuaded that motion, life, soul and thought are not present in what perfectly is?"

@p
If, then, the henads of the gods beyond being, which have their existence from the unshared cause of all things, must be shared, some by the first orders among beings, others by the middle, and others by the last, as was shown before, it is clear that some make divine the unshared portion of being, while others light up life, others intellect, others soul, and others bodies. In the last henads not only bodies share, but also soul, intellect, life and being. For intellect is in itself a fullness of life and being. From the henads above the world, intellect hangs, and the power of soul that exists beforehand in intellect. From the henads above these, unshared, intellectual intellect hangs. From those beyond these, the first, unshared life hangs. And from the highest henads hangs first being itself, the most divine of beings. So Parmenides, beginning from the one being, produces from it the whole orders of the gods. These things settled beforehand, let us speak about the divine dialogues, beginning from on high, and producing from the One the whole orders of the gods. And, following Plato, let us first show the several orders from other dialogues, by arguments that cannot be refuted. Then let us join and fit the conclusions of the Parmenides to the divine processions, fitting the first conclusions to the first processions and the last to the last.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@ch III.7 | The One again, honoured in silence
Before the second and third principles, Proclus recalls the first: the unshared cause, called the Good in the Republic, the One in the Parmenides, the king of all in the Second Letter, and the cause of all godhead in the Philebus. By whatever name, it is to be honoured in silence, and before silence by union.

@p
Again, then, the mystic teaching about the One must be taken up by us, so that, proceeding from the first principle, we may praise the second and third principles of the whole of things. Before all beings, and before the gods who produce beings, there exists one set-apart and unshared cause: a cause inexpressible by all speech, unknown by all knowledge and beyond grasp, bringing all things into light from itself, existing inexpressibly before all things and turning all things back to itself, as the best end of all things. This cause, truly set apart from all causes, which gives existence in a unified way to all the henads of divine natures and to all the kinds of beings and their processions, Socrates in the Republic calls the Good, and through its analogy to the sun reveals its wonderful and unknown transcendence over all intelligibles. Parmenides, again, calls it the One, and through negations shows the set-apart and inexpressible hyparxis of this One, the cause of the whole of things. The account in the letter to Dionysius, proceeding through riddles, praises it as that about which all things are, and as the cause of all beautiful things. And in the Philebus Socrates praises it as what gives existence to the whole of things, because it is the cause of all godhead. For all the gods have their existence as gods from the first God. Whether, then, it is lawful to call it the source of godhead, or the kingdom of beings, or the henad of all henads, or the goodness that generates truth, or a hyparxis set apart from all these things and beyond all causes, both those of fathers and those of generation, let it be honoured by us in silence, and, before silence, by union; and of the mystic end may it give us, by its light, a share fitting our souls.
!note The Philebus 23c: "we said that god had shown the limit and the unlimited of beings" (quoted in the next chapter).

@ch III.8 | The two principles after the One: limit and the unlimited
After the One, Plato, following older theology and Philolaus, sets two principles: limit and the unlimited of the Philebus, from which god mixes all beings. Limit is the one before being, the cause of stable, unifying godhead; the unlimited is its power that generates being, the leader of all procession and multitude. Everything, from eternity to matter, has its remaining from limit and its procession from the unlimited.

@p
But let us look with intellect at the twofold principles that proceed from it and come after it. For what else must be set after the union of the whole study than the dyad of principles? What, then, the two principles of the divine orders after the first principle are, we will next consider. For in keeping with the theology of our forebears, Plato too sets two principles after the One. In the Philebus Socrates says that god gives existence to limit and the unlimited, and, mixing all beings through these, has produced them; the nature of beings, according to Philolaus, being made of the joining of limited things and unlimited things. If, then, all beings are made of these, clearly they themselves exist before beings. And if second natures share in these mixed together, these will exist unmixed before the whole of things. For the procession of the divine orders begins not from things ranked together and existing in others, but from things set apart and set in themselves. As, then, the One is before things united, and what is acted on by the One has a second rank after the unshared union, so the two principles of beings, before being shared in and mixed with beings, are by themselves the causes of the whole of things. For limit must be before limited things, and the unlimited before unlimited things, by the likeness to the One of what proceeds from it. For again, if we produced beings straight after the One, we would nowhere find the character of the One existing purely. For being is not the same as the one, but shares in the one; nor is the first really the one, for, as has often been said, it is better than the one. Where, then, is what is most properly and wholly one? So there is a one before being, which gives existence to being and is first its cause; since what is before it is beyond union, a cause without relation to all things, and unshared, set apart from all things. But if this one is the cause of being and sets it up, there will be a power in it that generates being. For everything that produces, produces by its own power, which exists between the producer and what is produced: of the one it is the procession and, as it were, the stretching out; of the other, the generating cause set before it. For being, produced from these, which is not the One itself but one-like, has its procession from the one through the power that produces it and brings it into light from the one, and its hidden union from the hyparxis of the one. This one, then, which exists before power, and first exists from the unshared and unknown cause of the whole of things, Socrates in the Philebus calls limit, and its power that generates being he calls the unlimited. He speaks thus in that dialogue: "God, we said, has shown the limit and the unlimited of beings."
!flag nor in reality is that which is the first the one; for, as has been frequently said, it is better than the one | Proclus means that the first principle is beyond even the name "one"; the one that is the principle of being is limit, the first after the first.
!note The Philebus 16c (the ancients handed down that things are made of one and many, with limit and unlimitedness in them), 23c-d, 26e-27b. Philolaus, frag. 1-2: "nature in the cosmos was fitted together from unlimited things and limiting things". Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
The first, unified God, then, is called by him God without any addition, because each of the second gods is shared by being and has being hanging from it. But the first, as set apart from the whole of beings, is God, defined by the inexpressible itself, unified alone, and beyond being. But the limit and the unlimited of beings bring that unknown and unshared cause into light: limit as the cause of stable, one-like, holding-together godhead; the unlimited as the cause of power that proceeds to all things and can be multiplied, and, in short, as the leader of every generating distribution. For all union and wholeness and sharing of beings, and all the divine measures, hang from the first limit; while all division, fertile production and procession into multitude have their existence from this most ruling unlimitedness. So when we say that each of the divine orders remains and at the same time proceeds, we must grant that it remains stably by limit, but proceeds by the unlimited, and has at the same time unity and multitude; and we must hang the first from the principle of limit and the second from that of the unlimited. In short, of all the oppositions in the divine kinds, we must refer the better to limit and the lower to the unlimited. For from these two principles all things proceed into being, down to the last of things. Eternity itself shares at once in limit and the unlimited: as the intelligible measure, in limit; as the cause of an unfailing power of existing, in the unlimited. Intellect, so far as it is one-like and whole, and holds together the measures of the patterns, is the offspring of limit; but so far as it produces all things eternally, and exists in keeping with the whole of eternity, giving all things existence at once and always keeping its own power undiminished, it is the offspring of the unlimited. Soul, measuring its own life by returns and periods, and setting a boundary to its own motions, is referred to the cause of limit; but having no rest from motion, and making the end of one period the beginning of the whole of a second circuit of life, it is referred to the order of the unlimited. The whole of this heaven, too, in its wholeness, its holding together, the order of its periods and the measures of its returns, is limited; but in its fertile powers, its various unrollings and the unfailing revolutions of its spheres, it shares in the unlimited. Moreover, the whole of coming to be, since all its Forms are limited and always remain in the same way, and since its own circle imitates the heavenly circuit, is like limit; but in the variety of the particulars it is made of, their unceasing change, and the more and less that come into the sharing of Forms, it is the image of the unlimited. Besides these, every natural thing is like limit in its form, and like the unlimited in its matter. For these hang last from the two principles after the One, and the procession of their productive power reaches as far as these. Each of these too is one; but form is the measure and boundary of matter, and is more one. Matter is all things in potential, as having its existence from the first power. There, though, power generates all things; but the power of matter is imperfect, and needs the reality that generates all things in act. Rightly, then, does Socrates say that all beings come from limit and the unlimited, and that these two intelligible principles first have their existence from God. For what gathers both of them, perfects them, and brings itself into light through all beings is the One before the dyad. Union comes to all things through the first; but the division of the two orders of things comes from these first causes, and through these reaches to the unknown and inexpressible principle. Let it be clear, then, from these things, what the two principles of beings are that appear next after the One, according to Plato's theology.
!flag by restitutions and periods | Taylor's "restitutions" are returns (apokatastaseis): the soul's, and the heaven's, coming back round to the same state at the end of each period.
!note Taylor's footnotes correct the Greek he translated in five places ("hangs from" for "is set apart from", twice; "to remain", "lastly", and "generative").

@ch III.9 | The mixed: first being
The third thing, from limit and the unlimited, is the Philebus' mixed, which is first being: being itself, holding life and intellect in itself in the manner of being. It comes from the first God, who is the Philebus' fourth cause, the cause of the mixture, and from the two principles. From limit it has hyparxis and stability, from the unlimited power and hidden multitude. So it is one-many, the "first power" of the Sophist; Plotinus' intelligible matter is right if it means power, not formlessness.

@p
Next, let us show what the third thing is that appears from these principles. It is everywhere called the mixed, as having its existence from limit and the unlimited. But if limit is the limit of beings, and the unlimited is the unlimited of beings, and beings are the things that exist from both, as Socrates himself clearly teaches us, it is clear that the first of mixed things is the first of beings. This is nothing else than what is highest among beings: being itself, and nothing but being. My meaning is that this is clear from the things by which we show that what is first being holds all things in an intelligible way, life and intellect too. For we say that life is threefold in the manner of life, and intellect in the manner of intellect, and that these three, being, life and intellect, are everywhere. But all things exist beforehand, first and in the manner of being, in being. For there are being, life and intellect, the summit of beings. Life is the middle centre of being, and is called, and is, intelligible life. Intellect is the boundary of being, and is intelligible intellect. For in the intelligible there is intellect, and in intellect the intelligible; but there intellect is intelligibly, and in intellect the intelligible is intellectually.
!note The Philebus 23c-d: the third kind, the mixture of limit and the unlimited. Taylor's footnote supplies "third" in the Greek he translated.

@p
