Being is what is stable in being, woven together with the first principles and not departing from the One. Life is what proceeds from the principles, born together with unlimited power. And intellect is what turns itself back to the principles, joins the end with the beginning, and makes one intelligible circle. The first of beings, then, is what is mixed from the first principles, and is threefold: one thing it holds is in it in the manner of being, another in the manner of life, and another in the manner of intellect, but all things exist beforehand in it in the manner of being. By the first of beings I mean being in the strict sense. For being itself is the summit of all beings, and, as it were, the monad of the whole of things. In all things, then, being is first; and in each thing what belongs to being is oldest, as having its existence from Hestia, the hearth of beings. For the intelligible is most of all this; since intellect is what knows, life is thinking, and being is intelligible. If, then, every being is mixed, but being is being itself, before all other things being is what exists mixed from the two principles that proceed from the One. So Socrates, pointing out how the way the two principles are generated differs from that of the mixture, says that "god has shown limit and the unlimited". For they are henads that have their existence from the One, and, as it were, shining manifestations of the unshared and first union. But making a mixture, and mixing through the first principles, is as far below bringing into light as making is below unfolding, and generating below manifesting; and by so much the mixed has a procession from the One lower than that of the two principles.
!flag deriving its subsistence from the Vesta of beings | Taylor's "Vesta" is Hestia, the hearth: being is the fixed centre from which all beings have their existence.
!flag luminous patefactions | Taylor's "patefaction" means a laying open, a manifestation: limit and the unlimited are "shown" (Philebus 23c) by the first God, not made.

@p
The mixed, then, is intelligible being, and exists first from the first God, from whom the unlimited and limit come too. But it exists secondarily from the principles after the unified God, I mean from limit and the unlimited. For the fourth cause, which produces the mixture, is again God himself; since if any other cause besides this were granted, there would no longer be a fourth cause, but a fifth would be brought in. For the first cause was God, who brings the two principles into light; after him come the two principles, limit and the unlimited; and the mixture is the fourth thing. So if the cause of the mixture is different from the first divine cause, this cause will be the fifth and not the fourth, as Socrates says it is. Further, if we say that God most of all gives union to beings, and the mixture of the principles is itself a union into the reality of being, God is surely first the cause of this too. Moreover, Socrates in the Republic clearly shows that the Good is the cause of being and the very being of intelligibles, as the sun is of visible things. Must we not, then, if the mixed is first being, refer it to the first God, and say that it receives its procession from him? Again, the Craftsman in the Timaeus makes the being of the soul by itself from an undivided and a divisible being, which is the same as making it from limit and the unlimited (for the soul by limit is like the undivided, and by the unlimited like the divisible being); if, then, the Craftsman mixes the being of the soul from these, and again separately from same and different, and from these, now existing beforehand, makes the whole soul, must we not much more say that the first God is the cause of the first being? The mixed, then, proceeds, as we have said, from the first God; it does not exist from the principles after the One alone, but proceeds from these too, and is threefold. First, it shares from God in inexpressible union and the whole of its existence. From limit it receives hyparxis, the one-like, and a stable character. From the unlimited it receives power, and the hidden power of all things that is in it. For in short, since it is one and not one, the one is in it by limit, and the not-one by the unlimited. But the mixture of both, and its wholeness, come from the first God. So the mixed is a monad, because it shares in the One; it is twofold, so far as it proceeds from the two principles; and it is a triad, so far as in every mixture, according to Socrates, these three are necessary: beauty, truth and proportion. Of these, though, we will speak again.
!note The Philebus 23d, 26e-27b: the fourth kind is the cause of the mixture. Timaeus 35a: the Craftsman mixes the soul's being from the undivided and the divisible, and then from the same and the different.

@p
How being is what is first mixed we will now explain. For of all things this is the hardest to discover, what first being is, as the Stranger from Elea also says somewhere; for it is most puzzling how being is no less than non-being. How, then, being exists from limit and the unlimited must be shown. For if limit and the unlimited are beyond being, being may seem to have its existence from things that are not being. How, then, can things that are not being produce being? Or is this not so in all other things that exist through mixing with each other? For what is produced from things mixed together is not the same as the things not mixed. Soul is not the same as the kinds from which the father generated it by mixing them; nor is the happy life the same as the life of intellect or the life of pleasure; nor is the one in bodies the same as its elements. So it is not surprising if what is first being, though it is neither limit nor the unlimited, exists from both and is mixed: not that the natures beyond being are themselves taken into its mixture, but second processions from them come together into the existence of being. So being is made of these, sharing in both: it has the one-like from limit, and the generative, and in short hidden, multitude from the unlimited. For it is all things in a hidden way, and so is the cause of all beings. The Stranger from Elea points to this when he calls being the first power, as existing by sharing in the first power, and having hyparxis from limit and power from the unlimited. Later, though, the Stranger defines being as power, as fertile and generative of all things, and as being all things in a one-like way. For power is everywhere the cause of fertile processions and of all multitude: hidden power of hidden multitude, and the power that exists in act and brings itself into light of all-perfect multitude. Through this cause, I think, every being has powers born with it. For it shares in the unlimited, and has its hyparxis from limit but its power from the unlimited. And being is nothing else than a monad of many powers, a multiplied hyparxis; and so being is one-many. The many exist in a hidden way and without separation in the first natures, but with separation in second natures. For the nearer being is to the One, the more it hides multitude, and is defined by union alone. It seems to me, too, that Plotinus and his followers, often pointing to these things, produce being from form and intelligible matter, setting form as answering to the One and to hyparxis, and power as answering to matter. If they say this, they speak rightly. But if they give a certain formless and indefinite nature to intelligible being, they seem to me to stray from Plato's ideas on this subject. For the unlimited is not the matter of limit but its power; nor is limit the form of the unlimited, but its hyparxis. And being is made of both, as not only standing in the one, but receiving a multitude of henads and powers mixed into one being.
!flag calls being the first power | The Sophist 247d-e: the Stranger proposes "that being is nothing else than power" (dynamis), to act or be acted on. Proclus reads it as the power, born of the unlimited, that being holds.
!note The Sophist 243b-c, 250d-e: being is as hard to grasp as non-being. Plotinus on intelligible matter: II.4.2-5. The Philebus 22a-b: the good life is neither the life of pleasure nor that of thought alone. Taylor's footnote corrects "multitude" to "form" in the Greek he translated.

@ch III.10 | Limit and the unlimited are twofold
Coming to be, an image of first being, is also mixed of limit and the unlimited: its unlimited is matter, imperfect power, and its limit is form. But in being, the unlimited is perfect, generating power, not matter. Limit and the unlimited are twofold: set apart before being, as its causes, and in being, as the elements of the mixture. So the Philebus can set the mixed before them, and we can set them before the mixed.

@p
What is first being, then, is for these reasons called by Plato the mixed. And by likeness to it, coming to be too is mixed from limit and the unlimited. In this the unlimited is imperfect power, and the limit is the form and shape of this power. So we set this power down as matter, not existing in act, and needing to be limited by something else. But we no longer say it is lawful to call the power of being matter, since it generates activities, produces all beings from itself, and is fertile of the perfect powers in beings. For the power of matter, being imperfect, imitates the power of being in an unlike way; and becoming multitude in potential, it copies the labour with multitude in the power of being. Moreover, the form of matter imitates limit at the last, since it gives bounds to matter and ends its unlimitedness; but it is multiplied and divided about it. It is also mixed with the privation of matter, and represents the highest union of the hyparxis of being by its being always moving towards existence and always tending to decay. For what is in the first natures by transcendence is in the last by deficiency. For what is first being is mixed too, is set apart from the limit of unlimited life, and is its cause; but what is made of the last of forms and first matter is in its own nature without life, since it has life in potential. For there, generating causes exist before their offspring, and perfect things before imperfect; but here, things in potential come before things in act, and joint causes are set below what is produced from them. This happens by nature, I think, because the gifts of the first principles reach as far as the last of things, and generate not only more perfect natures but also those with a more imperfect existence. And for this reason the mixed is the cause of coming to be, and of the nature mixed here. But limit and the unlimited before being are the causes not only of this nature, but of its elements, of which the mixed, as mixed, is not the cause. For limit and the unlimited are twofold: one kind is set apart from the mixed things, the other is taken into the completion of the mixture. For I think that everywhere, before mixed things there must be unmixed ones; before imperfect things, perfect ones; before parts, wholes; and before things in others, things in themselves. And Socrates persuades us to grant this not of one thing only, but of beauty and proportion and all Forms. If, then, the second and third kinds of being and Forms exist before what shares in them, how can we say that limit and the unlimited, which run through all beings, first exist as mixed? We must grant, then, that they are unmixed and separate from being, and that being comes from them and at the same time is made of them. It comes from them, because they exist before; it is made of them, because they exist in being by a second procession.
!flag concauses are subject to the things which are produced from them | Taylor's "concauses" are joint or contributing causes (synaitia), such as matter: in the lower world they come before, and are set below, what is made from them.
!note Taylor's footnotes correct the punctuation of one sentence of the Greek he translated, and "first" to "last" (of form), since Proclus is speaking of body.

@p
The kinds of being too are twofold: some make beings, others are the elements of the nature of each being. For some exist beforehand by themselves, as having a productive power; others, generated from these, make up each particular being. So let no one any longer wonder how Socrates in the Philebus sets the mixed before limit and the unlimited, while we, on the contrary, show that limit and the unlimited are set apart from the mixed. For each is twofold: one is before being, the other in being; one generates, the other is an element of the mixture. Of this kind too are the limit and the unlimited of the mixed life, each being an element of the whole of happiness. So each needs the other: neither is intellect by itself desirable, nor perfect pleasure. The good, though, must be made of all these: the desirable, the sufficient and the perfect. Limit itself, then, and the unlimited, which are separate, exist as causes before the mixed; but the limit and the unlimited that are mixed are more imperfect than the mixture. From what has been said, then, it is clear what the things are of which the mixture is made.
!note The Philebus 20d-22b, 61a-64a: the good life is a mixture of pleasure and thought, neither enough alone.

@ch III.11 | Proportion, truth and beauty in the mixed
Every right mixture, says the Philebus, needs proportion, truth and beauty. In the first mixture, proportion makes being one, truth makes it real, and beauty makes it intelligible. Iamblichus defined the intelligible by these three. They stand in the vestibule of the Good, and here, hidden, is the first beauty.

@p
Next we must speak of the triad that exists together with this mixture. For every mixture, if it is rightly made, as Socrates says, needs these three things: beauty, truth and proportion. Nothing ugly, brought into the mixture, will give it rightness, since it will cause error and disorderly excess; nor, if truth is ever separated, will it let the mixture be made of things pure and truly mastered, but it will fill the whole with an image and with non-being. Nor without proportion will there be a sharing of the elements and a graceful company. Proportion, then, is needed for the union of the things mixed and a fitting sharing; truth for purity; and beauty for order, which also makes the whole lovely. For when each thing in the mixture has its fitting place, it makes both the elements and the arrangement that comes from them beautiful. Here, then, in the first mixture, these three things appear: proportion, truth and beauty. Proportion is the cause of being one to the mixture; truth, of the reality of its existence; and beauty, of its being intelligible. So it is intelligible and truly being. What is first being, too, is more one-like, and intellect is joined to it by its closeness to the beautiful. But each shares in existence, because it is being derived from being. The mixed is highest among beings, because it is united to the Good. It seems to me that the divine Iamblichus, seeing these three causes of being, defines the intelligible by these three, proportion, truth and beauty, and through them unfolds the intelligible gods in his Platonic theology. How the intelligible breadth is made of these will be clearest as we proceed. From what has been said, though, it is wholly plain why Socrates says that this triad is found in the vestibule of the Good. For what is first being shares in this triad through its union with the Good. Because the Good is the measure of all beings, the first being becomes itself in proportion. Because the Good is before being, the first being exists truly and really. And because the Good is good and desirable, the first being appears as the beautiful itself. Here, then, the first beauty exists too; and so the One is the cause not only of good but of beauty, as Plato says in his Letters. Beauty is here in a hidden way, since this order holds all things in a one-like way, existing first from the principles, limit and the unlimited. Where and how beauty comes into light, we will soon explain.
!note The Philebus 64a-65a: the mixture needs measure and proportion, truth and beauty, and "we find the good taking refuge in the nature of the beautiful"; the three stand "in the vestibule of the good" (64c). Iamblichus' Platonic Theology is lost. The Second Letter 312e: the king of all is "the cause of all beautiful things".

@ch III.12 | The first intelligible triad
The first triad of intelligibles is the Philebus' limit, unlimited and mixed: limit a god proceeding to the intelligible summit, giving existence to the fatherly and guarding gods; the unlimited his unfailing power; and the mixed the first and highest order of gods, holding all things hidden.

@p
Such, then, is the first triad of intelligibles, according to Socrates in the Philebus: limit, the unlimited, and what is mixed from these. Limit is a god proceeding to the intelligible summit from the unshared and first God, measuring and defining all things, and giving existence to every fatherly, holding and undefiled kind of gods. The unlimited is the unfailing power of this god, bringing into light all the generating orders and all unlimitedness, that before being, that of being, and that which proceeds as far as last matter. And the mixed is the first and highest order of gods, holding all things in a hidden way, completed through the intelligible triad that holds together, holding in a unified way the cause of every being, and setting its summit in the first intelligibles, set apart from the whole of things.
!flag deriving its completion indeed through the intelligible connective triad | That is, the mixed is completed through the triad of limit, unlimited and mixed itself, the intelligible triad that "holds together" (synektikē).

@ch III.13 | The second intelligible triad: intelligible life
The second triad mirrors the first: a henad (its limit), its power (the unlimited), and second being, which is intelligible life. The first triad is all things by limit, hidden; the second all things by the unlimited, as life; the third by the mixed. Of proportion, truth and beauty, proportion rules in the first, truth in the second, beauty in the third. And the third triad too must be made divine by a henad.

@p
After this first triad, existing from the One and joined to it, we will praise the second, proceeding from the first and completed through things answering to the triad before it. For here too being must share, and the one be shared, and this one, which is second one, must generate what is second being. For everywhere shared godhead sets up about itself what shares in it. So whole souls make bodies exist together with their causes, and partial souls generate irrational souls together with the gods. Much more, then, do the gods produce all things together with the One. So as the first of the henads generates the summit of being, so the middle henad sets up the middle being. And everything that generates, makes or produces has a power fertile of what is produced, by which it produces, strengthens and holds together its offspring. Again, then, a second triad will come into light, answering to the first. One thing is its summit, which we call one, godhead and hyparxis; another its middle, which we call power; and another its end, which we say is second being. This is intelligible life. For all things are in the intelligible, as was shown before: to be, to live, and to act by thinking. The summit of the intelligible order is all things as cause and, as we have often said, in a hidden way. Its middle makes multitude shine out, and proceeds from the union of being into plain light. And its end is now all intelligible multitude and the order of intelligible Forms. For Forms have their existence at the end of the intelligible order, since Forms must first exist and appear in intellect. If, then, being remains set apart in the first mixture, but now proceeds and is generated in a twofold way from the monad, there will be motion about it; and if there is motion, there must also be intelligible life. For everywhere motion is a certain life, since someone calls even the motion of material bodies life. The first in this second triad, then, may be called limit; the second, the unlimited; and the third, life. For the second triad too is a god, having fertile power, and bringing into light, from and about himself, what is second being. Here too, though, the triad answers to the first triad.
!flag some one calls even the motion of material bodies life | Aristotle, Physics VIII.1 (250b): motion is "a kind of life for all things that exist by nature". Taylor's footnote corrects "irrational" to "analogous" in the Greek he translated.

@p
But again we must grasp by reasoning the character of this triad. The first triad is all things, but intelligibly and in a unified way, and, as I may say, speaking as Plato does, in the form of limit. The second triad is all things, but in the manner of life, and, as I may say, following the philosopher, in the form of the unlimited; just as the third triad proceeds by the character of the mixed. For as in the procession in breadth the mixed appears third, so in the procession of intelligibles in depth the third has the rank of the mixed in relation to the higher triads. The middle triad, then, is all things, but marked by intelligible unlimitedness. For the three principles after the first set out for us in order the intelligible kind of gods: limit brings into light the first triad, the unlimited the second, and the mixed the third. It is by unlimited power, then, that the second triad is marked. For, being the middle, it exists by the middle of the first triad, being all things from all. For in each triad there are limit, the unlimited and the mixed; but the character of the monads, different in each, unrolls the intelligible order of the gods. The middle triad, existing in this way (I say "in this way" because it is made of all the things the triad before it is made of), holds and binds the middle of intelligibles by unlimited power. It is filled from a higher union, and fills the union after it with the powers of being. It is measured from there in a one-like way, and measures the third triad by its own power. It remains stably in the first triad, and sets in itself the triad next in order. In short, it binds to itself the intelligible centre and sets up one intelligible coherence, making shine out what is hidden and one-formed in the first triad, and gathering the intelligible multitude of the third triad and holding it on all sides. The being that completes this triad is mixed, as the being of the triad before it is, and receives the character of life. For the unlimited in it generates life.
!flag as in the progression according to breadth | Proclus' two dimensions: "breadth" is the spread of the three terms within one triad (limit, unlimited, mixed); "depth" is the descent of the three triads one below another.

@p
This triad must also share in the three, proportion, truth and beauty. What is first being exists chiefly by proportion, which unites it and joins it to the Good. The second triad exists chiefly by truth: because it shares in what is first being, it is being, and truly being. The third triad exists chiefly by the beautiful: for there intelligible multitude, order and beauty first shine out. So this being is the most beautiful of all intelligibles; but this will be discussed later. As there is a triad, then, in each of the mixtures, proportion most of all holds together the first, truth the second, and beauty the third. And this led the divine Iamblichus to say that Plato defines the whole intelligible order by these three. For all are in each, but one of them rules more in one of the intelligible monads than in another. Moreover, the third triad appears after this. For the end of being must also be made divine, and share in an intelligible henad. For beings are not more in number than the henads, as Parmenides says, nor are the henads more numerous than beings; each procession of being shares in the one; since this universe too, in each part of itself, is governed by soul and intellect. Much more, then, must the intelligible, in its first, middle and last realities, share in the intelligible gods.
!note The Parmenides 144c-e: the one is divided by being into as many parts as being, so that the one and being are equal in number.

@ch III.14 | The third intelligible triad: intelligible intellect
The third henad sets up intelligible intellect, in which all intelligible multitude and Form appear. The first triad remains, the second remains and proceeds, the third turns back to the beginning; together they announce the first God's union, his transcendence over powers, and his generation of beings. The first is an intelligible god, the second intelligible and intellectual, the third intellectual. Being is the set-apart cause; the Forms, divided causes and patterns.

@p
As the first henad, then, after the set-apart cause of all things, brings into light intelligible being, and the second henad intelligible life, so the third sets up about itself intelligible intellect, fills it with divine union, and sets up power as the middle between itself and being, through which it completes this being and turns it back to itself. In this, then, every intelligible multitude shines out to view. For the whole of this being is intelligible intellect, life and being. It is neither all things as cause, as what is first being is, nor does it make all things shine out, as the second being does; but it is, as it were, all things in act, and openly. So it is the boundary of all intelligibles. For since the procession of beings goes by likeness, the first being is most like the One; the second is in labour with multitude and is the origin of separation; and the third is now all-perfect, and brings into light in itself intelligible multitude and Form.

@p
Further, as the first triad remains hidden in limit, and fixes in itself everything stable in intelligibles, and the second at once remains and proceeds, so the third, after procession, turns the intelligible end back to the beginning and rolls the order back to itself. For everywhere it is the work of intellect to turn and converge to the intelligible. And all these are one-like and intelligible: the remaining, the proceeding and the returning. For each of these is not said in the same way among intelligibles. The intelligible kind of gods is unified, simple and hidden, joining itself to the One itself, which is before beings, and brings into light nothing else than the transcendence of the One. For these three triads announce mystically that unknown cause, the first and wholly unshared God: the first announcing his inexpressible union; the second his transcendence, by which he goes beyond all powers; and the third his all-perfect generation of beings. For as they are able to hold the principle that goes beyond both the union and the powers of all beings, so they show second natures his wonderful transcendence, receiving separately the unified power and rule of the first God, and bringing into light intelligibly the cause before intelligibles. For these gods, though they have a simplicity equally set apart from all the divine orders, still fall short of the union of the father. Of this triad, then, which turns all intelligibles back to the first principle and rolls the multitude that appears in it back to the stable union of the whole of things, one thing is limit, henad and hyparxis; another, the unlimited and power; and another, the mixed: being, life and intelligible intellect. But the whole triad exists by being, and is the intellect of the first triad. For the first triad is an intelligible god first; the triad after it an intelligible and intellectual god; and the third triad an intellectual god. These three godheads, triadic monads, complete the intelligible kinds. For they are monads by their godheads, since all other things hang from the gods, powers and beings too. But they are triads by a separate division. For limit, the unlimited and the mixed exist in three ways: in one place all things are by limit; in another all things by the unlimited; and in another all things by the mixed. In one place the mixed is being; in another, intelligible life; and in another, intelligible intellect. In this last, then, the Forms first exist. For the separating of intelligibles unfolds the order of Forms, because Form is being, but not simply being. So what is first being is being itself, and what is being. What is the second being is power, proceeding from the first being, and existing as a dyad, as it were, that generates the multitude of beings, but not yet multitude. And what is the third being is itself the multitude of beings, being there existing with separation. For being is the set-apart cause of the things that the Forms set up dividedly; and of the things that being produces together, the Forms are the cause with separation. For Forms are causes that produce separation in their effects; and also Forms are called the patterns of beings. Being is the cause of all that comes after it, but not their pattern. For patterns are the causes of things separated in existence and having different characters of being. After the One before beings, then, comes what is one-many in a hidden way, the united. So it is what is divided into multitude, and tends from the one-like to the bright. And the last of intelligibles is that from which a certain distribution into parts begins, and which holds intelligible multitude.
!flag the first triad is an intelligible God primarily. But the triad posterior to it is an intelligible and intellectual God. And the third triad is an intellectual God | Not the later orders of the intelligible-intellectual and intellectual gods (Books IV and V), but the three intelligible triads, each named by its character: the third, intelligible intellect, is "intellectual" within the intelligible. Taylor's footnotes correct the Greek he translated in three places.

@ch III.15 | The Timaeus: the living being itself, the third intelligible triad
In the Timaeus the pattern of the world is the "living being itself": all-perfect, holding all intelligible living beings, the most beautiful of intelligibles, only-begotten, and eternal. It is above the Craftsman, who holds the Forms in a divided way, with the mixing bowl. Being all-perfect multitude, it is the third intelligible triad; being eternal, it comes next after eternity.

@p
Socrates in the Philebus, then, gives us such aids to the study of the intelligible triads. But we must not only remain in these ideas; we must also show Plato's theology of these triads from other dialogues, and from them point out one truth suited to the things themselves. Let us take what is written in the Timaeus, follow our leader Syrianus, who unfolded to us the secret mysteries of these triads, and join to the end of what has been said the beginning of the discussion that follows. In the Timaeus, then, Plato, searching for what the pattern of the whole world is, finds that it holds all intelligible living beings, is all-perfect, is the most beautiful of intelligibles, is only-begotten, and is the intelligible of the Craftsman. He also calls it the living being itself, as the intelligible pattern of every living being, and of what is perceptible. So this living being itself, because it is all-perfect and the most beautiful of intelligibles, must be set in the intelligible orders. For though there is an intelligible living being in the Craftsman, it is intellectual rather than intelligible, and not the most beautiful of all intelligibles, but second to them in beauty and power. For first beauty is in the intelligible gods. In the Craftsman too there are not only the four Forms of things in the world but all the multitude of Forms; for in him the patterns of particular Forms exist beforehand. But the living being itself sets up all living beings as wholes by the intelligible tetrad. Again, the Craftsman is not, like the living being itself, only-begotten among beings; he exists together with the life-giving cause, with which he sets up the second kinds of being, mixing them in the mixing bowl for the generation of souls. For of the things that the intelligible living being produces and at the same time generates, the Craftsman has the cause in a divided way, together with the bowl. So, as I have said, the living being itself is set apart from the Craftsman, and is, as Timaeus everywhere calls it, intelligible.
!flag animal itself | Taylor's "animal itself" is the Timaeus' "living being itself" (autozōon), the pattern of the world.
!note Timaeus 30c-31b, 39e: the world's pattern is "the living being of which all other living beings are parts", "the most beautiful of intelligibles and in every way perfect", and one only. The "four Forms" are the four kinds of living being (39e-40a): the heavenly gods, birds, water creatures and land animals. The mixing bowl: Timaeus 41d.

@p
Yet because Forms are first separated in it, and because it is all-perfect, it exists in the third order of intelligibles. For neither what is first being nor what is second being is all-perfect. The first is beyond all separation; the second generates and is in labour with intelligibles, but is not yet the multitude of beings. If, then, neither of these is multitude, how can either be all-perfect multitude? But if all-perfect multitude shines out in the third triad of intelligibles, as was shown a little before, and the living being itself is the first pattern (for it holds all intelligible living beings, is an only-begotten pattern, and is not joined with any other principle), the living being itself must be set in this order. For either there will be no intelligible pattern (and then how will perceptible things be images of intelligibles, or how will the intelligible gods be the fathers of the whole of things?), or, if there is, it is the third among intelligibles. For the natures before the triad among intelligibles are not all-perfect, since they are set apart from division into multitude. And the natures after it are not only-begotten, for they proceed together with others, the male with the female, and those of a making character with those of a generating one. Nor are they the most beautiful of intelligibles; for beauty is in the intelligible. But the living being itself is all-perfect and at the same time only-begotten. So the first pattern of beings is ranked in the third triad of intelligibles. Moreover, the living being itself is eternal, as Timaeus himself says: "the nature of the living being is eternal". And again elsewhere he says that "the pattern is being through all eternity". If, then, it is eternal, it shares in eternity. And if what shares is everywhere second to what is shared, the living being itself is second to eternity. And if it is being through all eternity, it is filled with the whole power of eternity. If so, it exists next after eternity. For what enjoys the whole of its causes is ranked next after them.
!note Timaeus 37d ("the nature of the living being was eternal") and 38c ("the pattern is being through all eternity"). Taylor's footnote: the "first life" is intelligible life, life itself.

@ch III.16 | Eternity is the middle intelligible triad
Eternity is to the living being itself as time is to the world, so it is above it; it is the first immortal, life itself, and so in the middle of the intelligible order. It is not first being, which neither rests nor moves, nor intelligible intellect, which shares in it; it lies between them.

@p
Moreover, if eternity has the same ratio to the intelligible living being as time to the perceptible one, and the universe shares directly in time (for time came to be together with the universe), the living being itself must surely share first in eternity. So eternity is above the first pattern. For eternity measures the existence of the living being itself, and the living being itself is measured and filled with everlastingness from it. Besides, we say that eternity is the cause of immortality to all things. So eternity is what is first immortal. For as what is first being is the cause of existence to all things, and what produces form is itself before other Forms, so what is the cause of everlastingness and immortality is itself first immortal. The daemonic Aristotle also rightly calls eternity immortal and divine, that from which the existence and life of all things hang. If, then, it is first immortal, not by sharing, but is, as it were, immortality and everlastingness, it will be life, having the "always" from itself, scattering the power of everlastingness in abundance and reaching it out to other things, as far as each is by nature fit to receive it. For the immortal is in life and exists together with life. So Socrates in the Phaedo, after many beautiful proofs of the soul's immortality, says: "God, then, my dear Cebes, and the Form of life itself, are far more immortal." So intelligible life, and the god who holds this life together, first have the immortal, and are the source of the whole of everlastingness. And this is eternity. So eternity has its existence in life, and will be set in the middle of the intelligible order.
!flag The dæmoniacal Aristotle | "Daemonic" as high praise: Aristotle, On the Heavens I.9 (279a), calls eternity (aiōn) "immortal and divine", from which "being and life" depend for all things.
!note The Phaedo 106d: "god, I think, and the Form of life itself, and whatever else is immortal, would be agreed by all never to perish". Taylor's footnote corrects "Phaedrus" to "Phaedo" in the Greek he translated.

@p
Further, we must say that intelligible eternity is one of three things: it exists either by being, or by life, or by intelligible intellect. But being, as the Stranger from Elea says, by its own nature neither stands still nor is moved. For if being is being to all things, and being in the strict sense is of this kind, much more must this be so with intelligible being and what is first being; for they are nothing else than being only. But being unfolds motion and rest, and the other kinds of beings, in its own second and third processions. The first being, then, as we have said, is at once set apart from motion and rest. But eternity, according to Timaeus, remains in one. So too time, in its motion, imitates the intelligible remaining of eternity. Eternity, then, does not exist by what is first being. Nor does it exist by intelligible intellect. For neither is soul time, though it is moved through the whole of time. And in short, among divine beings what is shared is everywhere set above what shares. But the eternal shares in eternity, as the temporal shares in time. So eternity is before intelligible intellect and after being; so that it is set in the middle of the intelligible breadth. And as the living being itself is eternal, so eternity is what always is. For as the living being itself shares in eternity, so eternity shares in being, and is the cause of existence, of everlasting life and thinking, and measures the being, powers and activities of all things.
!note The Sophist 250c; Timaeus 37d: time is "a moving image of eternity, which remains in one". Taylor's footnote supplies a missing clause ("nor by intelligible intellect") in the Greek he translated.

@ch III.17 | The one in which eternity remains is the first triad
Timaeus says that eternity "remains in one". This one is not the unshared One, which nothing can be in, nor eternity's own one, for then it would remain in itself; it is the first intelligible triad, the cause of stability to all beings.

@p
Since eternity exists by the middle centre of intelligibles, and the living being itself by their end, the brightest of what is intelligible, what is the first of intelligibles, and how does Timaeus name it? He says of eternity that while it remains in one, time proceeds by number; and that by motion time sketches the remaining of eternity, and by number its stable union. What, then, is that one in which Timaeus says eternity remains? We must say either that it is the one of eternity, or the One that goes beyond all intelligibles, or the one of the first triad. If we say it is the unshared One, how can anything remain in what is set apart from all things, and admits neither relation nor sharing of second natures with itself? For everything that remains in something is in a way held on all sides by what we say it remains in. But it is wholly impossible for the first One either to hold any being or to be ranked with beings. If anyone supposes that it is the one of eternity in which Timaeus says eternity remains, then eternity will be in itself. Yet it must remain in itself by having its existence in remaining in what is before itself. For to remain in what is before is better than the setting of things in themselves, as that is more perfect than placing the better in less excellent natures. If, then, eternity remains in itself, to what shall we first give remaining in what is before itself? For this, being more divine, must have its generation before what is lower than it. If, then, eternity can remain neither in itself nor in the One before beings, it is clear that, remaining in one according to Timaeus, it is set in the one of the first triad, or rather in the whole of that triad. For, as we have said before, the first triad is the cause of stability to all beings, as the middle triad is the cause of their procession, and the third triad of their return to their principle.
!note Timaeus 37d: the maker made "an eternal image moving by number of eternity remaining in one", which we call time.

@ch III.18 | The three intelligible orders in the Timaeus
Timaeus gives the three intelligible orders as the one, eternity, and the living being itself. Eternity is limit and the unlimited together, a henad and power, the cause of unfailing life; Plotinus rightly defines it as unlimited life, all at once. The living being itself is only-begotten by limit, eternal by power, and all-perfect by intellect, and is the most beautiful of intelligibles, since beauty first shines out with the Forms.

@p
Again, then, three orders of intelligibles appear to us, according to Timaeus' teaching: the living being itself, eternity, and the one. Through this one, and its firm setting in it, eternity has fixed the intelligible kingdom; and through eternity the living being itself defines the boundary of the intelligible gods, by an everlasting and unchanging sameness. The living being itself, having proceeded as a tetrad, hangs from the dyad in eternity. For eternity is the "always" together with being. And the dyad in eternity shares in the intelligible monad, which Timaeus for this reason calls one, as the monad and principle of all the intelligible breadth. For indeed he rightly calls the first triad one, because it is most of all marked by limit, naming it from limit. The middle triad he calls, in a twofold way, eternity, joining the names, because this triad is defined by intelligible power. And the third triad he calls the living being itself, carrying the name over to the whole of it from the end of the triad. The first triad, then, is the union of all the intelligibles, being in a way ranked with them; for this union is different from the one set apart from intelligibles and unshared. It is also the giver of stable power, for all things are set firm because of it. But eternity is first being, and what is first set firm. So of the remaining of the whole of things, we say the first triad is that because of which this remaining comes about, and the second triad that by which it is produced. For the firm setting of beings is by this second triad, but because of the first. The second triad is the nearest measure of all beings, and is ranked with the things measured. In it at one and the same time are limit and the unlimited: limit, so far as it measures intelligibles; the unlimited, so far as it is the cause of everlastingness and the "always". For according to the Oracle, eternity is the cause of unfailing life, untiring power and unsluggish activity. Yet eternity is marked more by the unlimited than by limit, for it holds in itself unlimited time. Time has limit and the unlimited in a divided way: by its continuity it is unlimited, but by the now it is limited, for the now is a limit. But eternity sets limit and the unlimited in the same. For it is a henad and power: by the one it is limit, by power unlimited; which time too shows, as from images, because the middle triad of intelligibles has limit, the unlimited and the mixed. For from where does time get its limit, except from eternal limit? For the temporal limit too is undivided, as the limit of eternity is one; and the undivided is the image of the one. And from where does the unlimitedness of time's continuity come, except from the power of the unlimited? But the one is a stable unlimitedness, the other a moved one; and as the one stands still by the one, so the other is moved by number. For from where does time's kinship with lives come, except from the first principle of life, eternity? And time proceeds through all life in time.
!flag eternity is the cause of never-failing life, of unwearied power, and unsluggish energy | From the Chaldean Oracles (frag. 49 and 59 are related), where Aion is a god who gives unfailing life and power. Taylor's footnote supplies "time" in the Greek he translated.
!note "Eternity remaining in one": Timaeus 37d. The "now" as the limit of time: Aristotle, Physics IV.11 (220a).

@p
Again, then, it is clear from these things that eternity exists by the middle of the intelligible gods. For here is unlimited life, the cause of all life, intellectual, of soul, and that which exists divided in bodies. Eternity is the father and giver of unlimited life, since eternity is also the cause of all immortality and everlastingness. And Plotinus, showing in a most divinely inspired way the character of eternity according to Plato's theology, defines it as unlimited life, unfolding into light at once the whole of itself and its own being. For it sets its life in the intelligible centre: through the one it measures its being and fixes it in what is before itself, but through power makes it unlimited; it unfolds the one-like transcendence of the first triad, but defines the end of the gods, and stretches from the middle on all sides to all the intelligible breadth. Moreover, the third triad is filled with intelligible life, and so is an intelligible living being, and the first living being. For it first shares in the whole nature of this life, and brings into light in itself the first of the Forms, to which the making intellect, reaching out, sets up the whole world; and it is itself the intelligible universe, as the visible world is the perceptible universe. So Plato calls the living being itself all-perfect. Or rather, if you are willing, let us speak this way: in this third triad there are limit, the unlimited, and the mixed, which we have called intelligible intellect. So the whole triad is called only-begotten from the father in it. For the cause of limit gives what is unranked with other things and a set-apart transcendence. For what holds, says Timaeus, all the intelligible living beings will never be second with another; for again there would need to be another living being about it. So what holds in one all intelligible living beings is a whole. But everywhere the whole is referred to limit, and parts to the unlimited. So if the living being itself is only-begotten for this reason, it will have this character by limit. But again it is called eternal by its power. For this power belongs most of all to the eternal; for eternity is unlimited power remaining in one and proceeding stably. The living being itself is all-perfect by intellect. For what unfolds in itself all the intelligible separation of being is intelligible intellect; and that intellect, by Plato's decision, will be all-perfect which holds all intelligibles and defines the boundary of the intelligible order. The only-begotten, then, the eternal, the all-perfect, limit, the unlimited and the mixed show the nature of the intelligible living being. So Timaeus too reminds us of the pattern in these three conclusions: in the one that shows that the universe is only-begotten, in the generation of time, and in the all-perfect holding of all living beings.
!note Plotinus III.7.3-5: eternity is "the life that belongs to what is, in being, all together and full, without extension", "unlimited life". Timaeus 31a-b: "what holds all intelligible living beings would never be second with another". Taylor's footnotes correct the Greek he translated in three places ("character" for "everlastingness", "life", and "towards" for "before").

@p
If Timaeus also says that the living being itself is the most beautiful of all intelligibles, and it has the third order among intelligibles, this will not be surprising. For we said before that everywhere the cause of the best mixture is the triad of proportion, truth and beauty. But beauty shines out chiefly in the third procession of being, and shows its shining nature together with intelligible Forms, as truth shines out in the second, and proportion in the first procession of being. If, however, truth is first, beauty second, and proportion third, it is not at all surprising that in order truth and beauty should come before proportion, but that proportion, being plainer in the first triad than the other two, should shine out third in the second processions. For these three exist in a hidden way in the first triad. Truth, so far as it is intelligible knowledge, is in the second triad; beauty, so far as it is the Form of Forms, is in the third. That this triad exists there first is clear from this: truth is first in what is most of all being, before knowledge; beauty, which reaches as far as the last of beings, must be in the first being, from which the last beings come; and the first proportion is in what is first mixed. For every mixture needs proportion, so that what is produced from it may be one thing. Though these three, then, exist beforehand there (for we take as agreed by all that proportion is there, and the most beautiful of intelligible living beings, as Timaeus says), we will put aside further consideration of them for now, since we have discussed them elsewhere, and tried especially to establish what we take to be Plato's opinion of their order. For we have spoken of these things in a treatise of one book, in which we show that truth is ranked with the philosopher, beauty with the lover, and proportion with the musician; and that as the order of these lives is, so too is the relation of truth, beauty and proportion to each other.
!flag If, however, truth is indeed the first, beauty the second, and symmetry the third | In the Philebus (65a-66b) the order of goods is measure, proportion and beauty, then truth (intellect); Proclus means here the order in the third mixture's list, where truth comes before beauty and proportion.
!note The Phaedrus 248d: the first soul becomes "a philosopher, a lover of beauty, or one devoted to the Muses and to love". Proclus' one-book treatise on these three lives is lost.

@p
The living being itself, then, may most justly be called most beautiful, so far as it is held, in an eminent way, in intelligible beauty. For beauty is wont to ride on Forms, and is, as it were, the Form of Forms, unfolding what is hidden in the Good, making its loveliness shine out, and drawing to its own splendour the desire hidden about it. To the Good all things have a silent and secret leaning; but we are roused to the beautiful with wonder and motion. For its light and working pierce keenly through every soul, and, as the most like the Good of all things, it turns back every soul that looks at it. The soul, seeing what is secret shining out, as it were, to view, rejoices in what it sees, admires it, and is struck with wonder. And as in the holiest of the mysteries, before the mystic visions, the initiates are seized with wonder, so among intelligibles, before sharing in the Good, beauty shining out strikes those who see it with wonder, turns the soul to itself, and, set in the vestibule of the Good, shows what is in the inner shrine, and what the transcendence of hidden good is. Through these things, then, let it be clear where beauty begins and how it first shines out, and that the living being itself is the most beautiful of all intelligibles.
!note The Phaedrus 250c-e: beauty alone "has this lot, to be the most visible and the most lovely". The vestibule of the Good: the Philebus 64c.

@ch III.19 | The intelligible Forms: the four kinds of living being
In the living being itself the first patterns appear, four of them, and with them the kinds of being, hidden. The four answer to the principles: the Form of the heavenly gods to the One; of the gods of the air to limit; of the gods of the water to the unlimited; and of the gods of the earth to the mixed. This triad is the first cause of all making.

@p
Timaeus says that the first and intelligible patterns exist in the intelligible living being, and that they are four, coming first into light by the all-perfect tetrad. This being so, we must first consider that, as Forms appear in the intelligible, much more must the kinds of beings exist beforehand among intelligibles. For we cannot grant that the Forms are intelligible, but the kinds only intellectual. But as the Forms exist intelligibly in their first existence, while their fullness shines out in the intellectual gods, divides what is universal into more partial lessenings, brings the one-like out into multitude, and spreads what is set apart into ranked causes, so too the kinds of being are in the intelligibles hidden and undivided, but in the intellectual gods with separation. So the first triad has being as its mixed; the second has life, where there were motion and rest, life both remaining and proceeding; and in the third there are sameness and difference. For the all-perfect multitude is through intelligible difference, and what is united and holds the parts in common, by kinds and by one, is through intelligible sameness. And all these exist in these triads intelligibly, in the manner of being, and in a one-like way.
!note Timaeus 39e-40a: "as many Forms as intellect sees in the living being that is, so many and such the world must have: and they are four". The "kinds of being" are the Sophist's five great kinds (being, rest, motion, sameness, difference). Taylor's footnote corrects "intelligibles" to "intellectuals" in the Greek he translated.

@p
First, then, this should be inferred by those who love to look at the nature of things: they should give the intelligible Forms kinds ranked with them. For it neither was nor will be lawful for the kinds to shine out after the Forms. So much more must those who grant that there are intelligible Forms grant that the kinds exist in the intelligible in the way described. Next, besides this, we must see how this tetrad of Forms exists, and how it shines out in intelligible intellect, answering to the principles. It is divided into a monad and a triad. For so far as the Form of the heavenly gods is set before the others, it is defined by a divine cause. It seems to me that intelligible intellect, returning to the principles of the whole of things by its turning back, becomes the fullness of Forms, and is all things intellectually and at the same time intelligibly, holding in itself the causes of beings; and, full of the inexpressible and set-apart cause of all things, it sets up the monad of the gods; which is why, I think, Plato calls it the Form of the gods. Receiving the intellectual causes of the three principles after the One, it shows three Forms after this. One is the cause of the living beings that wander and fly in the air, proceeding in answer to limit. So it sets up gods who are one-like, uplifting and undefiled, united to the heavenly gods, receiving measures second in dignity to theirs, and having the same relation to the gods who govern coming to be, ranked with these, as the heavenly gods have to them by set-apart transcendence. It shows the cause of the gods of the water, ranked with generating and unlimited power, which produces gods who give motion and fertile abundance, and watch over life; since this perceptible water too is ruled by pouring out, unlimited flow and indefiniteness, and so is given to the life-giving powers. And intelligible intellect shows the prior cause of the gods of the earth, who walk on it, in a way suited to the nature of the mixed. It generates gods who hold the end of the whole of things, who are stable, who master the formless nature of matter by the last Forms, and fix the seat of the natures in the world in the one centre of the universe. For, having their existence, as it were, from the first Hestia, the seat of beings, they stably define this seat in the world. So the Forms first come into light in intelligible intellect, having their procession and order by the first principles. Third, besides these things, we must infer, following Timaeus, that by this triad the multitude of intelligible parts shines out, and the whole is divided into an all-perfect order of parts. For that, he says, of which the other intelligible living beings, one by one and by kinds, are parts, is the first and most beautiful pattern of the universe. But if the other intelligible living beings are parts of it, clearly it is a whole, holding in itself the multitude of intelligible parts, and binding all intelligible parts together. We must infer, then, that this triad is the first cause of production and making. For if it holds the first patterns of things, clearly the ordered distribution of second natures begins from it. And if it is a living being that sets up all living beings, every extent of soul and all the extent of bodies proceed from there; and it will also hold the intelligible causes of all the life-giving and making orders.
!flag the cause of air-wandering and volant animals | Timaeus 40a: the four kinds are the heavenly race of gods, the winged kind "that travels in the air", the kind that lives in water, and the kind "that walks on land". Proclus takes them as kinds of gods first, then of daemons and mortals.
!note Timaeus 30c-d: the pattern is "that of which the other living beings, one by one and by kinds, are parts". Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@ch III.20 | The Sophist: the one being, the whole and the all
Against Parmenides' "all is one", the Stranger separates the One from what shares in it in three steps: the one being is not the One; the whole, having parts, is not the One; and the all, divided into many parts, is not the One. These answer to the three intelligible triads: the one being to the first, the whole (eternity) to the second, the all (the living being itself) to the third.

@p
Such ideas, then, may be taken from what is written in the Timaeus about the three intelligible triads, in agreement with what is said of them in the Philebus, seeing in each limit, the unlimited and the mixed. If you are willing, we will also show from what is scattered in the Sophist that Plato had the same idea as we have about the first principles. The Stranger from Elea in that dialogue, raising doubts against Parmenides' claim that the universe is one, unfolds intelligible multitude and shows how it hangs from the One. First he argues from the one being (being marked by the one), and reminds us that this is acted on by the One and shares in the One, but is not the One itself, nor what is first one. Afterwards he brings out the idea of the distinction between the unshared One and being from the whole. For if the one being is a whole, as Parmenides testifies, and what is a whole has parts, and what has parts is not the One itself, the one being will not be the same as the One. Third, he argues from the all-perfect. For what is wholly divided and binds many parts together can never exist in the same way as what is wholly one. Having gone this far, he shows that what is free of multitude is by its own nature set apart from the one being, proceeding in the proof of this through three arguments, beginning at one time from the one being, at another from the whole, and at another from the all. It is better, though, to hear Plato's own words. That the One is not the same as the one being he proves through these words: "What about those who say the universe is one? Must we not ask them, as far as we can, what they say being is? Certainly. Let them answer this: Do you say that only one thing is? We do. Or will they not say so? They will. What then? Do you call something being? Yes. Is it what you call one, using two names for the same thing? Or how? What will be their answer after this, Stranger?" Through this, then, Plato, separating the One and being from each other, and showing that the idea of the One differs from that of being, and that they are not the same, shows that the most proper and first One is set apart from the one being. For the one being does not remain purely in a hyparxis free of multitude and having the form of the one. But the One itself is set apart from every addition; for by whatever you add to it, you lessen its highest and inexpressible union. So we must set the One before the one being, and hang the one being from what is one alone. For if the One and the one being were the same, and it made no difference to say one and being (since if they differed, the One would again differ from the one being); if, then, the One differs in no way from the one being, all things will be one, there will be no multitude among beings, and it will not be possible to name things, lest there be two things, the thing and the name. For being set apart from all multitude and all division, there will be neither a name of anything nor any speech about it; the name will appear to be the same as the thing. And a name will not be the name of a thing, but the name of a name, if the thing is the same as the name and the name the same as the thing; and the thing will be the thing of a thing. For all things will be about the thing as about the name, through the union of thing and name. If, then, these things are absurd, and the One is, and being too, and being shares in the One, the One and the one being are not the same.
!note The Sophist 244b-d: if only one thing is, then "being" and "one" are two names for one thing, and even having a name is absurd. Taylor's footnote corrects the order of "name" and "thing" in the Greek he translated.

@p
That the whole too is not the same as the One, Plato shows afterwards in the same dialogue, beginning thus: "What then? Will they say that the whole is different from the one being, or the same as it? Of course they will say, and do say, it is the same. If, then, it is a whole, as Parmenides says, 'like the mass of a well-rounded sphere, equally balanced every way from the middle; for it must be neither greater nor less here or there', then being, being such, has a middle and extremes; and having these, it must surely have parts. Or how shall we say? Just so. Yet nothing prevents what is divided into parts from having the property of one in all its parts, and being so, as all and whole, one. Of course. But is it not impossible for what has these properties to be the One itself? Why? Because what is truly one, by right reasoning, must be said to be wholly without parts. It must. But such a thing as this, being made of many parts, will not agree with that account." Through these things, then, the Stranger, arguing from wholeness after the one being, and from the division of the parts of wholeness, shows that the all is not one. For if the whole is among beings, as Parmenides testifies in his verses that it is, all things will not be the One. For the One is without parts, but the whole has parts. So the whole is not the One itself; for that goes beyond all things and wholeness, while the whole is acted on by the One. So it is called whole, for it is not the One itself. So all things are not one, without separation and multiplying.
!note The Sophist 244d-245b, quoting Parmenides frag. 8.43-45.

@p
Moreover, the all holds many parts. For the whole is at first made of two parts; but the all has a multitude of parts, and, sharing in wholeness, is at the same time all, as wholly distributed into parts. This, then, is not the One itself, but is acted on by the One. For the One itself is without parts, and without parts in such a way as to be set apart from all parts. So the all is not the same as the One. We have divided the whole and the all, but Plato joins them, when he says: "Nothing prevents what is divided into parts from having the property of one in all its parts, and being so, as all and whole, one." At the same time, though, they are divided in the way described. Through these three arguments, then, the Stranger from Elea separates the One from what shares in the One, and raises doubts against those who say all things are one: the one being, the whole and the all; of which the all shares in the whole, and is a self-perfect multitude made of many parts, and the whole shares in being. For being is not the whole, as Parmenides testifies. These having such an order, must not Plato's arguments be made in keeping with the three intelligible triads? For since Parmenides defined the one being among intelligibles, Plato had to draw from there his proofs of the distinction between the One before intelligibles and the one among intelligibles. For the doubts raised against Parmenides show in many places that the shared one has its existence from the unshared union. So the One is not in these triads, but the one being and the whole. And the all is clearly at the end of the intelligible order, for what is in every way perfect, and all intelligible multitude, exist at that end. The whole is in the middle centre, the bond of the intelligible breadth. For the whole is fit to exist before the all, since the all is a whole, but the whole is not necessarily all. For the all is divided multitude; but what holds multitude in itself, not yet separated, is the whole. And this belongs most of all to eternity. For eternity is the measure of all intelligible multitude, as the whole is the holding together and union of the all. And the one being is in the first triad. For the One is most of all the character of this triad, as Timaeus too has shown; and being, which is being in a hidden and intelligible way, the cause of being to all other things, first shines out there. Again, then, following the Stranger from Elea, three triads appear to us: the first by the one being, the second by the whole, and the third by the all. Looking to these, the Craftsman of the universe adorns the perceptible universe, defining the visible nature by that intelligible all, and time by the intelligible wholeness; which is why time too is continuous. And as the intelligible whole holds two parts but contains them in one boundary, so time too is bounded by the now, but by its twofold parts is unlimited. These things we will discuss more fully shortly, when we speak of the Parmenides. For the Stranger's ideas are the preliminary rites of the mysteries of the Parmenides. Before we turn to the Parmenides, though, let us discuss, if you agree, the three triads from the beginning, gathering Plato's idea from his sayings scattered in many places.
!flag the proteleia of the mysteries of the Parmenides | The proteleia were the preliminary rites before an initiation; the Sophist prepares for the Parmenides as these did for the mysteries.
!note Taylor's footnotes correct "intellectual" to "intelligible" in the Greek he translated, and supply "the One".

@ch III.21 | Father, power and intellect in the intelligible triads
In each intelligible triad, limit is the father, the unlimited power, and the mixed intellect. Plato calls the first God father, the Sophist calls being power, and the Craftsman of the Timaeus calls himself father, speaks of his power, and is intellect. Being is wider than eternity, and eternity than intellect. The Phaedrus' good, wise and beautiful also divide the three triads.

@p
There are three triads, then, as we have often said, and they are divided in this way into limit, the unlimited and the mixed. So there are threefold intelligible limits, threefold unlimiteds, and threefold mixtures. But of every intelligible triad, the limit in each is called father, the unlimited power, and the mixed intellect. Let no one think these names foreign to Plato's philosophy. For it will appear that he uses these names in these triads more than anyone. He calls the first God father and lord in his Letters. It is clear, though, that as the first God goes beyond even the fatherly order, the first fatherly order is in the intelligible gods. For these are most of all akin to the One, and unfold his inexpressible and unknown union intelligibly. If, then, the first God is called one and father from the natures that proceed next from him, then, as the intelligible gods are first henads, so too they are first fathers. For Plato names the inexpressible in two ways, either from the summits of beings or from all beings; for through these the transcendence of the One is known. Moreover, the Stranger from Elea calls being what has power, and power. The first power, then, exists before being and is united to the father; but it agrees most with being, which it also fills. So being, as sharing in power, is called powerful; but as united to it, and producing all beings by it, it is called power. And if both Plato himself and his truest disciples often call all true beings intellect (which is why in many places they make three principles, the Good, intellect and soul, calling every true being intellect), you will have the third in these too as intellect. But we must not fail to see the difference. One kind of intellect is so in relation to hyparxis. For when we call the henad in each triad intelligible, as the object of desire of being and as filling being, then we call the third in the triad intellect. For it is intelligible as being and intellect, not as the intellect of being, but of the father and godhead. For every shared godhead is intelligible, as the fullness of what shares in it. Another kind is the intellect of being, by which we say that the being of the third triad is the intellect of what is first being. For this is intellect of being, having its own being by its very activity. For all things are in it in the manner of being, both the simpler kinds and the first patterns; for it is intelligible intellect. And the third kind is intellectual intellect, which exists answering to intelligible intellect, is joined to it and filled from it, having intellectually what is in the other intelligibly. In short, everywhere the first in each series must have the form of what is before them. So they are called first, and have a certain transcendence of being over the natures ranked with them. Since what is before intelligibles is God, the first intelligibles are gods and henads. Since the intelligible is of being, the first intellects are beings. Since intellect everywhere is intellectual by its own nature, the first souls are intellectual. Since souls are the fullnesses of life, the first of bodies are most alive. And since everlasting bodies are moved in a circle, the summits of material bodies are moved together with everlasting bodies. This, then, is why the henads are often called intelligibles, and beings intelligible intellects.
!note The Sixth Letter 323d: "the god who leads all things present and to come, and the lord and father of the leader and cause". The Sophist 247e: being is power. "Father, power and intellect" is a Chaldean triad (Oracles frag. 3-4). Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
That Plato knew this triad, father, power and intellect, we will learn by looking at the making order. For in it the triad appears most remarkably. Because of its union with the intelligible it is filled with this triad, and has these things in a more divided way than the living being itself or intelligible eternity. At the very beginning of the making, in the Timaeus, the Craftsman calls himself father: "Of which works I am the maker and father." Shortly after he unfolds his power: "imitating my power in your generation." And this is remarkable, that he has given us the most theological idea of power. First, he calls it the power of the father, when he says "of which works I am the maker and father"; and that the power is his is clear from "imitating my power", so that for Plato power is the father's. Next, he gives this power a character that generates the whole of things, as is clear from "in your generation". Power, then, is the cause of generation and of the procession of beings. And last, he gives the intellectual character of the Craftsman: "Having said this, he poured again into the former bowl, in which he had mixed the soul of the universe, mixing the remains of the former mixture." For to pour, to mix, mixture, and producing soul belong to intellect. But what need is there to say this, since before this he calls the Craftsman intellect: "Whatever Forms intellect, by discursive reason, saw in the living being itself, such and so many he thought this universe must hold." So the Craftsman is father, power and intellect, and he has these as far as possible because of the intelligibles. He is a god as father because of them; he is also power and the generator of wholes, and knows beings intellectually, because of them. For in them intelligible knowledge first exists. Much more, then, are father, power and intellect in the intelligibles; filled from them, the Craftsman too shares in this triad. For Plato takes each of these by analogy. As the fatherly triad among intelligibles gives existence to intelligible eternity, so the Craftsman makes the works of which he is father indissoluble. And as among intelligibles eternity, proceeding by all power, generates the intelligible living being itself, so the making power gives existence to everlasting and divine living beings in the world, and gives the young gods another power, which generates mortal living beings. That anyone can take these names from Plato is clear from what has been said.
!note Timaeus 41a ("gods of gods, of which works I am maker and father"), 41c ("imitating my power in your generation"), 41d (the second mixing in the bowl), 39e ("as many Forms as intellect sees in the living being").

@p
Since being has a threefold reality among intelligibles, one is first being, before the eternal; another is second being and the first eternity; and another is being last, and is intelligible and eternal intellect. Here there is being, there eternity, and there intellect. And eternity is wider than intellect, and being than eternity. For every intellect is eternal, but not everything eternal is intellect; for soul in its being is eternal. And everything that shares in eternity shares far before that in being; for existence always goes together with everlasting existence. But what shares in existence is not always eternally being; for bodies too share in a way in the nature of existence, but they are not eternal. Intellect, then, as intellect, sets up only intellectual being, since as life and being too it sets up all things. Eternity sets up both intellectual and soul's being, for the mixture in the second triad was intelligible life. And being sets up the intellectual, the soul's and the bodily life. For matter too is being, most dimly: it is potential, a formless being and non-being, falling away from sharing in being. If someone says it is being in potential, still it has this power from being. For potential is the forerunning share of act. So much, then, for these things.

@p
What sufficient means of division does Socrates give us in the Phaedrus concerning these intelligible triads? And how, from what he says, can we go back to the idea of the reality of the most ruling gods? Socrates in that dialogue, inspired by the Nymphs, praises everything divine as beautiful, wise and good, and says that the soul is nourished by these. But if everything divine is of this kind, much more is the intelligible. All these are everywhere, but in the first triad the good chiefly exists, in the second the wise, and in the third the beautiful. For in this is the most beautiful of intelligibles. In the second triad are truth and the first thinking. And in the first is the well-proportioned, which we say is the same as the good. But Socrates in the Philebus says the marks of the good are the desirable, the sufficient and the perfect. The desirable belongs to limit, for it is the union and goodness of the whole triad, and the triad converges about it. The sufficient belongs to the unlimited, for sufficiency is a power able to reach all things and be present to all things without hindrance. And the perfect belongs to the mixed, for this is what is first threefold, since every mixture has its coming together from the triad. The marks of the good, then, unfold to us the first triad, and the marks of intelligible wisdom the second. For everything wise is full of being, generates truth, and turns imperfect natures back to their perfection. The full belongs to the second limit, for this is filled in a one-like way by sharing in the natures before it. For the full everywhere fits limit, as what cannot be filled fits the unlimited. The fertile belongs to the second power and the unlimited. For what does not remain in its own fullness but is fertile and generative of other things shows most of all the divine unlimited. And the turning-back belongs to the mixed. For this, having the end of the triad, turns everything imperfect back to the full, and unites itself before other things to the limit of the whole triad.
!flag being inspired by the Nymphs | In the Phaedrus (238c-d, 241e) Socrates says he is possessed by the Nymphs of the place where he speaks.
!note The Phaedrus 246e; the Philebus 20d; and Book I, chapters 21-23, on goodness and wisdom.

@ch III.22 | The marks of beauty: the third triad
The marks of beauty belong to the third triad: the lovely answers to limit, the delicate to the unlimited, and the bright to intellect. Beauty is hidden at the end of the first triad and shines out threefold in the third. So intelligible intellect loves the first triad, and from here intellectual love proceeds, with faith and truth.

@p
Moreover, the marks of beauty are the distinctive characters of the third intelligible triad. These are, as we said before, the lovely, the delicate and the bright. The lovely, set in answer to the desirable, belongs to limit. The delicate, ranked with the sufficient, belongs to the unlimited power in the beautiful. And the bright is of intellectual character. For it is the beautiful of beauty, lights up all things, and strikes with wonder those able to see it. And as visible beauty, shining most clearly, is seen through the clearest of the senses (for the objects of this sense have many differences, according to Aristotle, and this sense reaches further than the rest), so intelligible beauty appears to the intellect of the soul, shining intelligibly.
