For it is an intelligible Form. And for this reason the brightness of beauty is plain to intellect. Bright beauty, then, as Socrates calls it, shines out at the end of the intelligible order. For this is the brightest of intelligibles, is intelligible intellect, and sends out the intelligible light that, when it appeared, struck the intellectual gods with wonder and made them admire their father, as Orpheus says. Such, then, is the preparation for the science of the intelligible gods that may be taken from these things. And now it will be clear how beauty is hidden at the end of the first intelligible triad, but exists in the third triad as having come plainly into light. For in the first it exists in one form only, in the third in a threefold way. It is also clear how each of the triads is at one and the same time a monad and a triad. For the first triad, marked by the good, is completed by the three marks of the good. The second, marked by the wise, is held in the triad of wisdom. And the third, existing by the beautiful, is all-perfect through the triad of beauty. If, then, the beautiful is hidden in the first triad and shines out threefold in the third, it is clear that intelligible intellect loves the first triad, and has love joined to its beauty. And this is the intelligible love of the first beauty. From these, then, intellectual love proceeds, together with faith and truth, as we said before. For the three intelligible monads, the good, the wise and the beautiful, set up three powers that lead upward all other things, and before other things the intellectual gods. Of these things, though, we will speak later.
!flag that when it appeared astonished the intellectual Gods, and made them admire their father, as Orpheus says | From the Orphic theogony: when Phanes, the first-born, appeared, the gods marvelled at the light; Proclus sets Phanes at the end of the intelligible order.
!note Aristotle, Metaphysics I.1 (980a): we love sight above the senses because it shows many differences between things. The Phaedrus 250b-d, on bright beauty, and Book I, chapter 24.

@ch III.23 | The second hypothesis of the Parmenides unfolds the gods
The second hypothesis is the procession of the gods from the one being down to being made divine. Not the first, as some say: the first denies even being and the one of its subject, which fits no god but the first; and the intelligible gods are affirmed in the second, not denied in the first. The conclusions answer in order to the divine orders. The first intelligible triad is the one, power and being.

@p
Let us now turn our attention to the study of the Parmenides. But I want to remind the reader again of what we have shown before. It has been shown that the second hypothesis must be divided into the whole processions of the one being, and that this hypothesis is nothing else than the generation and procession of the gods, proceeding from on high, from the highest union of intelligibles, as far as being made divine. For the discussion is not, as some say, about God and the gods in the first hypothesis. For it was not lawful for Parmenides to join multitude with the One and the One with multitude, since the first God is wholly set apart from the whole of things. And in the first hypothesis being, and even the One itself, are taken away from the first God; and that such a taking away does not fit the other gods is plain to everyone. Nor, again, does Parmenides in the first hypothesis speak of the intelligible gods, as some say. They say that the negations are of these gods because they are joined to the One, and in simplicity and union go before all the divine kinds. For how can the like or the unlike, contact and lack of contact, and all the other things denied of the One, be in the intelligible gods? They seem to me right to say that the things taken away are likenesses of the gods, but not right to say that all of them are likenesses of the intelligible gods. Besides, against this claim, the discussion is about the intelligible gods again in the second hypothesis. For what is denied in the first is affirmed in the second. This, then, as I have said, has been shown: that the conclusions have, in relation to each other, the order of before and after, of causes and effects. Proceeding from the beginning, then, we must fit the first conclusions to the first orders, the middle conclusions to the middle orders, and the last to the last, and show that there are as many questions asked as there are processions of the divine orders. And first we must give Parmenides' teaching about the intelligible gods, of whom we have set out to speak, since Plato speaks of them in many places, partly pointing and partly unfolding his meaning clearly.
!flag some say it is, in the first hypothesis, concerning God and the Gods | Proclus' Commentary on the Parmenides (VI-VII) reviews earlier readings: some (perhaps Origen, or Amelius) took the first hypothesis to be about the first God and the gods, others about the intelligible gods. Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated.

@p
We must, though, gather into one the careful and synoptic study of each order, since it would not be fitting to repeat now the exposition we have given in our commentary on that dialogue. Taking each conclusion by itself, I will try to refer it to the fitting order of gods, following in this the divine inspirations of our leader Syrianus. For with his help we too, with a divine head, have followed these sacred paths in the study of the Parmenides, stirred with a divine frenzy and woken, as from a deep sleep, to this secret mystic teaching. So much for the way of the whole of the conclusions. From here I will pass to the account of what was set out.
!flag with a divine head pursued these sacred paths | A Homeric phrase ("with the head inspired"); Proclus means that he followed Syrianus as an initiate follows the guide, with the highest part of the soul.

@p
The first and unshared One, then, which exists beyond the whole of things, beyond not only the henads that share but those that are shared, is praised through the first hypothesis, shown to be the cause of all things inexpressibly, but not defined itself in any of all things, nor having any power or character akin to the other gods. After this unshared One comes what alone is beyond being and surpassing, unmixed with all hyparxis: a henad shared by being, setting up about itself the first being, and by the addition of this sharing becoming more than what is first one. This is a hyparxis beyond being, the hyparxis of the first intelligible triad. As there are these two in the first triad, the one and being, and the first generates and the second is generated, the first perfects and the second is perfected, the middle of both must be power, through which, and together with which, the one sets up and perfects being. For the procession of being from the one, and its return to the one, are through power. For what else joins being to the one, or makes the one shared by being, but power? For it is the procession of the one and its reaching out to being. So in all the divine kinds powers go before processions and generations. This triad, then, the one, power and being, is the summit of intelligibles: the first producing, the third produced, and the second hanging from the one but growing together with being.

@ch III.24 | The first intelligible triad in the Parmenides: the one being
The second hypothesis begins with "if the one is": the one shares in being and being in the one, and the relation between them is power, hidden. This is father, power and intellect in their first form, beyond rest and motion. The powers of the gods are beyond being, which is why the poets say the gods can do all things.

@p
This triad Parmenides gives at the very beginning of the second hypothesis, joining to the one the simplest sharing of being. He calls it the one being, and says that being shares in the one and the one in being. But their sharing is different. The one shares in being as lighting up, filling and making being divine; being shares in the one as hanging from the one and made divine by it. The relation that is the middle of both is not, with them, without being. For not even the relation among perceptible things is in no way being, much less the relation there. This relation is twofold: it belongs to the one and is born together with being. For it is the motion of the one and its procession into being. Parmenides gives this triad, beginning what he says about it thus: "See, then, from the beginning, if the one is, can it be and not share in being? It cannot." And he ends speaking of it with these words: "When someone says in brief that one is, will this be anything else than that the one shares in being? It will not." This, then, is the first intelligible triad: the one, being, and the relation of both, through which being is of the one and the one of being, in a wholly wonderful way; Plato pointing through these that the father is the father of intellect, and intellect the intellect of the father, and that power is hidden between the extremes. For godhead is the father of the triad, and being is the intellect of this godhead. Yet it is not intellect in the way we usually call the intellect of being. For every such intellect stands still and is moved, as the Stranger from Elea says; but what is first being neither stands still nor is moved, as he also teaches. The first triad, then, is called the one being, since power here is hidden. For the triad does not proceed from itself, but exists without separation and in a one-like way, defined first by divine union. So this is the first sharing in being, which shares in the one through power as the middle, which gathers together and separates both the one and being. And it is beyond being, but joined with being. So we must never think that all power is the offspring of being. For the powers of the gods are beyond being, and exist together with the henads of the gods themselves; and through this power the gods generate beings. Rightly, then, does poetry everywhere say that the gods can do all things. For powers of being cannot do all things, since they do not set up natures beyond being. The first triad, then, is unfolded to us by Parmenides through these things.
!flag the habitude which is the middle of both | Taylor's "habitude" is relation (schesis): the link between the one and being, which Proclus identifies with power.
!note The Parmenides 142b-c. "The gods can do all things": Odyssey X.306. Taylor's footnote corrects "one being" to "one" in the Greek he translated.

@ch III.25 | The second intelligible triad in the Parmenides: the whole
Next Parmenides shows that the one being is a whole with two parts, the one and being, held together by power, now more plainly. This is intelligible wholeness, eternity. Wholeness is threefold, before the parts, from the parts, and in a part, and the intelligible whole holds all three in a unified way.

@p
Right after this the second triad has its procession, which Parmenides marks by intelligible wholeness, as we showed in the Sophist. For the first triad, being one-like and having all things intelligibly and in a hidden way, hyparxis, power and being (so that power, the cause of division, existing between the one and being, is hidden, and appears through the sharing of the extremes with each other), the second triad proceeds, marked by the first intelligible power, and having the monads in itself distinguished from each other. For while all things are united and undistinguished in the first triad, distinction and separation shine out in this one. Being and power too are more divided from each other. And what is made of these is no longer the one being, but a whole, so that it has the one and being in itself as parts. For above, in the first triad, all things are before parts and wholeness; but in this triad there are both parts and a whole, power bringing itself into light. For since there is separation here, there are parts and the whole made of them. The second triad, then, is called intelligible wholeness. Its parts, the one and being, I call the extremes. And power, being the middle here, joins the one and being, and does not make them one as in the first triad. Since it is the middle of both, through its sharing with being it makes the one a one being, and through its sharing with the one it makes being wholly one. And so the one being is made of two parts: being marked by the one, and the one marked by being, as Parmenides himself says. He begins to speak of this triad thus: "Again, then, let us say, if one is, what will follow. Consider whether this hypothesis must not mean that the one is such as to have parts?" And he ends with these words: "What is one, then, is a whole, and has a part."
!note The Parmenides 142c-d.

@p
Through these things, then, Parmenides defines the second order of intelligibles as a wholeness. For as existence comes to all things from the first triad, so the whole from the second, and an all-perfect division from the third; but this we will consider later. Wholeness, then, is threefold, according to Plato's teaching: before the parts, made of parts, or in a part. In the Statesman he calls a genus a whole and a species a part, not that the genus is completed from the species, but that it exists before it. And in the Timaeus he says that the world is a whole of wholes. The whole world is completed from parts that are wholes, but each of the parts is a whole not as the universe is, but partially. Wholeness, then, being threefold according to Plato, as we have said, now is given the henad, the intelligible and hidden cause of these, holding and setting up in a unified way three wholenesses: by its own hyparxis, the wholeness before the parts; by its power, the wholeness made of parts; and by its being, the wholeness in a part. For the one is before all multitude; power shares in a way with both extremes and holds their characters in itself; and being in a way shares in the one. So the first of the wholenesses, before the parts, comes from a unified hyparxis. For it is a monad, and itself sets up the parts and the multitude in them. The second wholeness comes from power. For it is completed from parts, as in the power that gathers the one and being, the extremes in a way shine out to view. And the third wholeness comes from being. For being is a part, the offspring of both power and the one, and has each of these partially. After the intelligible, then, three wholenesses are divided among the different orders of beings. But the intelligible wholeness holds the three in a unified way, and is the monad that intelligibly binds this triad together, stretching its powers every way from the middle of the intelligible and hidden order.
!note The Statesman 262a-263b: a part is not the same as a species. Timaeus 33a: the world is "one whole of wholes". Taylor's footnote corrects "being" to "one" in the Greek he translated.

@ch III.26 | The third intelligible triad in the Parmenides: unlimited multitude
Next each part of the whole, the one and being, again has both parts, and so on without end: the one being is an unlimited multitude. This is the third triad, the first-born, where "coming to be" first appears. Its unlimitedness is not of quantity but of power: all multitude is offspring of the first unlimited, and this triad holds all intelligible multitude.

@p
Right after this triad we can see another proceeding, in which all intelligible multitude shines out, and which Parmenides makes a wholeness, but a wholeness made of many parts. For after the hidden union of the first triad and the twofold separation of the second, the procession of the third comes about, which has its existence from parts, but the parts are many, the multitude with which the triad before it is in labour. For in this triad there are a henad, power and being; but the one is multiplied, and so are being and power. And so the whole triad is a wholeness; but each of its extremes, the one and being, as a multitude joined through gathering power, is again divided and multiplied. For this power, joining unified multitude to the multitude of beings, makes some of these each, by procession, being marked by the one, and others each, by sharing, the one marked by being. For here there are two parts of the wholeness, the one and being; but the one shares in being, for it is joined with it, and being shares in the one. The one of being, then, is again divided, so that the one and being generate a second henad joined to the part of being. And being, sharing in the one, is again separated into being and the one. For it generates a more partial being hanging from a more partial henad. And being is made of more partial divinised beings, and is a more specific monad. The cause of this procession is power. For power makes two things, and works multitude. The one calls out into multitude, and being turns back to sharing in the divine henads. Where, then, does Parmenides begin to teach us about this triad, and where does he end his account of it? The beginning of what he says on it is this: "What then? Can each of these parts of the one being, the one and being, leave each other, so that the one is not a part of being, or being not a part of the one? It cannot." And he ends thus: "So will not the one being in this way be an unlimited multitude? It seems so."
!note The Parmenides 142d-143a. Taylor's footnote corrects the Greek of the quotation.

@p
First, then, we must understand the manner of procession of the divine kinds: that after the intelligible monad, which we rank by the one being, the dyad after it, which we call a wholeness, proceeds; that it is made of two parts separated by power; and that intelligible multitude appears from the monad and the dyad. For when all things are said to be parts of the one being, that is, second things, which appear through the separating cause of power, then Parmenides gives the union that runs from the monad to the third triad. But when power, separating and joining the henads and beings, completes multitude, then the sharing in the dyad becomes wholly plain, as I think Parmenides shows when he says "so that two must always come to be, and never one". This triad, then, proceeds by both the triads before it, flowing, according to the Oracle, and proceeding to all intelligible multitude. For unlimited multitude points to this flowing, and to the ungraspable nature of power. So, first, I have said that the reality of this triad is shown through these things to hang from the triads before it. And next I say that this triad, according to Parmenides, is first-born. For it first gives the power of coming to be; and Parmenides calls the multitude in it coming to be. For he says: "and the part will come to be from two parts at least". And again: "whatever part comes to be will always have these parts". And in what follows: "so that two must always come to be, and never one". Does not he, then, who so often uses the word coming to be in teaching the procession of intelligible multitude, proclaim that the natures before this order are more united to each other? But this order proceeds further, unfolds the hidden nature of the triads before it, and is first-born, unfolding fertile power in itself.
!flag flowing according to the Oracle | A Chaldean phrase for the procession of the intelligible into multitude.
!flag this triad, according to Parmenides, is primogenial | Taylor's "primogenial" is first-born (prōtogonos): Proclus links the third intelligible triad with Phanes, called Protogonos in the Orphic poems.

@p
Besides this, we must understand the unlimitedness of multitude not as those who take the unlimited in quantity see fit to speak of it. Since in the principles of the whole of things there are limit and the unlimited, the first the cause of union, the second of the separation of multitude, Parmenides calls the first and intelligible multitude unlimited, because all multitude, by its own nature, is unlimited, as the offspring of the first unlimited. All intelligible multitude is of this kind. For it is the first multitude, and multitude itself; and multitude itself is the first offspring of intelligible unlimitedness. Intelligible multitude, then, is unlimited for this reason, as bringing into light the first unlimited; and this unlimitedness is the same as the all-perfect. For what has proceeded to the all, and as far as an intelligible nature should proceed, through the power that generates the whole of things, is unlimited; for it cannot be held by anything else. But intelligible multitude holds all intelligible multitude. For if what is first unlimited were unlimited in quantity, we would have to grant that the intelligible is an unlimited multitude of this kind. But since the intelligible is unlimited power, what shares in the first unlimited must make unlimitedness shine out by the power that holds all prior natures. And if I must give my own opinion: as what is first one is first limit, so what is first multitude is unlimited multitude. For it receives the whole power of the unlimited, and, producing all henads and all beings down to the most individual natures, has unfailing power. So it is more universal than all multitude, and an unlimited that cannot be grasped. So, bringing all multitude into light, it bounds and measures it by unlimited power, and through wholeness brings limit to all things. These things, then, may be taken from Parmenides about the third intelligible triad.

@ch III.27 | The three triads in all three dialogues; why one Form makes gods, daemons and mortals
The first triad is the Timaeus' one, the Parmenides' one being, and the Philebus' limit, unlimited and mixed; the second, eternity and the first whole; the third, the living being itself, unlimited multitude, the intelligible spread into many beings. One hard question: how the same four Forms produce gods, daemons and mortals. Each Form leads a whole series; and Parmenides' parts, "one being" and "being that is one", divided again, give gods, angels, daemons and mortals.

@p
Next, let us speak in common of all the intelligible triads. The first triad, which is hidden and has the intelligible summit among intelligibles, Plato at one time, proceeding from the union in it and its set-apart transcendence over the other triads, calls one, as in the Timaeus: for eternity, he says, remains in one; and reason shows that this one is the first triad of intelligibles. At another time, proceeding from the extremes in it, what is shared and what shares, he calls it the one being, taking the power held in these as inexpressible, since it exists in a one-like and hidden way. And at another time he unfolds the whole of it by the monads in it, limit, the unlimited and the mixed: limit pointing to its divine hyparxis, the unlimited to its generative power, and the mixed to the being that proceeds from this power. Plato, then, as I have said, teaches us the first intelligible triad through these names, unfolding it to our view at one time through one name, at another through two, and at another through three. For there is a triad in it, by which the whole is marked; a dyad, by which the extremes share with each other; and a monad, which shows the inexpressible, hidden and unified nature of the first through its own monads.

@p
The second triad after this Plato calls eternity in the Timaeus, and the first wholeness in the Parmenides. How these have the same character we may learn by considering that everything eternal is a whole, if it is wholly eternal and has the whole of its being and activity present at once. Every intellect is of this kind, setting at once in itself the whole of its intellectual perception. It does not have one part of being and lack another, nor share in activity in part, but holds in sum the whole of being and the whole of thinking. If, though, it proceeded in time in its activities but had an eternal being, it would have the whole of its being, always stably the same, but would have its activity variably, acting differently at different times. Eternity, then, is everywhere the cause of wholeness to the natures to which it is first present. And the whole too everywhere holds everlastingness. For no whole leaves its being or its proper perfection; what first perishes and is spoiled is a partial nature. This is why the whole world is everlasting, because it is a whole; and so with all that the heavens hold, and with each of the elements. For everywhere wholeness holds together what underlies it. So eternity exists together with wholeness, and whole and eternity are the same. Each is a measure: the one of eternal things and all everlasting natures, the other of parts and all multitude. But since there are three wholenesses, one before parts, one from parts, and one in a part, through the wholeness before parts eternity measures the henads of divine natures set apart from beings; through the wholeness from parts, the henads ranked with beings; and through the wholeness in a part, all beings and whole beings. For these wholenesses, being parts of the divine henads, have in a divided way what exists beforehand in the henads in a unified way. Moreover, eternity is nothing else than the "always" shining out from the henad joined with being. And the whole is made of two parts, the one and being, power being what gathers the parts. By both these ideas, then, the dyad of the middle intelligible triad unfolds the one-like and hidden reality of the first triad.
!note Eternity as "always" (aei) and "being" (ōn): Aristotle, On the Heavens I.9 (279a); Plotinus III.7.4.

@p
Moreover, in the Timaeus Plato calls the third triad of intelligibles the living being itself, intelligible, all-perfect and only-begotten. In the Parmenides he calls it unlimited multitude, and a wholeness holding many parts. And in the Sophist he keeps calling it the intelligible distributed into many beings. All these sayings, then, are offspring of one science, and tend to one intelligible truth. For when Timaeus calls this triad the intelligible living being, he also says it is all-perfect, and holds intelligible living beings as its parts, one by one and by kinds. So the living being itself is, by this, a whole holding intelligible living beings as its parts. And Parmenides, when he shows that the one being is all-perfect multitude, shows that it exists together with this order. For the unlimited will be all-powerful and all-perfect, as we said before, holding in itself an intelligible multitude of parts, which it also generates, some more universal and others more partial, and, as Timaeus says, one by one and by kinds. Further, as Timaeus calls the living being itself eternal and only-begotten, so Parmenides first gives the "always" and "coming to be" to unlimited multitude, when he says: "And so, by the same reasoning, whatever part comes to be will always have these two parts; for the one will always hold being, and being the one; so that two must always come to be, and never one."
!note The Parmenides 142e-143a; the Sophist 245b-d, 246b-c.

@p
Who, then, so clearly reminds us of the eternal living being and the first-born triad as Parmenides, who first takes up, in this order, coming to be and the "always", and uses each of them so continually? The same thing, then, is an all-perfect living being and all-powerful intelligible multitude. For since the first unlimitedness is power, and every intelligible exists by it and receives from it a division into parts, I think it right to call it all-powerful, so avoiding the name "unlimited", which disturbs the multitude. But what in these things is hard to understand, and for which Plato most deserves admiration, we must not leave out, but show to the true lovers of truth. The intelligible living being holds four intelligible Forms, by which it sets up not only the kinds of gods but the higher kinds of beings after the gods, and mortal living beings themselves. For, generating, it stretches out the Form of the beings that wander in the air, the Form of those in the water, and the Form of those on the earth, from the gods down to mortal living beings. Since the living being itself, then, holds four Forms, and through the same patterns produces, as wholes, divine, daemonic and mortal living beings, this rightly raises a question for those who love the contemplation of truth: how, with the same causes and the same first patterns existing beforehand, some of the natures set up are gods, others daemons, and others mortal living beings. For if all these are generated in relation to one Form, how can they not have the same form and nature, since one Form must everywhere generate things of like form? For this is why we grant the hypothesis of Forms: so that the intelligible kind of gods may have and hold, before multitude, monads that produce like natures. This question, so hard, someone may solve logically, by saying that not all things that exist by one Form share the name in the same sense, nor share alike in their common cause, but some first and others last. For each Form leads a certain series, beginning from on high and sinking as far as the last of things. For according to the Oracle, all things begin from on high to stretch their wonderful rays down to the place below. So it will not be surprising that the same Form should exist beforehand as the cause of gods, daemons and mortal living beings, producing all things as wholes, and handing on the more partial separation of things to the making order, as this order hands on the production of individuals to the young gods. For intelligibles are the causes of whole series; intellectual gods, of divisions by common kinds; Forms above the world, of specific differences; and Forms in the world, of things that are now individuals. For they are causes that are moved, and lead their offspring into change.
!flag all things which subsist according to one form are not synonimous | Taylor's "synonimous" is Aristotle's term (Categories 1a): things that share both a name and its definition. Proclus means that gods, daemons and mortals share a Form's name but not in the same sense. Taylor's footnote corrects "unlimitedness" to "difficulty" in the Greek he translated.

@p
But if we must look at the thing itself by itself, and see how one intelligible Form is the cause of gods, daemons and mortals, Parmenides alone can satisfy us about the parts held in intelligible multitude. For he marks some things by being and others by the one. The one being is taken up by the one, but being that is one is rather taken up by being; and the one being, and being that is one, hold in themselves each of the intelligible living beings. By the one being, then, Parmenides sets up the divine kinds, with a fitting character; by being that is one, the kinds after the gods. By the one being of being that is one, he sets up the kinds of daemons; and by being that is one, the mortal kinds. And again, by the one being of the one being, he sets up the first and highest kinds of gods; and by its being that is one, the second kinds, which have an angelic order. And so all things are full of gods, angels, daemons, living beings and mortal natures. And you see how the middle of the higher kinds is kept. For being that is one is the angelic boundary of the one being that produces the gods; and the one being is the daemonic summit of being that is one, which adorns second natures. As for the unions of second natures, it is not unclear that they come near to multitude and to the procession of the natures set above them. Nor should you wonder that being that is one is the cause of angels, but the one being of daemons. For in one place being that is one is a part of the one being, in another the one being is a part of being that is one. And here the union is of being, but there being has the form of the one. For the summit of being that is one is of this kind. Rightly, then, is intelligible multitude all-powerful, and the intelligible living being all-perfect, as being at once the cause of all things, as far as the last of things; Plato all but crying out, in the words of the Chaldean Oracle: "From there a fiery whirlwind, sweeping along, darkens the flower of fire, leaping at the same time into the hollows of the worlds." For the divine henads, proceeding step by step, generate the multitude of all the natures in the world. This triad, then, is the source and cause of all things; and from it all the life and procession of the gods, of the kinds above us, and of mortal living beings exist. For it produces all things as wholes and in a one-like way, and binds to itself the whole principles of the divided rivers of life-giving and of the production of Forms.
!flag according to the one being indeed of being which is one, he constitutes the genera of dæmons, but according to being which is one, the mortal genera | Proclus' scheme, hard to follow in any language: each part of the one being, "the one" and "being", is again "one being" and "being that is one"; the parts in which the one rules give gods and angels, those in which being rules give daemons and mortals. Taylor's footnote supplies "cause" in the Greek he translated.
!note "All things are full of gods": Thales (Aristotle, On the Soul I.5, 411a), and the Laws X 899b. The Chaldean Oracles frag. 35 or 37 (the "whirlwind" of the Forms leaping from the Father's intellect).

@ch III.28 | A hymn to the intelligible gods
The intelligible kind of gods is set apart from all the other orders as the One is from every kind of gods. It is not intelligible as known by an intellect, but as the unshared, divine intelligible that fills all intellect. Its simplicity is not a Form but the hyparxis of the gods, united with the Good; and from it every order of gods has its own intelligible fullness.

@p
Again, then, let us go back from the divided study of intelligibles to the all-perfect and one science of them, and say to ourselves that this intelligible kind of gods is set apart in a unified way from all the other divine orders. It is called intelligible not as known by a partial intellect, nor as grasped by thinking together with reason, nor yet as existing beforehand in all-perfect intellect. For it goes beyond both universal and partial intelligibles, and exists before all objects of intellect, an unshared and divine intelligible. So it has the same transcendence over all the intelligible orders as the One over every kind of gods. For this intelligible is unshared, and fills from above the divine and intellectual orders. For if every intellect is intelligible to itself, it has this property through the intelligible gods, since fullness comes to all things from there. And so the intelligible is at once set apart from intellect, existing itself by itself, and at the same time not outside intellect. For there is an intelligible joined with intellect: the ranked coming from the set-apart, the shared from the unshared, what is in another from what exists before, and the multiplied from the one-like. Intelligible simplicity, then, must not be defined like what we usually say of intelligibles. For in these the one becomes equal to multitude, and separation to the one-like sameness of being. But intelligible simplicity is one-like, without separation and hidden, above every divided form of life and intellectual multitude. So I do not set intelligible simplicity in the rank of a Form. For a Form is partial, and below intelligible union. I take it to be the hyparxis of divine natures, generating the whole of the good distributed to all divine natures, in which the gods themselves exist. For the goodness of the gods is neither form nor habit, but the fullness of divine self-sufficiency and divine power, by which the gods fill all things with good. Much more, then, are the intelligible gods, because they are united to the Good, wholly full of goodness beyond being, and, set in this, hold in it their own highest hyparxis. Rightly, then, do we say that the intelligible gods unfold the inexpressible principle of all things, and his wonderful transcendence and union: themselves existing in a hidden way, but holding multitude in a one-like and unified way; reigning over the whole of things in a set-apart way, and unranked with all the other gods. For as the Good lights up all things with light beyond being, and shows the gods who are the fathers of all things, so the intelligible kind of gods, by its likeness to the Good, gives from itself to all the second gods intelligible fullness. So in each distribution of the gods there is a fitting intelligible multitude, just as a monad answering to the Good exists before each of the divine orders. This monad is the leader, existing beforehand, of union to second natures; and intelligible multitude is the source, existing beforehand, of beauty, self-sufficiency, power, being and all intelligible goods. For the gods hold beforehand and intelligibly all intellectual natures, and hold all things in themselves by highest union.
!note Taylor's footnote: with one correction ("all things by union"), the end of the book is complete, and not cut short, as Portus, the Latin translator, thought.

@book IV | The intelligible and intellectual gods
Book IV turns to the gods who are both intelligible and intellectual: they think the gods above them and are thought by those below, and they hold together life that is not shared, between being and intellect. They are divided into three triads. Proclus finds them in the Phaedrus, as the place above the heaven, the heaven's circuit, and the arch beneath the heaven, and in the Parmenides, as number, the continuous whole, and shape. Taylor's text heads all thirty-nine chapters.

@ch IV.1 | What marks the intelligible and intellectual gods
After the intelligible gods come the intellectual gods, and first those that are both intelligible and intellectual. Being, life and intellect are three monads above the divided gods; these middle gods have their being by life, life not shared, which is filled from being and fills intellect. So they are called intelligible and intellectual at once, filled from the first intelligibles and filling the intellectual gods.

@p
Let our discussion of the intelligible gods, unfolding Plato's mystic teaching about them, end here. It follows next that we consider in the same way the account of the intellectual gods. But since among intellectuals some are both intelligible and intellectual, those that, as the Oracle says, are thought at the same time as they think, while others are intellectual only, we will begin with those that are intellectual and at the same time intelligible, and first set out what they have in common, which will make the teaching about each of their orders clearer. Let us recall, then, what we showed a little before: that there are three monads as wholes, wholly beyond the gods divided into parts, namely being, life and intellect. And these, before the partial ones, share in the henads beyond being. Being is set apart from the rest; life has the middle rank; and intellect turns the end of this triad back to its beginning. All these are intelligibly in being, intelligibly and intellectually in life, and intellectually in intellect. And as second natures always share in those set above them, while these, before being shared, exist themselves by themselves; and as in each order there are three things, the cause of remaining, the cause of procession and the cause of return, intellect taking its form more by return, life by procession, and being by remaining; this being so, the first intellectual gods, whose being is by life, must join intellect that is not shared to the intelligible kind of gods, holding together the many processions of second natures in a one-like way, and unfolding and spreading out the stable hyparxis of the causes before them. For life that is not shared is of this kind: it surrounds what is first being and intellect, sharing in being and shared by intellect. This is the same as to say that thinking is filled from the intelligible, and fills intellect from itself. For being is the intelligible, and life is thinking. Being is marked by a divine hyparxis, life by power, and intellect by intelligible intellect. For as being is to hyparxis, so intellect is to being. And as intelligible power is to each of the extremes, so life is to the intelligible and to intellect. And as power is born from the One and hyparxis, but together with the one sets up the nature of being, so life proceeds from being and gives existence to a power other than that in being. And as the One itself, which is before being, gives being a second unity from itself, so life, having its existence before intellect, generates intellectual life. For true being and the intelligible, which come before the rest, supply both life and intellect with union. So life that is not shared, but shares in the intelligible monads, is second after being; it generates intellect that is not shared, completes this middle, and holds the bond of intelligibles and intellectuals. It is lit up by gods who have a union second to the hidden existence of the intelligibles, but who, as cause, come before the separation of intellectual natures. For the unitary, undivided, simple and first nature of the intelligibles sinks, through these gods, into multitude and separation and the unfolding, beyond telling, of the divine orders. So, I think, the gods who hold together unlimited life, being the middle of the intelligible and intellectual gods and carried in their own divisions as in a vehicle, are called intelligible and at the same time intellectual: filled from the first intelligibles, and filling the intellectual gods. For we call the intelligible gods intelligible not as ranked with intellect. The intelligible in intellect is one thing, what produces the intellectual gods another. And we call the gods who exist by life intelligible and intellectual at once, not as completing intellect, nor as set in intellectual thinking and giving intellect its power of thinking and the intelligible its power of being thought, but because they have their existence from the intelligible monads and generate all the intellectual hebdomads. And because they are lit up with intelligible life but exist before intellectuals as generating causes, we think it right to name them in common, joining their name from the extremes, as they also have, among the divine orders, a character that gathers wholes together.
!flag according to the Oracle perceiving intellectually are at the same time intellectually perceived | The Chaldean Oracles speak of gods who "think and are thought"; the phrase "intelligible and intellectual" (noētoi kai noeroi) names this whole order.
!flag generating all the intellectual hebdomads | The intellectual gods form a hebdomad (seven): Kronos, Rhea, Zeus, the three Kouretes (the "unpolluted" guardians), and the god who separates (Book V).

@p
It is clear, then, that they exist by this middle, and are next to the intelligible gods, who are both monads and triads. For the intelligible triads are triads in relation to the highest union, which is set apart from all things; but in relation to the divided being of triads they are monads, bringing whole triads out into light from themselves. Since the intelligibles, then, in their threefold procession, do not leave their unitary hyparxis, the intelligible and intellectual gods exist as triads, showing in themselves the separation of the monads, and through divine difference proceeding into multitude and a variety of powers and beings. For natures further from the one principle of all things are more multiplied than those before them: they are lessened in power and in the grasp they have of second natures, but divided into more numbers, further from the monad. They also leave the union that causes what first produces, and take variety in exchange for the hidden hyparxis of those first beings. By this reasoning the intelligible and intellectual separation is greater than the separation that is only intelligible. And the partial orders, again, have far more division than these, so as to unfold a multitude of gods that cannot be held in the numbers within ten. Their characters too cannot be described or unfolded by our thoughts, and are plain only to the gods themselves and to their causes. Such, then, are the intelligible and intellectual gods, and such is the character they have: a character that binds the extremes together, brings into light what comes before, and turns back what comes after. For they think the gods before them, and are thought by the gods after them. So Timaeus too makes the all-perfect living being the most beautiful of intelligibles: because there are intelligibles after it, which it surpasses in beauty, being above them, and because it is the boundary of the first intelligibles, while what comes after it exists intellectually. By this reasoning, then, the first intellectual gods are also intelligible; and we do not bring these things in from elsewhere and pin them on Plato, but say them with his help. This will be clearer from what follows.
!note Timaeus 30d: the living being itself is "the most beautiful of intelligibles". Taylor's footnote corrects a pronoun in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.2 | How the intelligible and intellectual gods come from the intelligible gods
They unfold the hidden union of the intelligible gods. From intelligible power they take the character that generates all things; from intelligible life, the being spread beneath them; and from the threefold intelligibles, their division into first, middle and last. So the divine orders run on without a break, each joined by likeness to the one before it.

@p
Next, then, we will discuss how the gods who light up the breadth of unshared life proceed from the intelligible gods. Since the intelligible gods set in themselves in a one-like way what is multiplied, in a hidden way what is divided, and the many kinds of beings by a wonderful transcendence of simplicity, the first intellectual gods, unfolding their undistinguished union and the unknown nature of their existence, and filled through intelligible power and life of being with the fertile abundance of wholes, have a kingdom that ranks second after them. They always produce, perfect and hold themselves together, but receive from the intelligible gods a hidden generation: from intelligible power, a character that generates all things; and from intelligible life, which exists beforehand as cause in the intelligible, the nature spread beneath them. For life is in intelligibles first, in intelligibles and intellectuals second, and in intellectuals third: as cause in the first, in its being in the second, and by sharing in the last. The first intellectual gods, then, proceed from the intelligible gods, multiplying their union and their unitary powers, unfolding their hidden hyparxis, and through generating, holding-together and perfecting causes making themselves like the transcendencies of intelligibles, which are of being, whole and all-perfect. For in intelligibles there were three powers that first produce: one setting up the being of wholes; another measuring what is multiplied; and another producing the Forms of everything generated.
!note Taylor's footnote supplies "intellectual gods" in the Greek he translated.

@p
In answer to these the intelligible and intellectual powers exist: one producing, by its very being, the life of second natures with a certain intelligible grasp; another holding together everything divided, and lighting up with the intelligible measure the natures that leave the one union of all things; and another supplying all things with shape, form and perfection. The intelligible and intellectual orders of gods, then, are generated by all the intelligible causes. From power they have the character of procession, and from life they receive the portion of being that hangs from them. For life is joined with power, since life is of itself unlimited (all motion has the unlimited in its nature), and the power of the unlimited generates all things. From the threefold existence of intelligibles they receive their distribution into first, middle and last. For all things must be held by a threefold procession, and before all other things the kinds of gods that are intelligible and intellectual at once. Because they exist as the middle of wholes and complete the bond of the first orders, by their summit they are made like intelligibles, and by their extremity like intellectuals. They are partly intelligible and partly intellectual. For everywhere the processions of the divine kinds come about through unbroken likeness, and the first of what is lower are united to the ends of the causes before them. But as the first and the last in this middle of wholes are both intelligible and intellectual, there must be a middle that holds them together, in which the character of these gods appears most of all. For what is intelligible and intellectual at once has more of one in one part, and in another shares equally in both. From these things the continuity of the procession of the divine orders appears wonderful. For the extremity of intelligibles was intellectual, but as among intelligibles. The summit of the intelligible and intellectual gods is intelligible, but has this character in the manner of life. Again, the end of the intelligible and intellectual gods is intellectual, but in the manner of life. And the beginning of intellectuals is intelligible and rules over the intellectual gods, but has the intelligible intellectually. And so all the divine kinds have an unbreakable link and communion, a wonderful friendship, a well-ordered lessening, and a transcendence partly ranked with them and partly set apart. What proceeds is always continuous with the cause that produces it; and second natures, while firmly set in their causes, make a procession from them. And there is one series and kinship of all things, second natures always coming from those before them through likeness. How the intelligible and intellectual gods come into light from the intelligible gods may be recalled through these things.

@ch IV.3 | The three triads of the intelligible and intellectual gods
They too are three triads: joined to the intelligible by their summit, to the intellectual by their end, and in the middle having both equally. Each triad holds being, life and intellect, but in the manner of life. The first unfolds the generating triad of the intelligibles, the second, which holds together, the measuring eternity, and the third, which perfects, the all-perfect living being.

@p
Next let us show how they are divided in their processions, and how their triads differ from the intelligible triads. These gods too are divided in three, in the way described: joined to the intelligible through their summit, to the intellectual through their end, and, through the middle bond of the extremes, having the character of each equally, reaching both the intelligible and the intellectual kinds of gods as the centre of these two orders, holding the communion of wholes in a one-like way. They are divided in three, too, because in them all things, being, life and intellect, are in the manner of life, as they are intelligibly in the gods before them, and intellectually in the gods who come from them. Being is the intelligible of life; life is the middle and the character of this order; and intellect is its extremity, and what is carried next in the intellectuals as in a vehicle. Since all things exist in these gods, then, there will be a division of them into first, middle and last kinds. And third, they are divided in three because life must remain, proceed and return to its principles, since, among beings, the first triad was said to establish all things, and before other things the second triad. Eternity, then, remains stably in the first triad. The triad after it supplies the whole of things with procession, motion, and life in act. And the third triad supplies the return to the One, and the perfection that turns all second natures back to their principles. So the intelligible and intellectual gods must share first in these three powers: remaining in their own summit, but proceeding from there and reaching out to all things, and again returning to the intelligible look-out, joining the end of their whole procession to the beginning of their generation.

@p
The intelligible and intellectual gods, then, are divided in three, as I have said. Being ranks first in them, life is the middle, and intellect the extremity. But since each of these three is perfect, and shares in the intelligible monads (I mean the being there, intelligible life, and intelligible intellect), they are tripled by sharing in the causes that first produce. The intelligible of their life has being, intellect and life intelligibly; the intelligible-and-intellectual of it has being, life and intellect intelligibly and intellectually at once; and its intellectual part has these intellectually and intelligibly. Everywhere there is a triad in each section, but with its own character. So three intelligible and intellectual triads appear to us, lit up by the divine henads, but each holding an all-various multitude. For since among intelligibles there was an all-powerful and all-perfect multitude, how could this multitude not be far more unfolded and multiplied in the gods second to the intelligible order, by their generative cause? Each triad, then, holds in itself a multitude of powers and a variety of Forms, bringing intelligible multitude into act and bringing into light the generative unlimitedness of the intelligibles. We, setting out from what shares, find the character of the gods beyond being who are shared. But in the order of things the intelligible and intellectual monads generate about themselves beings, all lives, and the intellectual kinds. And through these they unfold their own unknown transcendence, keeping by itself the cause, existing beforehand, of the whole of things. There are, then, as we have said, three intelligible triads; and there are three triads after them, which appear tripled from them by their fertile perfection.
!note Taylor's footnote supplies words missing from the Greek he translated ("intelligibly and intellectually, and the intellectual of it ... and intelligibly").

@p
But the character of the intelligible triad, and of the intelligible and intellectual triad, must be defined in another way. In the intelligible order each triad had only the third part of being; for it was made of limit, the unlimited, and what comes from both. This was being in the first triad, intelligible life in the second, and intelligible intellect in the third. The natures before them were henads and powers beyond being, which complete the whole triads. But in the intelligible and intellectual order, each triad has being, life and intellect: one intelligibly and intellectually at once, but more intelligibly, as continuous with the first intelligibles; another intellectually and intelligibly, but more intellectually, being carried next in the intellectuals; and another in equal parts, holding both characters in itself. So the first triad, to speak of each, was among intelligibles limit, the unlimited and being, for being was what was first mixed. But here the first triad is being, life and intellect, with their own henads. For being hangs from the first god of this triad, life from the second, and intellect from the third. And these three monads beyond being unfold the monads of the first triad. The second triad after this was, in the intelligible order, a henad beyond being, power, and hidden intelligible life. Here, though, being, life and intellect are all in the manner of life, and hang from the gods who hold the single bond of this whole order. For as the first henads had a power that unifies the middle kinds, so the second henads after them show the holding-together character of the causes that first produce. Then comes the third triad, which in the intelligible order was a henad, power and intelligible intellect; but here it is made of three gods beyond being, who close the boundary of the intelligible and intellectual gods and gird all things round intellectually, I mean being, life and intellect. They also supply divine perfection, imitating the all-perfect intelligible triad, as the gods who hold together imitate the intelligible measure, and the gods before them the generative cause of intelligibles. The three intelligible and intellectual triads, then, are generated in this way, and differ in this way from the intelligible triads.
!flag the connectedly containing Gods | Taylor's "connectedly containing" (synochic) is Proclus' name for the second of these triads: the gods who "hold together" all things. The third are the "perfecting" (teletarchic) gods.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects "most perfect" to "of perfection" in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.4 | How the Phaedrus leads us to this order of gods
In the Phaedrus, Zeus leads the twelve gods and the hosts of daemons to the look-out within the heaven, then to the arch beneath the heaven, then to the heaven and its back, from which souls see what is beyond. Above the heaven is the place where true being is, the plain of truth, Adrastia and the choir of virtues. To find the arch and the place above, we must first ask what the heaven is.

@p
Returning again to Plato, let us agree with him, and show the science of each of these triads that exists beforehand in him. First let us take what is written in the Phaedrus, and look, from Socrates' own words, at how he unfolds to us the whole ordering of these triads and the differences it holds. In the Phaedrus, then, twelve leaders are said to rule over the whole of the world's affairs, to lead all the gods of the world and all the herds of daemons, and to turn them back to the intelligible nature. Zeus is said to lead all twelve, to drive a winged chariot, to set all things in order and care for them, and to lead all the army of gods that follow him, first to the look-out within the heaven, and to the blessed sights and paths of discursive reason among the intelligibles there; and next to the arch beneath the heaven, which closely girds the heaven and is held in it, and after this to the heaven itself and its back, where divine souls stand and, carried round with the heaven, look at all the being that is beyond it. Socrates adds that before the heaven there is what is called the place above the heaven, in which true and real being, the plain of truth, the kingdom of Adrastia, and the divine choir of virtues exist; and that souls, nourished by thinking these monads, are blessed, following, in their contemplation, the circuit of the heaven.
!flag blessed spectacles, and discursive energies of the intelligibles | The Phaedrus 247a: "many and blessed are the sights and paths (diexodoi) within the heaven". Taylor renders diexodoi "discursive energies", as if it meant the passage of thought; the Greek means ways through, or courses.
!note The Phaedrus 246e-247e. Adrastia ("the inescapable") is named at 248c, in "the law of Adrastia".

@p
These things are said in the Phaedrus, Socrates being clearly inspired by a god, and speaking of mystic matters. But before other things we must consider what the heaven is of which Socrates speaks, and in which order of beings it is set. For once we have found this, we may also look at the arch beneath the heaven and the place above the heaven. For each of these is taken by its relation to the heaven: the one set first above it, the other ranked first beneath it.
!flag according to habitude towards the heaven | Taylor's "habitude" is relation (schesis). Taylor's footnote supplies "and the place above the heaven" in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.5 | The heaven of the Phaedrus is not the visible heaven
If the heaven to which Zeus leads the gods were the visible heaven, the higher kinds would turn to what is beneath them, and the gods' blessed thinking would be of perceptible things; and souls move bodies, not bodies souls. So the heaven is intelligible, as Plotinus and Iamblichus held, and the Cratylus confirms it: Ouranos is "sight looking at the things above", father of Kronos, grandfather of Zeus. It is thinking, the middle between intellect and the intelligible.

@p
What, then, is the heaven to which Zeus leads the gods? If we say it is the perceptible heaven, as some others do, the higher kinds will have to turn to things lower than themselves by nature. For if Zeus, the great leader in the heaven, goes to this perceptible heaven and leads to it all the gods who follow him, he will turn to what is lower and after himself. And with Zeus, so will all the leaders, and the gods and daemons who hang from them; though the same Socrates in the Phaedrus says that even a partial soul, once perfected, travels on high and governs the whole world. How, then, could the leaders of whole souls turn to the perceptible heaven and exchange the intelligible look-out for a lower lot, when through these souls they rule the universe, in order to light up the things of the world with a power that is free and unconstrained? Besides this, what blessed thoughts of the gods are there within this perceptible heaven, and what unfoldings of all knowledge of perceptible things? For in short the gods know perceptible things not by turning to them but by holding their causes in themselves. So, thinking themselves with their intellect, they know perceptible things as their cause, and rule them, not by looking at them and leaning toward what they govern, but by turning lower natures back to themselves through love. It is not lawful, then, for the gods who order the whole heaven and think it worth their providence to be ever placed beneath the circuit of this heaven; there is no blessedness in contemplating the things beneath it; and the souls that turn to this contemplation are not among those who are happy and follow the gods, but rank among those who exchange intelligible food for the food of opinion, such as Socrates says are the souls that are lamed, have broken their wings, and are sunk down. Since these afflictions, then, belong to partial souls, and not to happy ones, how can we credit the ruling and leading gods with turning to the perceptible heaven?
!flag exchange intelligible for doxastic nutriment | The Phaedrus 248b: souls that fail to see true being "use the food of opinion" (trophē doxastē). Taylor's footnote says the sentence after this one is too corrupt in the Greek he translated to render, and he leaves it out.
!note The Phaedrus 246c: the perfect, winged soul "travels on high and governs the whole world".

@p
Further, Socrates says that souls standing on the back of the heaven are carried round by the heaven's own revolution; but Timaeus and the Athenian Stranger say that souls lead everything in the heavens by their own motions, cover bodies from outside with their motions, and, living their own life through all time, give bodies powers of motion as second causes. How, then, do these things agree with those who make this heaven perceptible? For souls do not contemplate and dance round the intelligibles through the circling of the heavens; it is through the unseen revolution of souls that bodies turn in a circle and make their circuits about them. If anyone says, then, that the perceptible heaven carries souls round, and that it is divided into the back, the depth and the arch beneath the heaven, he must accept many absurdities.
!note The Phaedrus 247b-c; Timaeus 36e; the Laws X 896e-898c.

@p
But if someone says that the heaven to which Zeus is the leader, and all the gods, and with them the daemons, follow him, is intelligible, he will unfold Plato's inspired accounts in keeping with the nature of things, and will follow the most famous of his interpreters. For Plotinus and Iamblichus hold that this heaven is something intelligible. And before them Plato himself, in the Cratylus, following the Orphic theogonies, calls Kronos the father of Zeus, and Ouranos, Heaven, the father of Kronos. He shows that Zeus is the Craftsman of the whole of things through the names he is called by, looking into the truth about them. He shows that Kronos holds together a divine intellect, and that Ouranos is the thinking of the first intelligibles. For sight, he says, looking at the things above, is Heaven. So Ouranos exists before every divine intellect, with which the great Kronos is full; but he thinks the things above, beyond the order of heaven. The great Ouranos, then, has a kingdom between the intelligible and intellectual orders. For the circuit mentioned in the Phaedrus is thinking, through which all the gods and souls come to contemplate the intelligibles. But thinking is a middle between intellect and the intelligible. We must say, then, that the whole heaven is set in this middle, and holds the one bond of the divine orders, being the father of the intellectual kind but generated from the kings before it, which it is also said to see. On one side of it the place above the heaven must be set, and on the other the arch beneath the heaven.
!flag sight, says he, looking to the things above, is Heaven | The Cratylus 396b-c: ouranos (heaven) is explained as horōsa ta anō, "looking at the things above". Zeus' name (Zēna, Dia) is "through whom (di' hon) life (zēn)" comes; Kronos' is koros nou, "pure intellect".
!note Plotinus V.8.13 and Iamblichus (in a lost commentary on the Phaedrus) took the heaven as intelligible.

@ch IV.6 | The place above the heaven is intelligible as the summit of the intellectuals
The place above the heaven holds a separated multitude, truth, science, justice, temperance, the meadow and Adrastia, which do not belong to the first intelligibles, and it is seen by a partial intellect. So it is lower than the intelligible triads but above the heaven: intelligible, but the summit of the intellectual gods, and the food of souls.

@p
Again, if the place above the heaven is the unshared and hidden kind of the intelligible gods, how can we set so great a divine multitude there, and with separation: truth, science, justice, temperance, the meadow and Adrastia? For neither the sources of the virtues nor the separation and variety of Forms belong to the intelligible gods. For the first and most unitary of the Forms reach out the making intellect of wholes to the intelligible pattern and the grasp of Forms there. But Socrates in the Phaedrus says that a partial intellect contemplates the place above the heaven. For this intellect is the pilot of the soul, as the philosophers before us rightly say. If, then, we must look by this analogy for the difference among intelligibles, as the making intellect is not shared but a partial intellect is shared, so of the intelligible, the one that is the first pattern of the Craftsman belongs to the first intelligibles, but the one that is the first pattern of a partial intellect belongs to the second intelligibles, which are intelligibles but have an intelligible transcendence as existing at the summit of the intellectuals. But if the place above the heaven is beyond the heavenly circuit, but lower than those intelligible triads, since it is more spread out (for it is the plain of truth, and is not unknown, is divided into a multitude of Forms, has a variety of powers, and its meadow nourishes souls and is seen by them, whereas the first intelligibles light up souls with inexpressible union, but are not known by them through thinking); if so, the place above the heaven must exist between the intelligible nature and the heavenly circuit. And if Plato himself grants that being that truly is exists in this place, how could he not grant that it is intelligible, and shares in the first intelligibles? For because it is being it is intelligible; but because it truly is, it shares in being.
!flag a partial intellect contemplates the supercelestial place | The Phaedrus 247c: being in the place above the heaven "is seen only by intellect, the soul's pilot". A "partial" intellect is the individual intellect of a soul, as against the one making intellect of the whole.

@p
Moreover, having in itself a multitude of intelligibles, it will not be ranked with the first triad; for there is the one being, not the multitude of beings. Having the many-sided life that the meadow points to, it is lower than the second triad; for intelligible life is one and without separation. And again, since it shines out with divided Forms, all-various orders and fertile powers, it falls short of the all-perfect triad among the intelligibles. If, then, it is second to these in dignity and power, but set above the heavenly order, it is intelligible, but the summit of the intellectual gods. This is why souls get their food from there. For the intelligible is food, since the first intelligibles too, the beautiful, the wise and the good, are said to nourish souls. By these, Socrates says, the soul's wing is nourished, and by their contraries it is spoiled and destroyed. These things, though, the first intelligibles do in a set-apart way, through union and silence. But the place above the heaven is said to nourish through thinking and activity, and to fill the happy choir of souls with intelligible light and the fertile rivers of life.
!note The Phaedrus 246e.

@ch IV.7 | The arch beneath the heaven ends the intelligible and intellectual gods
The arch is beneath the heaven, not in it, and is held by the heaven on every side; it is not the summit of the intellectuals, which stands apart from the heaven's kingdom. The gods rise to the feast through it: by it they turn back, from the heaven they get their vigorous thinking, and from the place above they are filled with intelligible goods. So the place above is the intelligible summit, the circuit the middle, and the arch the intellectual end.

@p
After the place above the heaven and the heaven itself comes the arch beneath the heaven, which plainly must be ranked under the heaven, not in it. For Plato does not call it the heavenly arch, but the arch beneath the heaven. That it lies next beneath the heavenly circuit is clear from what is written about it. But if anyone would make the arch, being such, the same as the summit of the intellectuals, and not as the end of the intelligible and intellectual gods, he must now consider what follows. The summit of the intellectuals sets itself apart from the kingdom of the heaven, but the arch beneath the heaven is held by it on every side. And the summit sets up the whole of intellect, intellectual multitude, and, as Socrates says, the blessed paths of the gods; but the arch only bounds the heavenly series, and supplies the gods with the way up to the heaven. For when the gods rise to the feast and the banquet, and are filled with intelligible goods, they go up to the arch beneath the heaven, and through it are raised to the heavenly circuit. So if you say that the arch perfects the gods and turns them back to the whole heaven and the place above the heaven, you will not stray from Plato's meaning. For the gods are fed by the intelligible, by the meadow and by the divine Forms that the place above the heaven holds; but they are filled with this food through the arch beneath the heaven. For through it they also share in the heavenly circuit. So they return through the arch, receive a vigorous thinking from the heavenly order, and are filled with intelligible goods from the place above the heaven. It is clear, then, that the place above the heaven has the intelligible summit, the circuit of the heaven the middle breadth, and the arch the intellectual extremity. For all things are in it. Intellect turns back, but the intelligible is what is desired. And divine thinking completes the middle, perfecting the returns of divine natures and binding them to the first, but unfolding their reachings toward the intelligibles, and filling second natures with the goods that come before them. I think, though, that through these things we have reminded the reader enough of the order of these three.
!flag the arch, the intelligible extremity | Taylor prints "intelligible" here; the sense (the place above the heaven is the intelligible summit, the arch the extremity where intellect turns back) seems to want "intellectual", and the Workshop renders it so. This is a reading from the sense, not from the Greek.
!note The Phaedrus 247a-b: "when they go to the feast and the banquet, they climb up to the arch beneath the heaven".

@ch IV.8 | Why the order is named from its middle, the heaven
Why are the extremes named "above the heaven" and "beneath the heaven"? Because this whole kind binds the extremes together, turning back the one and unfolding the other, and gathers all the kinds of gods into itself as into a centre. As the intelligible gods are named from their summit, these middle gods are named from their middle.

@p
Perhaps someone may ask us why we mark the whole procession of the intelligible and intellectual gods by its middle, and why we call one of the extremes "above the heaven" and the other "beneath the heaven", from their relation to the middle, pointing to the set-apart transcendence of the one and the close, joined lessening of the other. We may answer him briefly that this whole kind of intelligible and intellectual gods binds both extremes together, being the cause of return to the one, and to the other of being unfolded into light and being present to second natures. As we call all the intelligible gods fatherly and unitary, marking them from their summit, and say that they are the boundaries of the whole of things, those that make being, those that cause everlastingness, and those that are sources of the production of Forms; so we unfold these middle gods from the middle in them, as the leaders of all bonds. For this whole middle order gives life, holds together and perfects. Its summit unfolds the impressions of the intelligibles and their inexpressible union. Its end turns the intellectuals back and joins them to the intelligibles. And its middle gathers into itself and fixes, as in a centre, the whole kinds of the gods. For to the extremes too, by reference to the middle, we give the relation of transcendence and lessening, calling one above and the other beneath the middle.

@ch IV.9 | Plato's way up to the intelligible is the initiates' way
Plato leads souls and gods up as the mysteries do: first to the source gods through the free leaders, then to the perfecting gods, then to the gods who hold together (the heaven's circuit), and so to the place above the heaven and the first intelligibles, where contact is through silence. Our souls go up the same steps. The Phaedrus' words for initiation, the "whole, simple, still visions", and the soul freed from the body like the initiate buried to the head, show it; and love, truth and faith are the three powers that lead up.

@p
Through these things, then, as I have said, we may answer briefly anyone who is puzzled about these names. But here we should admire Plato's divine science, because he has told of the way all things go up to the intelligible in keeping with the highest of the initiators. For first he raises souls, and the gods themselves, to the source gods through the free leaders. For the blessed and most abundant sights and paths are chiefly in these sources, in which the theurgists also place all their hope of salvation. They are blessed through the undefiled monads, most abundant through the cause of divine difference, and sights and paths through the intellectual and fatherly powers. Second, Plato raises souls and gods from the sources, and through the sources, to the leaders of perfection. For after many divided thinkings the good of the perfecting gods shines out, spread from above from the intellectual gods themselves, and lighting up us, and before our souls whole souls, and before these the gods themselves. From the perfecting gods Plato raises souls and gods to the gods who hold together all the intellectual orders. For the perfecting gods hang from these gods, exist together with them, and are held by them. So close is the communion and union of these gods that some of the most famous interpreters of Plato have supposed an all-perfect, undivided sameness among them, being unable to grasp by reasoning the separation in them. Here too it may seem to someone that Plato calls the extremity of the heavenly circuit the arch. But this is not so. For he does not call the arch heavenly, but beneath the heaven. As what is above the heaven is in its being set apart from the heaven, so what is beneath the heaven is lower than the kingdom of the heaven. For the first points to transcendence, the second to a lessening ranked next below.
!flag through the liberated leaders | The "liberated" (apolytoi, "unbound") leaders are the twelve gods who lead in the Phaedrus, ranked by Proclus among the gods above the world; the "source" (fontal) gods are the intellectual gods, sources of the orders after them. The order of the ascent runs from the lowest of these orders upward.
!note The "theurgists" are the Chaldean priests and their followers, for whom the soul is saved by rites that join it to the gods.

@p
After this circuit, which holds together the whole of things, Plato raises souls and the gods to the place above the heaven and the intelligible union of the intellectuals, where the gods, remaining, are fed, are in a happy state, and are filled with inexpressible and unitary goods. For with the theurgists too, the way up to the inexpressible and intelligible powers that are the summits of all intellectuals is through the gods who hold together. How the gods are here joined to the first intelligibles, though, Plato no longer unfolds in words; for the contact with them is inexpressible, and through what cannot be spoken, as he teaches in what he says of them in the Phaedrus. Through this order the mystic union with the intelligible and first-producing causes comes about. With us, then, the way of joining is the same; and through this the way of theurgic ascent is the more credible. For as wholes go up to the principles set apart from them through the natures placed next above them, so parts too, imitating the ascent of wholes, are joined through middle steps to the simplest and inexpressible causes. For what Plato has said in this dialogue about whole souls he afterwards unfolds about ours. First he joins them with the free gods; then through these he raises them to the perfecting gods; then through these to the gods who hold together; and in the same way up to the intelligible gods. Socrates, then, telling how we go up to intelligible beauty, and how, following the gods, before bodies and generation, we shared in that blessed sight, says: "For then it was lawful to see beauty shining, when with that happy choir we saw this blessed vision and sight, we following Zeus, others with some other god, and were initiated into the mysteries which it is right to call the most blessed of mysteries." How, then, were we once joined with intelligible beauty? Through being initiated, he says, into the most blessed of mysteries. What else does this say, than that we were joined with the perfecting leaders and initiated by them, so as to be filled with beauty? And what goods does the initiation bring? "Which rites we celebrated," he says, "when we were whole and untouched by evils, and were initiated into, and became watchers of, whole, simple, still and happy visions."
