The whole, then, comes to souls from the heavenly circuit. For this holds all the divine kinds together, and our souls too. Everything that holds parts in a whole also holds what is divided, and gathers what is many into union and simplicity. But the whole, still and simple visions are unfolded to souls from above, from the place above the heaven, through the gods who hold together. For the mystic impressions of the intelligibles shine out in that place, and the unknown and inexpressible beauty of their characters. For muesis and epopteia, being initiated and becoming a watcher, are symbols of inexpressible silence, and of union with mystic natures through intelligible visions. And most wonderful of all: as the theurgists order the whole body to be buried except the head in the most mystic of initiations, Plato too foresaw this, moved by the gods themselves. "For being pure," he says, "and freed from this garment around us, which we now call the body, we were initiated into and saw this most blessed sight, full of intelligible light." For the pure brightness he mentions unfolds intelligible light as a symbol. So when we are set in the intelligible we shall have a life wholly freed from the body. But raising the charioteer's head to the place beyond the heaven, we shall be filled with the mysteries there and with intelligible silence. It seems to me, too, that Plato unfolds well enough the three causes that lead up, love, truth and faith, to those who read him with care. For what but love joins us with beauty? Where is the plain of truth but in this place? And what but faith causes this inexpressible initiation? For initiation is not through thinking or judgement at all, but through the unitary silence that faith gives, which is better than every act of knowing, and which sets both whole souls and ours in the inexpressible and unknown nature of the gods. These things, though, have run to this length from my sympathy with such matters.
!flag muesis and epopteia | The two grades of the Eleusinian mysteries: muēsis, initiation (from muein, to close the eyes), and epopteia, the final "watching". Taylor's footnote quotes Hermeias' commentary on the Phaedrus: in muesis the soul no longer takes in the divine mysteries by sense but with the pure soul itself, and in epopteia it is set in them and becomes their watcher.
!flag theurgists order the whole body to be buried, except the head | A Chaldean rite of "immortalisation", known only from this passage and a few others: the initiate was buried to the neck, as if the body were already dead.
!flag elevating the head of the charioteer to the place beyond the heaven | The Phaedrus 248a: the best souls "raise the head of the charioteer into the place outside".
!note The Phaedrus 250b-c. Taylor's footnote supplies a word missing from the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.10 | The place above the heaven: intelligible, and the mother of the gods
The place above the heaven is "being that truly is", not the one being of the first triad: intelligible as above the heaven, as Kronos is intelligible in relation to the Craftsman. It answers to the first triad as its feminine, generating counterpart: a "place" because it receives the fatherly causes and brings forth the gods, holding all its offspring within itself.

@p
Returning again to the theology we set out, let us unfold the ideas Plato points to about each order of the intelligible and intellectual gods. The place above the heaven, then, is intelligible. So Plato says it is being that truly is, and is seen by the intellect of the soul. It is also the one grasp and union of the intellectual gods. For it is not intelligible in the way the living being itself is, nor the first eternity, nor what is itself first the one being. For as these are first intelligibles, they are set apart from all other intelligibles and exist beforehand by themselves. But the place above the heaven is set next above the heavenly circuit and is its intelligible, yet it is not simply intelligible. That we say this rightly Socrates too bears witness, giving the thinking of this intelligible to souls too, through the heaven. For in this period, in which they are carried round with the circuit of the heaven, they see justice, they see temperance, they also see science, and each of the beings that truly and really are; so that if the place above the heaven is intelligible and real being, it is still intelligible as being above the heaven. The first intelligibles, though, are intelligible by their own being, and by the set-apart, first-producing cause of all intellectual natures. For the great Kronos too, though an intellectual god and the fullness of intellect, is intelligible in relation to the Craftsman; for he is the summit of the intellectual triad. So in this way the place above the heaven also has an intelligible transcendence over the heavenly circuit, and is intelligible as among the first intellectuals. So it also exists in answer to the first triad of intelligibles. That triad, though, was simply intelligible. For the intelligible among intelligibles exists at once before all second and third intelligibles. But the place above the heaven is not simply intelligible; for it is the summit of the intellectuals, not of the intelligibles. So Plato calls the first triad of intelligibles the one being, but names the place above the heaven being that truly is. The first goes before all beings in a wonderful simplicity and the hidden unity of being. For that being is the intelligible itself: not intelligible in one respect and intellectual in another, nor what is acted on by being, but the seat and most ancient monad of being. This order, the place above the heaven, falls short of the union of that triad: it shares in being, but is not simply being. So Plato calls it being, and being that truly is, as receiving this intelligible and being-like nature from what is first being. And the first triad of intelligibles was fatherly, for it exists by divine union and limit, and is the hidden and highest boundary of all intelligibles. But the place above the heaven is motherly, existing by the unlimited and the power of the unlimited. For this order is feminine and fertile, and produces all things by intelligible powers. So Plato calls it a place, as the receptacle of the fatherly causes, bringing forth and bringing out the generative powers of the gods into the existence of second natures. For having called matter too a place, he calls it the mother and nurse of the forming principles that proceed into it from being and the fatherly cause.
!note The Phaedrus 247c-e. Timaeus 49a, 51a, 52a-d: the receptacle, "nurse" and "mother" of becoming, and "place" (chōra). Taylor's footnote corrects "intellectual as among the first intelligibles" to "intelligible as among the first intellectuals" in the Greek he translated.

@p
By this analogy, then, Plato names the place above the heaven, as feminine, and as the cause in a motherly way of those things of which the intelligible father is the cause in a fatherly way. Matter, though, only receives Forms; but the mother and nurse of the gods not only receives but also sets up and generates second natures, together with the father. Nor does this generating goddess bring out her offspring from herself into a place outside and separate them from her own embrace, as things that generate here bring their offspring into the light outside themselves; she generates, holds and sets all things in herself. So she is also their place, as a seat that holds them on every side, and as, by her fertile and first-producing powers, taking beforehand into herself and holding all the processions, multitude and variety of second natures. For all beings exist in the gods and are held and kept safe by them. For where could they go away from the gods and from the embrace in them? And how, if they left them, could they remain even for the smallest part of time? But the powers that generate divine natures are in a special way said to hold their offspring, being their nearest causes, and setting up their being with fuller division and a more particular providence. For fatherly causes produce second natures in a one-like way, set apart and unranked with them, and hold their own offspring in a unitary way. In simplicity they take beforehand their variety, and in union their multitude. It is clear, then, from what has been said, that the place above the heaven is intelligible, and how it is intelligible. It is clear besides how it is feminine; for place fits the generating gods for the reasons given. And the meadow is the source of a life-giving nature, as will soon be shown. Socrates also takes all the divine natures in this place to be of this kind, feminine, I mean science itself, justice itself, temperance itself, truth itself, and Adrastia; which may be taken as a special sign that Plato, and not only the other theologians, gives the feminine to this order.

@ch IV.11 | Why the place above the heaven is praised both by denials and by affirmations
The summit of the intellectuals is unknown and inexpressible in its own character, knowable only by intelligible impressions, as the image of the unknown union of the intelligibles. So, as the One is praised by denials alone, Plato praises this summit by denials, "without colour, without shape, without contact", and also by affirmations, "being that truly is", "the plain of truth", as sharing in what is before it; as the Timaeus' Craftsman is named "father" and "good" but is "hard to find and impossible to declare".

@p
What, then, is the reason why Plato first praises this god by denials, in answer to the One? And what are the denials? He calls it without colour, without shape and without contact, and takes from it these three hyparxes, colour, shape and contact. I say, then, that this order, being the summit of the intellectual gods, is unknown and inexpressible in its own character, and is to be known only through intelligible impressions. For as the summit of the intellectuals it joins itself with the intelligibles. For how could intellectuals be joined with intelligibles unless they first set up an intelligible transcendence of their own? And what link and communion of the whole orders of things could be seen, unless the extremities of the first had a certain likeness to the beginnings of the second? Because of this likeness these grow together, and all things exist in one series. As the end of the intelligibles was intellectual, then, so the beginning of the intellectuals has an intelligible hyparxis. Each of these is intelligible; but the one is simply intelligible, and the other is not intelligible without the addition of the intellectual. These exist together with each other. The one is the fatherly cause of the whole of things, as intelligible, and the intellectual in it is spread out intelligibly. The other sets up the same things in a generating way, because it is intellectual and intelligible good rules in the intellectual kind. All things, then, come from both: in a set-apart way from the intellectual of the intelligibles, and in a ranked way from the intelligible of the intellectuals. Both delight in unknown hyparxes, and are known only, as Plato says, by intelligible, mystic and inexpressible impressions. So he calls it boldness to try to unfold the secrets about them and explain in words their unknown union.
!note The Phaedrus 247c: "being without colour, without shape, without contact"; and "one must be bold enough to speak the truth".

@p
From the end of the intelligible order, though, the summit of the intellectuals has its unknown character. For as far as it joins itself to the first intelligibles and is filled with their unitary, inexpressible and fatherly hyparxis, so far it exists in an unknown way before the intellectuals. So it cannot be grasped by the natures after it, but is known by those before it, reaching up over itself into an unbroken union with them. It knows the natures before it intelligibly; but this does not differ at all from one-like and inexpressible knowledge. For intelligible knowledge is the union, cause, summit, and unknown and hidden hyparxis of all knowledge. Since, then, the one and united triad is, if we may say so, the intellectual image of the unknown union of the intelligibles, and rules over the same one-like and unknown power in the intellectuals as its own cause does, Plato unfolds it in a mystic way through denials. For everywhere the highest and the unknown answer to the unitary God. As we are taught to praise this God through denials, so we try to unfold the one-like and unknown summits of the second orders by denial. And in short, since Socrates in the Phaedrus makes the ascent as far as the place above the heaven, setting it, in this order and in the ascent of souls, in answer to the first, he praises it by denials. For in the Timaeus Plato holds that the one Craftsman, through whom every making kind of gods exists, is inexpressible and unknown; and everywhere the highest has this transcendence over second natures. For it imitates the cause that is at once set apart from all beings in a unitary way. We praise this cause through denials alone, as existing before all things; but we unfold the summits that proceed in answer to it by affirmation and denial at once. As sharing in the natures before them, we praise them by affirmation. For Plato calls the place above the heaven being that truly is, the plain of truth, the meadow, and the gods' intelligible look-out, and does not only call it without colour, without shape and without contact, so mixing affirmations with denials. For this order is a middle between the intelligible gods and the first intellectual divine orders, holding the bond of both. It keeps watch intellectually, by a one-like and unknown transcendence, but passes on the fullnesses of the intelligibles as far as the last of things. It raises all things at once, by one common union, up to the intelligible father, and generates and brings them out as far as matter. Set between the unitary and the multiplied gods, then, it is unfolded by denial, through the unknown way in which it goes beyond second natures, and by affirmation, through its sharing in the first natures. For the first Craftsman is called in the Timaeus maker and father and good, and all such names, so far as he shares in the causes that exist before him; but so far as he is the monad of all making, Plato leaves him unknown and inexpressible, set apart from all the makers of things. For he says, "it is hard to find him, and, having found him, impossible to declare him to all." So Plato unfolds the place above the heaven by affirmation, as filled from the first causes, calling it at one time being that truly is, at another the plain of truth, and at another something else of this kind; but so far as it goes beyond the intellectual gods, and so far as it is highest and unitary, he praises it by denial, as he does the principle set apart from all things.
!flag the one and united triad | Proclus' name for the first of the intelligible and intellectual triads, the place above the heaven: one and "united" (hēnōmenē) as the image of the hidden union of the intelligibles.
!note Timaeus 28c: "the maker and father of this universe it is hard to find, and having found him, impossible to declare to all". Taylor's footnotes correct a pronoun and supply "but" in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.12 | What the three denials deny
In the Parmenides the denials of the One come from all the divine orders. In the Phaedrus, what is denied of the place above the heaven are the three orders next below it: colour is the heaven, which holds together and lights up; shape is the arch beneath the heaven; and contact belongs first to the summit of the intellectuals, as the Parmenides shows. The place above is free of them not by lacking them but as their cause. Readers who take colour, shape and contact as perceptible miss the point: even nature is free of those.

@p
Next, then, we should consider what the denials are, and from which orders they are generated. In the Parmenides, the denials of the One are produced from all the divine orders, because the One is the cause of all of them. Everything divine shares, by its own hyparxis, in the first principle; and the One, being beyond these, is far more set apart from the natures after them. For all things proceed from these, since they receive their characters in a divided way. This is clear from the other hypotheses, in which the same conclusions come round again, joined at one time by denial and at another by affirmation. For what could exist that was not first held as cause in wholes? But in the Phaedrus, the things denied of the intelligible summit of all intellectuals are the natures set next after this summit: the sacred kinds that hold together, that perfect, and the fatherly kinds of what are properly called intellectuals. For this summit, being set apart from these, also goes beyond all the intellectual gods. For what every kind of the gods is to the One, the three orders after this summit are to it. Plato, then, calls colour the heavenly order that holds wholes together and lights them up with intelligible light, because the visible beauty of this perceptible heaven too shines with colours of every kind, and with light. So he calls that heaven intellectual colour and light. For the light proceeding from the Good is, in the orders above the heaven, unknown and hidden, remaining in the inner sanctuaries of the gods; but in this order it shines out and from unseen becomes plain. So it is likened to colour, the offspring of light.

@p
Further, if the heaven is sight looking at the things above, its intelligible may very fitly be called colour, which is joined with sight. The cause of the intelligibles in the heaven, then, is without colour, but set apart from them; for perceptible colour is the offspring of the sun's light. Plato calls figure the order that comes next after the heavenly order, which we have called the arch beneath the heaven. For "arch" is itself the name of a shape. And in this order, in short, Parmenides too sets intellectual shape. But Plato first gives contact to the summit of the intellectuals, as is clear from the conclusions of the Parmenides. For in the first hypothesis, taking shape away from the One, he uses this as a middle term, that the One does not touch itself. "But the one," he says, "does not touch itself." And the conclusion is plain. Here, then, contact first exists, and exists as cause. For of those things of which the Craftsman is the nearest cause, the father before him is the cause as pattern. In this order, then, contact is the pattern of the free gods. So these three orders follow one another: colour, shape and contact. And from these the place above the heaven is set apart in its being. So it is without colour, without shape and without contact. Nor does it go beyond these three by lacking them, but by the excellence of a cause. For it gives colour a share of light from the intelligibles; on shape it lights up intellectual limit; and into contact it puts, from above, union and continuity, and perfects all things by its power: what is touched through union, what is shaped through sharing in limit, and what is coloured through the lighting-up of light. And it draws upward and lures to itself everything in an inexpressible way and through intelligible impressions, and fills everything with unitary goods.
!flag But the one,” says he, “does not touch itself | The Parmenides 138a: the One is "neither in itself nor in another", so it neither touches itself nor anything else; Proclus takes the denial of contact as one of those that come from a definite order of gods. Here "the one" is Parmenides' one.

@p
If we say these things rightly, then, we must not accept the reading of those who busy themselves with perceptible colours, contacts and shapes, and say that the place above the heaven is set apart from these. For these are trifles, and do not fit that place at all. For even nature, not only nature as a whole but partial nature too, is set apart from perceptible colours, visible shapes and bodily contact. What, then, is so venerable in this, if it belongs even to natures? But we must stretch colours, shapes and contacts from on high down to the last of things, and show that the place above the heaven is set apart from all of these alike. For soul and intellect too share in shape; and contact is often among bodiless natures, by the communion of first with second beings, and it is usual to call these communions contacts, and to call the touchings of intellectual thoughts graspings. We should not, then, be carried from the first things to the last, or compare the highest order of the intellectuals with the last of beings, above which both soul and nature are set. For in doing so we shall go wrong, and not attend to Plato, who cries out that it is boldness to say these things about it. For where is the boldness, and what the unknown power beyond our thoughts, in contemplating the truth about perceptible colours, shapes and contacts? Such an existence is known by natural philosophers, not by the sons of the theologians. Such, then, is the power of the denials through which Plato praises the place above the heaven.
!flag known by physiologists | Taylor's "physiologists" are the natural philosophers (physiologoi), the students of nature, as against the theologians.

@ch IV.13 | What is affirmed of the place above the heaven
It is "being that truly is" because it shares in what is first being, as the one comes from the first principle and being from the intelligibles. It is seen by the soul's pilot, intellect, because it has an intelligible transcendence over the other intellectual gods. For the intelligible and what truly is go together: every intelligible gives being, as every intellect gives knowledge.

@p
Again, then, let us next look at the affirmations, and how they exist by sharing in the first intelligibles themselves. The place above the heaven is said to be being that truly is, because it shares in what is first being. For to be, and truly to be, belong to all things as the offspring of intelligible being. For as the one comes from the first principle, which is before the intelligibles, so the nature of being comes from the intelligibles. For the one being exists there, as Parmenides taught us a little before. But the place above the heaven is seen by the pilot of the soul, because it has an intelligible transcendence over the other intellectual gods. So its intelligible good is made plain by its being known by intellect. This intelligible, then, like what truly is, comes to it from the unitary gods. For they are first, unshared intelligibles, and the first producing causes of all intelligibles. These two also go together, what truly is and the intelligible. For every intelligible truly is, and everything that truly is is intelligible. For intellect is intelligible by the being in it, but by its power of knowing it is intellect. So every intellect supplies knowledge, and every intelligible supplies being. For what each is first, it lights up on the orders after it.

@ch IV.14 | Science, temperance and justice in the place above the heaven
The souls who go round with the heaven see justice itself, temperance itself and science itself: not intellectual Forms but three intelligible goddesses, sources of the intellectual virtues. Science gives the gods steady thinking of what is before them, temperance their return to themselves, and justice their just distribution of good to what comes after; and they answer to the three intelligible triads.

@p
Third, then, the kind of true science is said to be set about the place above the heaven. For two things go up to the contemplation of that being: intellect, the pilot of the soul (this is a partial intellect, set above souls, and raising them to their fatherly harbour), and true science, which is the perfection of the soul. Science acts about that place, going round in harmonious measures about being step by step; but intellect contemplates it with simple thinking. Further, the science in us is one thing, and that in the place above the heaven another. The first is true, the second is truth itself. What, then, is it, and where does it come from? It is a goddess who is the source of all intellectual knowledge, and the first cause of undefiled and steady thinking. But it shines out in the first triad of the intellectuals, because this perfects all other things, and divine souls too. For they, going up to this one-like power of all knowledge, perfect their own knowledge. For each of the undefiled souls, Socrates says, going round with Zeus and the heaven, sees justice, temperance and science. So these three sources are there, being intelligible goddesses and the sources of the intellectual virtues, and not, as some think, intellectual Forms. For Plato usually marks the Forms by the word "itself", as "science itself" and "justice itself", as Socrates says somewhere in the Phaedo. But here, when he says justice herself, temperance herself and science herself, he seems to unfold to us certain self-perfect and intelligible goddesses who exist as a triad. Of these, science is the monad, temperance has the second rank, and justice the third. Science supplies undefiled, firm and unchanging thinking; temperance gives all the gods the cause of their return to themselves; and justice gives them the cause of distributing the whole of good as each deserves. Through science each of the gods thinks the natures before him and is filled with intelligible thinking; through temperance he turns back to himself, and enjoys a second union and a good that goes with the return to himself; and through justice he rules the natures after him, "on a silent path", as they say, measures what they deserve, and gives each its fitting share. These three sources, then, hold all the activities of the gods. Science proceeds in answer to the first triad of intelligibles; and as that triad gives being to all things, so this lights up the gods with knowledge. Temperance proceeds in answer to the second triad of intelligibles. For temperance imitates the holding-together and measuring power of that triad, since it measures the activities of the gods and turns each of them back to itself. And justice proceeds in answer to the third triad of intelligibles. For it too separates second natures by what each deserves, as that triad separates them intelligibly by the first patterns.
!flag elevating them to their paternal port | "The fatherly harbour" (patrikos hormos) is a Chaldean phrase for the soul's home in the intelligible, to which it returns.
!flag in a silent path, as they say | Euripides, Trojan Women 887-888: justice "leads all mortal things aright, going on a noiseless path".
!note The Phaedrus 247d-e. Taylor's footnotes correct "union" to "thinking" twice and restore "as each deserves, as that triad" in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.15 | The plain of truth, the meadow, and the food of the gods
A second triad in the place: the plain of truth, spread out to intelligible light; the meadow, the fertile power of life and of the Forms; and the food of the gods, an intelligible union that gives them strength. The food is twofold: ambrosia, solid, answering to limit, keeps the gods stable in what is above them; nectar, liquid, answering to the unlimited, carries their providence to all things. Before both is the one "feast and banquet".

@p
After these things, then, we may look at another triad that exists beforehand in this place, which Socrates also praises: the plain of truth, the meadow, and the food of the gods. The plain of truth is spread out intellectually to intelligible light, and shines with the lights that proceed from there. For as the One sends out intelligible light, so the intelligible gives second natures a share that produces being. The meadow is the fertile power of life and of forming principles of every kind, the grasp of the first causes of life, and the cause of the variety and generation of Forms. For the meadows here too produce forms and forming principles of every kind, and bear water, the symbol of life-giving. And the cause that nourishes the gods is a certain intelligible union, holding in itself the whole perfection of the gods, and filling them with vigour and power, so that they may care providently for second natures, and have an unchanging intellectual thinking of the natures that are first. Above, though, the gods share in these in a one-like way, but in their processions in a divided way.
!note The Phaedrus 248b-c: "the plain of truth", and "the fitting pasture for the best part of the soul comes from the meadow there".

@p
As to the food, Plato calls one kind ambrosia and the other nectar. "For the charioteer," he says, "stopping the horses at the manger, puts ambrosia before them, and after it gives them nectar to drink." The charioteer, then, being fed with intelligibles, shares in a unitary way in the perfection lit up by the gods; but the horses share in it in a divided way, first in ambrosia, and afterwards in nectar. For from ambrosia they must remain steadily and without straying in the higher natures; but through nectar they must provide unchangingly for second natures. For they say ambrosia is solid food and nectar liquid, which Plato too points to when he says that the charioteer puts ambrosia before the horses and afterwards gives them nectar to drink. So the food of nectar shows the unconstrained and unbreakable nature of providence, and its going to all things without defilement; and the food of ambrosia shows stability and a firm seat in the higher natures. From both it is clear that the gods both remain and proceed to all things, and that neither is their unstraying nature, which does not turn toward lower beings, barren, nor is their fertile power and procession unstable; but remaining they proceed, and, set in the gods before them, they provide for second natures without being stained. Nectar and ambrosia, then, are the perfections of the gods so far as they are gods; other things are the perfections of intellect, nature and bodies. So Plato, having taken these into souls, calls the souls nourished with them gods. For so far as they too share in the gods, so far they are filled with nectar and ambrosia. In their processions, though, these are divided in two: one supplies the gods with stable and firm perfection, the other with unstraying providence, free governance, and an ungrudging, abundant giving of good, by the two principles of the whole of things that rule over such a distribution. For we must grant that ambrosia answers to limit and nectar to the unlimited. So nectar is as if moist, and not bounded from itself, and ambrosia as if solid, and having a boundary from itself. Nectar, then, is fertile, perfects the gods' secondary presence, and is the cause of power, of a vigour that provides for the whole of things, and of an unlimited and unfailing supply. Ambrosia is stable perfection, like limit, the cause to the gods of their being set in themselves, and the giver of firm and unstraying thinking. Before both, though, is the one source of perfection and seat of all the gods, which Plato calls food, and the feast and the banquet, as perfecting in a unitary way the divided multitude of the gods, and turning all things back to itself through divine thinking. For dais, "feast", shows the divided distribution of divine food, and thoinē, "banquet", the united return of the whole of things to it. For it is the thinking of the gods so far as they are gods. But the food holds both these powers together, being the fullness of intelligible goods and the one-like perfection of divine self-sufficiency.
!flag the one is as it were humid, and not bounded from itself; but the other is as it were solid | Here "the one" is nectar and "the other" ambrosia, though ambrosia was named first in the sentence before; the next sentences show it. The modern version names them.
!note The Phaedrus 247e; 247a ("to the feast and the banquet").

@ch IV.16 | The place above the heaven is a triad
Plato marks it as a triad from the start: three denials, and three goddesses, which Syrianus divided into three monads, after the Orphic theology. Food (intelligible fullness) is the mark of the first monad, the meadow (fertility) of the second, the plain of truth (spread-out light) of the third. So science belongs to the first, which stays united to the intelligible father; temperance to the middle, which returns to itself; justice to the third, which divides.

@p
Enough, then, about these things for the present study. Next we should discuss the division of the place above the heaven into three. For the intelligible summit of the intellectuals is, as we observed before, a triad. Right at the first idea of this place, then, Plato unfolds its threefold nature, taking three denials: without colour, without shape, and untouchable. Having also set three goddesses in it, science, temperance and justice, our teacher and leader, Syrianus, thinks right to divide this triad into three monads, and shows this in keeping with the Orphic theologies. But if we must find the definite characters of these three goddesses from what has already been laid down, we must understand that the plain of truth, the meadow, and the cause that nourishes the gods are set there. To nourish, then, belongs to intelligible perfection. So the soul's wing is given its upward drive, and intellectual perfection too, by the food that flows from there into the soul. The character of the meadow is to have a power that generates forming principles and Forms, and the causes of the production of living beings. So souls too graze about the meadow; and pasture (nomē) is food, but in a divided way.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects "image" to "causes" in the Greek he translated.

@p
The plain of truth, though, is the spreading-out and showing of intelligible light, the unfolding of inner forming principles, and perfection going out everywhere. This, then, is the character of the third monad. Fertility is the character of the second, and intelligible fullness of the first. For the whole place above the heaven is lit up with the light of truth; so all the natures in it are called true. And Socrates says "that whatever soul, following a god, has seen any of the things that truly are, shall be free from harm until the next circuit." For everything in that place is truly being and intelligible, and full of divine union. In the first monads, though, this intelligible light exists gathered in, and set as if hidden in the inner sanctuary; but in the third monad it shines out, spread out together and divided together with the multitude of powers. From these things, then, we may see the differences of the three monads in keeping with Plato's hypotheses. And if science belongs to the first monad, temperance to the second, and justice to the third, the triad will be wholly plain from these too. Does not science, which is steady, the one-like thinking of wholes, and existing together with intelligibles, belong to the power that is united to the intelligible father, and neither proceeds nor separates its union from the godhead of that father? Does not the kind of justice belong to the power that divides, separates the intellectual kinds, leads intelligible multitude into order, and lights up a distribution by desert? And does not the kind of temperance belong to the power that is the middle of both, turns back to itself, and holds the common bond of this triad? For the harmonious, and a communion with the extremes by reason, are the shining good of this middle power.
!flag In the first monads however [i.e. in the plain of truth and the meadow,] this intelligible light subsists contractedly | Taylor's brackets take "the first monads" as the plain of truth and the meadow, and "the third" as the cause that nourishes the gods. The sentence before says the opposite (the plain of truth is the third monad); Proclus means the first two monads, food and meadow, with the light shining out in the third, the plain of truth.
!note The Phaedrus 248c. Taylor's footnote explains "perfection going out everywhere".

@p
Not to be long, then, since what has been said is enough to remind us of Plato's meaning: we praise these three goddesses who divide the place above the heaven. All of them are intelligible as among intellectuals; they are summits, and gather all things into one intelligible union. But one does so steadily, another generatively, and another by turning back, having a power that first produces among the intellectuals. For one unites the monads of all the gods and gathers them about the intelligible; another does this about the processions of the gods; and another about their returns. All of them together, though, gather into one the whole of a hyparxis that always remains, proceeds and returns. So Plato also raises the gods distributed in the world to this one place, and turns them back, acting about it, as about what gathers the whole orders of the gods into sharing in the intelligibles. These monads, then, lead out intelligible Forms, fill them with a share in divine union, and call back the natures that have proceeded and join them to the intelligibles. About this whole triad, though, what has been said may be enough.

@ch IV.17 | Adrastia and her ordinance
Adrastia rules in the place above the heaven, setting the measures of a blameless life from the vision of the intelligibles. Her one ordinance binds together all the divine laws, of Kronos, of Zeus, and of fate, and nothing escapes it: in Orpheus she guards the Craftsman and sounds her drum to turn all the gods to her. Socrates echoes that sound: "This is the ordinance of Adrastia".

@p
It remains, then, to pass to the discussion of Adrastia, since Socrates points out that she holds her kingdom in this place. For what sets the measures of a blameless life for souls, from the vision of these intelligible goods, surely has its first unfolding into light there. For the cause that leads up, being second to the objects of desire, can raise both itself and other things to the place above the heaven by turning back. But what sets and measures for souls the fruits of the vision of the intelligible, since it has its hyparxis in the intelligible, lights up blessedness for them from there. It is set, then, as I have said, in that place. But it rules all the divine laws in a one-like way, from on high down to the last of things. It binds to its own one ordinance all the ordinances: those of intellect, those above the world, and those of the world. Whether, then, there are laws of Kronos, as Socrates in the Gorgias says there are, when he says, "This law, then, which was in the time of Kronos, is still now among the gods"; or laws of Zeus, as the Athenian Stranger says, when he says, "But justice follows Zeus, avenger of those who leave the divine law"; or laws of fate, as Timaeus teaches, when he says, "that the Craftsman declared to souls the laws of fate"; of all these the ordinance of Adrastia holds them together by one intelligible simplicity, and at the same time gives them all existence and the measures of their power. And if I must give my own opinion, the inescapable guarding power of this triad, and the unchangeable embrace of order that runs everywhere, exist beforehand in this goddess. For these three goddesses not only unfold and gather all things but are also guardians, according to the Oracle, of the works of the father and of one intelligible intellect.
!flag the sacred law of Adrastia | Taylor's "sacred law" is thesmos, "ordinance", as against nomos, "law". Adrastia ("she whom none escapes") was in the Orphic poems the nurse and guardian of the infant Zeus.
!note The Phaedrus 248c; the Gorgias 523a; the Laws IV 716a; Timaeus 41e.

@p
This guarding power, then, the ordinance of Adrastia points to, which nothing can escape. For as to the laws of fate, not only the gods are above them, but partial souls too, when they live by intellect and give themselves up to the light of providence. The gods of Kronos are, in their being, set apart from the laws of Zeus, and the gods who hold together and who perfect from the laws of Kronos; but all things obey the ordinance of Adrastia, and all the distributions of the gods, all measures and all guardianships exist because of it. In Orpheus she is said to guard the Craftsman of the universe, and, taking bronze drumsticks and a drum of goatskin, to make so loud a sound that she turns all the gods to herself. And Socrates, imitating this mythical sound, which sends a kind of proclamation to all things, likewise brings out the ordinance of Adrastia to all souls. For he says, "This is the ordinance of Adrastia: that whatever soul has seen anything of the truth shall be free from harm until another circuit," all but voicing the Orphic sound in this proclamation, and saying it as a hymn to Adrastia. For first, he calls it thesmos, an ordinance, and not nomos, a law, as he does the laws of Kronos and of Zeus. For thesmos goes with godhead and belongs more to the intelligibles than to the intellectuals; but nomos, pointing to intellectual distribution, fits the intellectual fathers. Second, he speaks of it in the singular, not the plural, as Timaeus does of the laws of fate. Third, he extends it to all the kinds of souls, and shows that it is the common measure of their happy and blessed life, and the true guard of the souls that can remain on high free of all being acted upon. For such is the meaning of the words, "and if it can always do this, it shall always be free from harm". This ordinance, then, holds all the undefiled life of divine souls, and the temporary blessedness of partial souls. The first it guards intelligibly, and the second it measures by the vision of intelligible goods. And so much about Adrastia.
!flag receiving brazen drumsticks, and a drum made from the skin of a goat | Orphic fragment (Kern 105, 152): Adrastia stood before the cave of Night and beat a bronze drum, so that the gods heard her laws. Taylor's footnote corrects a word of the Greek he translated ("a kind of proclamation").
!note The Phaedrus 248c.

@ch IV.18 | The place above the heaven in sum
It is the first intelligible and intellectual triad, with three marks, unfolding, gathering and guarding. It joins intellectuals to intelligibles, brings out the fertile powers of the intelligibles, gives existence to the three virtues, and draws up all its images. We must go up to it leaving behind the life of becoming and the body.

@p
For what remains, then, we shall say in sum that the place above the heaven is the first triad of the intelligible and intellectual gods, with three characters: unfolding into light, gathering, and guarding. It holds all these intelligibly and in an unknown way: joining intellectuals to intelligibles, calling out the fertile powers of the intelligibles, receiving into itself the fullness of the Forms from the intelligible patterns, and bringing out its own meadow from the summit of the source there. From the one intellect it gives existence to the three virtues, perfects the whole of itself with intelligible impressions, and receives in its inexpressible bosom the whole of intelligible light. At one and the same time it remains in the hidden nature of the intelligible gods and proceeds intelligibly from there, shines out to the view of the intellectuals, and turns back and draws up by inexpressible powers all the images of its own union that it has sown in everything. To this place too we must go up in a mystic way, leaving on earth all the life that brings generation, and the bodily nature with which, coming here, we were surrounded as by a wall, and waking only the summit of the soul to share in the whole of truth and the fullness of intelligible food.
!flag producing its own meadow from the fontal summit which is there | Proclus means the source of life in the second intelligible triad (eternity), from which the meadow, the life-giving power of this place, is drawn.

@ch IV.19 | Why there must be gods who hold together, before the intellectual gods
What first holds things together? Not body or breath (pneuma), as the Stoics say, for body itself falls apart; not the forms and states in bodies, which cannot hold themselves; not souls, which are mixed and move in time; not intellect, where division first shines out. So holding-together comes before the intellectual gods. And there are three triads, gathering, holding together and perfecting, for remaining, procession and return.

@p
After this intelligible and unknown triad, which rules over all the intellectual kinds, let us look at the triad that holds their bond together, intelligibly and intellectually at once. For before intellect and the intellectual gods the cause of holding together must be in these gods; and, set in the middle of the intelligible and intellectual order, it must reach all the divine multitudes, all the kinds of beings, and all the divisions of the world. For what is it that first holds things together? If, as some say, it is the nature of breath and local motion, body itself, which holds other things together, will need holding together. For every body, by its own composition, can be scattered and divided; as the Stranger from Elea too points out to those who make bodily principles, saying that the being they praise so much is broken up and scattered. Body, then, is not fitted by nature to hold other things together; and even if a power of this kind belonged to bodies, breath could not give it to us, since it is always flowing away and scattered, and spreading beyond what bounds it. But if we suppose that the states and holding forms divided about bodies light up what underlies them with holding-together, they must surely do so by being present to them; but how will these states and forms hold themselves together? It is hard to see how this could be done. For being distributed about masses of matter and divided along with what underlies them, they need a boundary and a holding-together. But they are not fitted by nature to be bounded or held together by themselves, since they do not have a self-begotten and self-existent being. What neither produces nor perfects itself cannot hold itself together. Moreover, every state and every material form is moved by another, and depends on another, more ancient cause; and so it cannot be separated from what underlies it, being unable to turn to itself.
!flag the nature of spirit and local motion | The Stoics held that pneuma ("breath", Taylor's "spirit"), a fiery air pervading bodies, holds them together by its "tensile motion". Taylor's footnote corrects "intelligibles" to "intellectuals" in the Greek he translated.
!note The Sophist 246b-c: the giants' "bodies", which their opponents break into small pieces.

@p
But if, leaving these, we should say that souls, which are bodiless and self-begotten, are the first causes of holding-together, where shall we place the divisible and at the same time undivided nature of souls, mixed from the divisible and the undivided, sharing in the kinds of being, and divided into harmonious forming principles? For souls hold bodies and natures together, because they share in an undivided character; but they need another holding nature, which may give the first principle of mixture to the kinds, and of holding-together to the divided forming principles. For the self-moving nature of souls, passing step by step and spread out in time, needs what may hold its one life together and make it whole and undivided. For the whole that holds parts together exists before the parts, since the whole made of parts receives its holding-together brought in from something other than itself. But if, going with reasoning beyond souls, we look at intellect, whether the intellect that is shared or, if you like, the unshared and divine one, and in short if we look at once at the intellectual kind of gods, and if this first holds beings together, we shall find in it too an all-various multitude, divisions of kinds, and, as Socrates says, "many blessed sights and paths". For the separation of divine natures and the variety of Forms appear among the intellectuals, and so do the mythical cuttings and generating powers. How, then, can what holds together be first here, where the dividing kind shines out? And how could intellectual multitude not refer its share of holding-together to another, more ancient cause? For intellectual multitude is what is first held together (since it is what is first divided, and what needs holding together is divisible, while the undivided itself is beyond the holding hyparxis); but it is not what first holds together. For everything held together is held together by another thing that first has the power of holding-together. It is clear, then, from what has been said, that the holding order of beings is set before the intellectual gods.
!flag fabulous sections | The mythical "cuttings", the castration of Ouranos by Kronos and of Kronos by Zeus in Hesiod and the Orphic poems, which Proclus reads as the separation of the intellectual orders.
!flag discussive energies | The Phaedrus' diexodoi again (247a), the "paths" of the gods (see IV.4).

@p
The intelligible and hidden hyparxis supplies union to all things, as existing next after the One, and being undivided and one-like. But holding-together is the drawing of multitude into undivided communion; so it exists as second to the intelligibles. For the middle there was intelligible, and the united cause that first produces holding-together. But the holding-together of the intelligible and intellectual gods imitates the unifying power of the intelligibles. For there the three threefold monads were the unions of wholes: one by transcendence, another by the middle centre, and another by return. But in the intelligible and intellectual orders these three triads are second after those unions, and grow together with multitude. So one of these triads gathers, another holds multitude together, and another is of a perfecting nature. For what is gathered, held together and perfected is multitude. Whether it is intellectual, or above the world, or of the world, or any other multitude, then, it is gathered, held together and perfected through these three triads. When gathered, it is raised to the union of the intelligibles and firmly set in them. When held together, it remains undivided and unscattered in its offspring. And when perfected, it receives its completion from its own parts or powers.
!note Taylor's footnote corrects "holding" to "held together" in the Greek he translated.

@p
But since beings, remaining, proceeding and returning, must enjoy this threefold providence, there are three gathering monads existing beforehand, three that hold together, and three that perfect. And we do not say that the first natures are divided so, and rule over so many orders and powers, for the sake of the good of second natures; they are always the first causes of good to the lower things, while we go back from the lower natures to the causes of wholes. The intelligible and intellectual triads, then, perfect things in a threefold way, and always hold them together and gather them into union. But the intelligible monads generate their remainings, processions and returns without separation and in a unitary way. About other things, though, we have partly spoken and shall partly speak again.

@ch IV.20 | The heaven's circuit is the order that holds together
Socrates calls the triad that holds together "the circuit of the heaven": "circuit" because it surrounds all lives and divine thinkings, "heaven" because, as the visible heaven presses the elements together and leaves no void, it binds all beings into undivided communion. Intellect, soul and body each share in it in their own measure.

@p
Let us speak now, then, about the triad that holds together. This Socrates, in the Phaedrus, calls the circuit of the heaven. Because it has the middle centre of unshared life, and is what is most alive of life itself, he calls it a circuit, as holding all other lives and divine thinkings round about on every side. Because of this, souls raised to it are perfected in their thinking and joined with intelligible sights. The circuit of the heaven, though, is always the same; for it is an eternal, whole, one and united thinking. But the circuit of souls comes about through time, is more partial, and is not a grasp of the intelligibles gathered all at once. So souls are carried round in a circle and come back to where they started, while the heavenly circuit always remains the same. But because it completes the bond of the intelligible and intellectual gods, and holds together all the orders in their remaining, procession and return, Socrates calls it heavenly. For Timaeus says that this perceptible heaven too presses together on every side the elements beneath it, so that no room is left for a void. As the visible heaven, then, holds together all things beneath it, and is the cause of their continuity, coherence and sympathy (for a void coming between would break the continuity of things, and the overturning of this continuity would destroy the sympathy of bodies), so that intellectual heaven binds all the multitudes of beings into an undivided communion, lighting up each with its fitting portion of holding-together. For intellect shares in the holding cause in one way, the nature of soul in another, and bodily being in another. Through the highest share of holding-together intellect is undivided; through second measures of sharing, soul is divisible and undivided by one mixture; and through a last lessening, bodies, which have a divisible existence, still remain held together, and do not perish by being scattered, but enjoy their own division and weakness. The whole triad that holds together, then, is called heaven by its own hyparxes; but the breadth of life spread beneath it is called circuit. For among visible things the revolution of the heavens is motion, and as it were the life of body.
!note Timaeus 58a: "the circuit of the whole ... presses together and leaves no empty place".

@ch IV.21 | The heaven is one and three: back, depth and arch
The heaven has a back, a depth and its own arch (distinct from the arch beneath the heaven). The back is intelligible, holding all the heaven together, and on it the souls stand and see what is beyond; the depth is life, the middle; the heavenly arch is intellect, the lower boundary. So Ouranos, father of the intellectual gods, is one and three. The first of the Synocheis, the gods who hold together, stands at the summit "beyond", shut in by the place above the heaven.

@p
But if we must find its threefold nature from what has been laid down, we must use the way of analogy. Since Plato himself calls the back of the heaven one thing and its depth another, it is clear that the heavenly arch is the third; for the arch beneath this he plainly calls "beneath the heaven". And as we say that the place above the heaven is set above the back of the heaven, so we must grant that the arch beneath the heaven is different from the heavenly arch. For the heaven is bounded above by the back, and below by the arch. It is held by the place above the heaven, and holds the arch beneath the heaven. It is clear from these things, then, that the heaven shows itself as a triad: by its back holding all things together in one simplicity; by its arch bounding the whole triad; and by its depth proceeding into itself, and setting up the middle breadth of holding-together and coherence. The back of the whole heavenly order is an intelligible god, perhaps getting this name from that. But it is intelligible as in the triad that holds together, pressing from outside and holding together all the kingdom of the heaven. It lights up all the gods with a one-like and simple grasp of second natures, and is filled from above with intelligible union. So too divine souls, led through all the heavenly depth, stand on the back of the heaven, and the circuit carries them round as they stand; and so they look at what is called the place above the heaven. Their standing, then, is the setting of souls in the intelligible watch-tower of the heaven, giving souls sameness, undefiled power and unstraying thinking. The carrying round is the sharing in a life full of vigour and the keenest activity. And the presence of both together holds the fertile activity, the quiet motion and the steady thinking of the intelligibles. The heavenly depth is the one continuity of the whole triad, and the middle god who joins the whole heavenly order, proceeding from the intelligible grasp but ending in the heavenly arch, which sets the boundary of the whole heaven. There is, then, one union and holding-together of all this triad, and an unbroken procession from the back to the arch, through this middle god, who grows together with both extremes, unfolding the multitude that holds together but bounded on each side by the extremes, one holding it from above and the other bounding its procession from below.
!note The Phaedrus 247b-c. Taylor's footnote corrects "of wholes" to "the whole" in the Greek he translated.

@p
The heavenly arch, then, remains, which is the lower boundary of the triad; so too is the intellect in it, filled by life but united by the intelligible, and turning the whole triad back to its principle. For the arch too is like the back of the heaven, though less in extent. By being beneath, then, it is lessened; but by likeness it turns back to the heavenly summit. And this is the heavenly intellect, the nearest sunocheus, holder-together, of the arch beneath the heaven. So each arch is called the intellectual boundary of the intelligible and intellectual gods. The whole triad that holds together, then, is divided like this: the back (nōton) by the intelligible (noēton), the depth by life, and the arch by intellect. But the whole of it is one and continuous, because what holds all other things together should much more hold itself together. For each character of the gods begins its activity from itself: the gathering character fixes itself in a gathered way in the highest union; what turns wholes back turns itself back to its principle; and the undefiled keeps itself pure of matter before other things. So the character that holds together also, before what shares in it, holds itself together intelligibly and intellectually; and through this holding-together the nature of the heaven is said to be one and continuous. For the whole triad converges on itself, and keeps its own wholeness united and most like itself by nature. The arch holds all the intellectuals together most nearly, and presses them on every side. But before this the heavenly depth itself, which also holds the arch, binds together the whole orders. And before these the heavenly back, in a one-like way, holds by one sweep of simplicity the whole heavenly kingdom itself and all things beneath it, and binds them to itself by its holding power and hyparxis. For among visible things too the concave circumference of the heavens presses the elements most nearly, and does not let them scatter and blow away in their unbounded motions on every side. Before these, the heavenly mass presses all things hard and drives them to the middle, leaving no empty place. But there is one embrace of all these, the back of the heavens, which is the cause of likeness to the heavens, and to the elements of contact with the heavens. For the smooth and even nature of the back of the heavens, as Timaeus says, makes the whole heaven like itself; and always the natures that embrace hold together the natures embraced. From visible things, then, we must carry the likeness over to the father of the intellectual gods, Ouranos, and see how he is both one and three: above and below having the intelligible and intellect, and in the middle having life, which, as the cause of processions, intervals and generating powers, we have rightly set, by interval, under the heavenly depth; since Plato too calls the summit the back. "For those called immortals," he says, "when they go beyond and reach the top, stand on the back of the heaven." He calls the summit of the heavenly order, and "beyond", the back of the heaven; and these are in a remarkable way the privileges of the first of the Synocheis. For, holding all things together in the one summit of his hyparxis, according to the Oracle, he exists wholly "beyond", and is united to the place above the heaven and to its inexpressible power, closed in by it on every side, and shutting himself in the one-like grasp of the intelligibles. For what difference is there between saying that the first of the Synocheis is shut in the intelligible look-out, and showing that he is held most nearly by the place above the heaven, which was intelligible but spread out among the intellectuals? If what is "beyond" is the first, the summit is plainly ranked with the rest and set apart from them. But if the first is of this kind, set at the intelligible summit and lighting up the other gods with contact with the intelligible and the fatherly harbour, there must be a middle and an extremity: the first by the heavenly depth, the second by the boundary of the whole circuit. And if the circuit of the whole heaven is one and continuous, the character of this order must be given as the cause of this. For holding together the whole orders of the gods, and before other things itself, and being as it were the centre and bond of the divine kinds, it first binds and holds itself together and stretches itself to one life. The heaven, then, is one and at the same time three, and proceeds into three monads, both unseen and seen and what is between them, imitating the intelligible gods who settle into intelligible triads.
!flag the proximate sunocheus of the subcelestial arch | Sunocheus is "holder-together", one of the Synocheis, the Chaldean name for the gods of this order. Taylor's footnotes explain the word, correct a gender to agree with "arch", and gloss "interval" as the heavenly depth.
!flag the back (το νωτον) according to the intelligible (κατα το νοητον) | A play on words in the Greek: nōton (back) and noēton (intelligible) differ by one letter.
!note The Phaedrus 247b-c ("they go outside and stand on the back of the heaven"); Timaeus 34b ("smooth and even"). The Chaldean Oracles speak of a god who "holds all things together in one summit, being himself wholly beyond".

@ch IV.22 | Ouranos in the Cratylus: the middle of the middle gods
The Cratylus gives Ouranos a twofold relation: he "sees the things above" and "generates a pure intellect". So he is the thinking of the intelligibles and the intelligible of the intellectuals, the exact middle. Of his three monads, the first most thinks the intelligibles, the third (Ouranos the father of Kronos in Orpheus) most generates the intellectuals, and the middle does both. The purity of the intellect he generates points to the undefiled gods.

@p
If you are willing to see the character of this order from what is written in the Cratylus, first take this as an argument that the Synocheus is set in the middle: a twofold relation of it is given, one toward the intelligibles and the other toward the intellectuals. For it is said to see the things above and to generate a pure intellect. So it is the thinking of intelligibles, and the intelligible of intellectuals. For the cause of intellect exists before an intellectual cause; and what is both at once most of all completes the middle order of intelligibles and intellectuals. For the gathering god, thinking the intelligibles, or rather united to them, does not first give existence to a divine intellect. And the perfecting god, producing intellectuals together with the middle god, most nearly thinks the heavenly order, not the intelligibles before the heaven. But the middle god alone, holding the intelligible and intellectual centre, reaches equally to both; thinking the intelligibles intellectually, it is the cause of the intellectuals intelligibly. But since the relation to its causes comes before its power of generating intellectuals, Socrates, starting from this relation, gives also a second power as hanging from it. Sight turned to the things above is very fitly given the name heavenly (ourania), as seeing the things above (horōsa ta anō). This, then, perfectly defines for us a relation more ancient than the order that holds together, taking it as intellectual in relation to the intelligibles and as sight in relation to what is seen, though it thinks itself and is intelligible in itself. But its intelligible, in relation to what is first intelligible, has an intellectual rank. What follows, though, unfolds the relation of this middle to the intellectuals. For Socrates adds: "Hence also, Hermogenes, those who study things on high say that Ouranos generates a pure intellect, and that the name is rightly given." The order of Ouranos, then, is spread as a middle in the middle, intelligible and intellectual gods, holding the intelligible and intellectual at once in one undivided holding-together, standing alike toward each, and equally distant from the first intellectuals and the unitary intelligibles. So it is said to think the things above and so to produce a pure intellect.
!note The Cratylus 396b-c. Taylor's footnote corrects "powers" to "power" in the Greek he translated.

@p
Taking this, then, first from what has been laid down, we should next note this: the heavenly order being threefold, and the whole of it thinking intelligibles and producing intellectuals, the first monad thinks intelligibles in a special way. For it mingles itself with intelligibles, knows intelligible intellect, is united to the natures before it, and is undivided as among undivided things, reaching up over itself toward intelligible simplicity. The third monad most of all generates intellectuals, since it is the intellect of the whole triad that holds together. And with the Orphic theologians too, Ouranos, father of Kronos, is the third. The middle monad produces the intellectual order of the gods together with the third, but is joined, together with the first, to the intelligibles, and is filled with intelligible union from the first, and fills the third with fertile powers. Do you not see, then, how Plato, through the character of the extremes, unfolds to us the whole heavenly order? He joins its intelligible hyparxis to the intelligibles, its intellectual hyparxis to the intellectuals, and gives us the means of gathering its hyparxis that is the middle of both and proceeds by a common character. For if you also wish to take this from what has been said, the heavenly light is joined to the light of the intelligibles; for sight is nothing else than light. The middle order, then, by its own light and its divine summit, is joined to the first natures; but by an intellectual nature, and the boundary of the whole triad, it generates intellect, and all the undefiled godhead of the intellectuals. For it does not produce intellect alone, but together with purity. For Socrates himself says: "Hence they say that a pure intellect is generated from it." So the heavenly order is the first cause of the intellectual hyparxis and of undefiled power. But if purity must not be in intellect by accident, it is the godhead of beings set apart from second natures, and the giver of unchangeable power; and the great Ouranos, producing it together with intellect, is at the same time the cause of the gods who are the sources of purity and of the intellectual fathers. These signs, then, of the truth about the gods who hold together may also be taken from the Cratylus.
!flag Heaven the father of Saturn is the third | In the Orphic theogony there are three kings before Kronos (Phanes, Night, Ouranos); Ouranos is the third. Taylor's footnote corrects "first" to "third" in the Greek he translated.
!flag the Gods who are the sources of purity | The "undefiled" (amiantoi) gods, the Kouretes who guard the intellectual fathers, which Proclus discusses in Book V.

@ch IV.23 | Earlier interpreters on the arch beneath the heaven
Earlier interpreters had already set the arch apart as its own order. Theodorus of Asine, following Plotinus, made it what comes first after the inexpressible, calling the first God Ouranos; Iamblichus made the heaven an order of intelligible gods and the arch the order just beneath it. Theodorus' view cannot stand, since the first God does not see; Iamblichus is free of that objection but leaves the heaven undefined. Syrianus settled the question.

@p
It remains, then, following what is written in the Phaedrus, to look at the arch beneath the heaven and the character of the gods there. Before we begin the teaching about it, though, I want to say this first: that some of the most famous interpreters before us, taking this arch beneath the heaven as a divine order ranked under the heaven, have thought fit to rank it right after the first God, calling the first God Ouranos. Others have set both the heaven and the arch beneath the heaven in the breadth of the intelligibles. For the philosopher from Asine, Theodorus, persuaded by Plotinus, calls what proceeds next from the inexpressible the arch beneath the heaven, as he reasons about these things in his treatise on names. But the great Iamblichus, taking the great heaven as a certain order of the intelligible gods (and in one place he takes it to be the same as the Craftsman), says that the order set next under the heaven, and as it were girding it, is the arch beneath the heaven. He writes these things in his commentaries on the Phaedrus. Let no one think, then, that we make any innovation in the theology of this order, or that we are the first to divide the arch beneath the heaven from the heaven; we are chiefly persuaded by Plato, who distinguishes these three orders, the place above the heaven, the heavenly circuit and the arch beneath the heaven; and after Plato by those who have studied his thought with inspiration, Iamblichus and Theodorus. For why need I speak of our leader, Syrianus, who was truly a Bacchant, possessed by the god as the followers of Dionysus are, in a remarkable way in all that concerns Plato, and who made the wonder of Plato's thought, and the amazement that goes with it, shine out down to our day?
!flag who was truly a Bacchus | A Bacchant (bakchos), one possessed by Dionysus: Plato's Phaedo 69c, "many carry the wand, but few are Bacchants", meaning true philosophers.
!note Theodorus of Asine (4th century), a pupil of Iamblichus and of Porphyry; his treatise on names is lost.

@p
Syrianus, then, in his treatise on the harmony of Orpheus, Pythagoras and Plato, has unfolded most perfectly the character of this order, the arch beneath the heaven. The two wise men just named differ very much from each other in their thought. For Theodorus, calling the first cause Ouranos, can no longer let the heaven be sight that sees the things above, as Socrates in the Cratylus explains the name. For the first God neither sees nor is sight, nor is lower than anything. So Theodorus does not accept this explanation of the name, nor does he praise the place above the heaven, as Socrates does under divine inspiration. For there is no place, nor intelligible, of the One, nor any multitude of Forms, nor does the kind of souls go up beyond the first God, since there is nothing beyond him. The divine Iamblichus, since he supposes that Ouranos exists, undefined, after the first cause, and has not given the character of its hyparxis, is free of the doubts just mentioned; but he should teach us what the heavenly order is, how it exists, and what kind of gods before the Craftsman completes it. But the one who has perfected everything on this subject, and confirmed all he said with arguments that cannot be beaten, is our teacher Syrianus, who looked at all the orders between the first God and the kingdom of the heaven, beheld the character of this order with his intellect, and gave us, his initiates, the exact truth about it. In this way, then, our fathers and grandfathers differ from one another; but all of them in common distinguish the arch beneath the heaven from the heavenly circuit.
!flag our fathers and grandfathers | Proclus' teachers (Syrianus) and their teachers (Iamblichus, Theodorus), in the succession of the Platonic school. Taylor's brackets supply the end of the first sentence, which is broken off in the Greek.

@ch IV.24 | The arch beneath the heaven perfects: the teletarchs
The arch closes the middle procession and turns it back to its principle; through it the gods rise to the heaven. It is perfection that exists before the intellectual gods, not the "entelechies" of the Aristotelians, nor soul, nor intellect. In it souls are initiated by the gods themselves, which is why these gods may be called teletarchs, rulers of initiation. The arch too has a summit, a depth and an extremity.

@p
This too, then, we must suppose, and besides this, that this order of gods, the arch beneath the heaven, is set next under the heaven. So, since the heaven, being one and three, has the order that holds together, and the place above the heaven the highest order of the intelligible and intellectual gods, the arch beneath the heaven must surely end the middle procession of the gods, close this whole order and turn it back to its principle; it must receive an order second to the heaven, but one which it turns round to the highest union; it must grow together with the middle kinds, but exist before the intellectuals. For these separate their kingdom from the heavenly power; but the arch beneath the heaven is united to the heaven and held by the heavenly order, which is also why it is called "beneath the heaven". As it is joined, then, to the heavenly circuit and exists next from it, it turns all second natures back to the intelligibles and perfects them by the intellectual look-out. For since the intellectual gods are generated by return, and turned round to themselves by one spherical union, the perfecting rule must be set next above them.

@p
So I wonder at those who do not know this divine order, and do not keep the whole source of perfection; some of them betake themselves to entelechies, of whom we accept only this, that they too join the perfect with the form of holding-together. They do not know, then, the perfection that is separate from what underlies it, gladly embrace the likenesses of true perfections, and busy themselves with these. Others again give soul as the cause of perfection, not knowing that they are not claiming a perfection existing beforehand in eternity, and starting from the life that acts in time and has its perfection in periods. But a perfection whose whole exists at once must come before what is divided, and stable perfection before what is moved. For motion in time itself needs an end and an object of desire, and is unrolled about it part by part. Third, after these, others go back to intellect, and suppose that the first perfection is intellectual. For intellect is activity and intellectual perfection; but it longs for divine perfection, exists about it, and turns back to itself through it. So the cause of return must exist before the intellectual kinds that turn back to divine perfection, and the leader of the one perfection must be spread out above the natures that are perfected.
!flag betake themselves to entelechias | Aristotle's entelecheia, "being-at-the-end", the actual completeness of a thing (his definition of soul in On the Soul II.1); Proclus means the Peripatetics, who find perfection only in forms in their subjects.

@p
Rightly, then, does the arch beneath the heaven, before all intellectual natures, set up an order of gods that turns back and perfects all the second divine kinds. That is why Plato raises the gods and daemons who follow Zeus to this arch, and through it to the heaven and the place above the heaven. For when they go to the feast and the banquet, they climb up to the arch beneath the heaven. So through this they are perfected, share in the circuit of the heaven, and reach out to the intelligible. For the intelligible is what feeds and fills all things. The perfecting order, then, is set beneath the order that holds together. It perfects all the natures that go up to the intelligible, opens souls wide to receive divine goods, and lights up intellectual light. And holding in its bosom the second kinds of the gods, it sets all things in the holding circuit of wholes.

@p
Through these things, then, Socrates too says shortly after that the souls raised with the twelve gods to intelligible beauty are initiated, that is, made perfect, in the most blessed of the mysteries, and through this initiation receive the mysteries with a pure soul, and are set in, and become watchers of, things inexpressible. So the initiation of the gods is there; the first mysteries are there. Nor is it at all surprising if Plato also lets us call the gods of this order teletarchs, rulers of initiation, since he says that the souls there are initiated, the gods themselves initiating them. For how else could we name the first sources of telete, initiation, than teletarchs? For I, seeing the gods so much at work even in the names themselves, do not see how they could be called otherwise. But initiation being one and three (for the perfecting gods are divided along with the gods who hold together), Plato calls its one union the arch beneath the heaven, as he calls the order that holds together Ouranos. And he points to the depth in it by granting that there is in it an extreme lowness and a steep path to the top of the arch. As in the order before this, then, we thought it right to set the intelligible at the summit, life at the depth, and the intellectual at the extremity that bounds the whole heavenly circuit, so in this perfecting order we must take the intelligible of the arch as its summit, naming it in the same way as the back of the heaven, because these are ranked with each other; take the depth, through which souls go to the summit, as ranked with life; and take the extremity that closes the whole arch as ranked with intellect.
!flag Teletarchs | "Rulers of initiation" (teletarchai), the Chaldean name for the gods of this third triad.
!note The Phaedrus 247a-b ("steep is the way up to the arch beneath the heaven"), 250b-c.

@ch IV.25 | The three perfecting gods
The perfecting gods are spread under the whole triad that holds together. One gives stable perfection, one a perfection that generates, one leads the return to causes. Perfection, like wholeness, is threefold: before the parts (the gods' henads), from the parts (the world), and in a part (each part of the world). Before them stands the all-perfect intelligible triad, of which this triad is the image.

@p
This whole order, which is united to the one before it, we must divide by analogy. For the perfecting gods are spread under the whole triad that holds together. One of them supplies the gods with stable perfection, setting all the gods in themselves and uniting them to themselves. Another is the first source of a perfection that generates wholes, stirring what comes first in being to providence for second natures. And a third leads the return to causes, turning everything that has proceeded back to its own principle.
