For through this triad everything perfect is self-sufficient and exists in itself; everything that generates is perfect, and generates full of vigour; and everything that longs for its own principle is joined to it through its own perfection. Whether, then, you take the power of nature that perfects what is generated, or the perfect number of the soul's restorations to where it began, or the perfection of intellect set in one by activity, all these hang from the one perfection of the gods, and, referred to it, some have a greater and others a smaller portion of perfect hyparxis; and every perfection proceeds from there. In short, perfection is threefold. One is before parts, such as the perfection of the gods. For this has its existence in unity, existing beforehand self-perfectly before all multitude. For such is the one of the gods: not like the one of souls or of bodies, since these are joined in kinship with multitude and mingled with beings. But the henads of the gods are self-perfect and exist before beings, generating multitudes and not generated along with them. Another perfection is made of parts and completed through parts, such as the perfection of the world; for it has its all-perfectness from its fullnesses. And a third, other perfection is that in parts; for so too each part of the world is perfect. For as this universe is a whole made of wholes, so it is perfect from the perfect parts in it, according to Timaeus. And in short, perfection is divided in the same way as wholeness; for, as Timaeus says, they are joined with each other.
!note Timaeus 32d-33a: the world is "whole and perfect from wholes and perfect parts". Taylor's footnote corrects "generative" to "stable" in the Greek he translated.

@p
So the perfecting kind grows together with the holding kind, and the perfecting monad is ranked beneath all the holding kinds. And as the wholeness of the heaven that holds parts together is threefold, so perfection is threefold. And if I must give my own opinion, all the perfections come from all the leaders; but the perfection before parts belongs more to the first leader, that made of parts to the middle, and that in a part to the third leader. Before this triad, though, is the intelligible triad, which is one-like perfection and an all-perfect hyparxis, and which Timaeus too calls "perfect in all respects". There the three perfections existed beforehand in a united way, or rather there was one source of every perfection. As the holding triad, then, is the unfolding of intelligible holding-together, and the gathering triad of the unifying and first in the intelligibles, so the perfecting triad is the image of the all-perfect triad. For the intelligible and intellectual gods proceed in answer to the intelligible triads. Perfection, then, is threefold: before parts, from parts, in a part. In another way, too, perfection is stable, generative, and turning back. And in another, there is one perfection of intellectual and undivided beings, another of the beings of soul, and another of the natures divided about bodies. Very rightly, then, there are three leaders of perfection before the intellectual gods, who make one order beneath the heavenly circuit, raise through themselves all second natures to the intelligible, perfect them with intelligible light, turn them back and join them to the kingdom of the heaven, give an unflagging activity to the natures perfected, and guard their undefiled perfection.
!note Timaeus 31b: the living being itself is "perfect in all respects". Taylor's footnote corrects "that" to "holding" in the Greek he translated.

@ch IV.26 | The ascent of souls, and a prayer to the teletarchs
The arch beneath the heaven is also "the most blessed of mysteries". In it telete comes before muesis, and muesis before epopteia: we are perfected by the perfecting gods, see whole and still appearances with eyes closed through the gods who hold together, and become watchers of the intelligible look-out through the gods who gather. Proclus ends Book IV's middle part with a prayer to the teletarchs.

@p
Such are the ideas that may be taken from Plato about the third triad of the intelligible and intellectual orders, which at one time he calls the arch beneath the heaven, with a summit, middle and extremity, and at another a blessed mystery, the most ancient and holy of all mysteries, through which he raises souls and joins them to the mystic fullness of the intelligibles. For this triad opens the heavenly paths, being set beneath the heavenly circuit, and shows the self-shining appearances of the gods, which are whole and firm, and open out to the mystic beholding of intelligible sights, as Socrates says in the Phaedrus. For telete, being perfected, comes before muesis, closing the eyes, and muesis before epopteia, watching. So in going up we are initiated (teleioumetha) by the perfecting gods. We see with closed eyes, that is with the pure soul itself (muoumetha), whole and steady appearances, through the gods who hold together, among whom is intellectual wholeness and the firm setting of souls. And we become set in, and watchers of (epopteuomen), the intelligible watch-tower, through the gods who gather wholes. We speak of all these in relation to the intelligible, but we gain a different thing by a different order. For the perfecting gods initiate us into the intelligible through themselves, and the gathering monads are, through themselves, the leaders of the beholding of intelligibles. There are many steps up, but all of them reach to the fatherly harbour and the fatherly initiation. In this may the teletarchs, the leaders of all good, also set us, lighting us up not by words but by deeds. May they think us worthy to be filled with intelligible beauty under the great Zeus, and free us wholly from the evils of generation with which we are now walled round. And may they light up for us this most beautiful fruit of the present study, which, following the divine Plato, we have given well enough to those who love the contemplation of truth.
!note The Phaedrus 250b-c. Taylor's head for this chapter explains the three grades of initiation, as his note on IV.9 does.
